Transport noise restores uniqueness and prevents blow-up in geometric transport equations
[

Abstract
In this work, we demonstrate well-posedness and regularisation by noise results for a class of geometric transport equations that contains, among others, the linear transport and continuity equations. This class is known as linear advection of k k -forms. In particular, we prove global existence and uniqueness of L p L^{p} -solutions to the stochastic equation, driven by a spatially α \alpha -Hölder drift b b , uniformly bounded in time, with an integrability condition on the distributional derivative of b b , and sufficiently regular diffusion vector fields. Furthermore, we prove that all our solutions are continuous if the initial datum is continuous. Finally, we show that our class of equations without noise admits infinitely many L p L^{p} -solutions and is hence ill-posed. Moreover, the deterministic solutions can be discontinuous in both time and space independently of the regularity of the initial datum. We also demonstrate that for certain initial data of class C 0 ∞ , C^{\infty}_{0}, the deterministic L p L^{p} -solutions blow up instantaneously in the space L l ​ o ​ c ∞ L^{\infty}_{loc} . In order to establish our results, we employ characteristics-based techniques that exploit the geometric structure of our equations.



2021

1,2]Aythami Bethencourt de León

1,3]So Takao

MSC Classification]60H50, 60H15, 35R25, 53Z05.

keywords
well-posedness by noise, transport noise, stochastic Lie transport equations, blow-up prevention, stochastic flows, geometric mechanics.

1
Introduction

The aim of this work is to demonstrate that noise has a regularising effect for the geometric class of SPDEs

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\mathbf{K}(t,x)+\mathcal{L}_{b(t,x)}\mathbf{K}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{i}(t,x)}\mathbf{K}(t,x)\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{i}=0,\\
&\mathbf{K}(0,x)=\mathbf{K}_{0}(x),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{split}

(1)

where

•

\mathbf{K}(t,x)
is a stochastic process taking values in the space of differential
k
-forms on
\mathbb{R}^{n},
for any
k\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
.

•

b(t,x)
and
\xi_{i}(t,x),

i=1,\ldots,N,
are deterministic vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying some regularity conditions.

•

\mathcal{L}_{v}
denotes the Lie derivative
1
1

1

The Lie derivative generalises the notion of directional derivative to tensor fields. More concretely,
\mathcal{L}_{v}T
describes the infinitesimal rate of change of a tensor field
T
under the flow of
v
(see Appendix
9
for supplementary material on tensor algebra and calculus).
with respect to a vector field
v
.

•

\{W_{t}^{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
is a family of independent Brownian motions and the notation
\circ
indicates stochastic integration in the Stratonovich sense, which in this introduction is understood formally.

•

\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)
is a deterministic differential
k
-form serving as initial condition.

Without the noise, i.e.
\xi_{i}=0,

i=1,\ldots,N,
equation (
1
) is known as the (Lie) transport equation of
k
-forms. Since the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}
can be understood as the infinitesimal rate of change of
\mathbf{K}
when transported along the flow of the vector field
b
, the PDE
\partial_{t}\mathbf{K}+\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}=0,

\mathbf{K}(0,\cdot)=\mathbf{K}_{0}
describes the advection of the
k
-form
\mathbf{K}_{0}
along the flow of
b
(see Section 6 in
[
23
]
).
The multiplicative noise term added in (
1
) is known as “Lie transport noise” or “transport noise” (see
[
10
,
1
]
), which simply perturbs the transport velocity in the deterministic equation by noise. Indeed, equation (
1
) can formally be expressed as
\partial_{t}\mathbf{K}+\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{b}}\mathbf{K}=0
, where
\tilde{b}(t,x):=b(t,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_{i}(t,x)\circ\dot{W}_{t}^{i}
.

We prove that under some regularity conditions on the vector fields
b
and
\xi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N,
which we state in more detail later, equation (
1
) is globally well-posed in
L^{p}
. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the noise term in (
1
) has the effect of
restoring uniqueness of solutions and preventing the development of instantaneous singularities
in the same equation without the noise. Results of this type are normally referred to as “well-posedness/regularisation by noise” in the literature, and have attracted significant attention in the last decade. This is due to their relevance in fluid dynamics (indeed, the equations studied are often of fluid dynamics origin) and the prospect that some of the research could help tackle the famous 3D Navier-Stokes existence and smoothness problem. In this work, we establish novel well-posedness and regularisation results, generalising existing ones in the literature and unifying them under a single framework.

1.1
Background and motivations

Two main motivations led us to consider equation (
1
).
Our first source of inspiration stems from the groundbreaking results in
[
18
]
, where the authors prove that noise restores uniqueness of solutions of the stochastic linear transport equation, corresponding to the case
k=0,

\xi_{i}:=e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n
in our equation (
1
). The latter work is fundamental as it provides the first example of a PDE that becomes well-posed under the presence of noise.

Our second source of inspiration comes from
[
22
]
, where the author introduces formal variational principles that yield stochastic fluid equations with Stratonovich Lie transport noise. The resulting equations comprise a coupling between a nonlinear momentum equation and a linear equation of the form (
1
), the latter being a natural geometric generalisation of the linear transport equation considered in
[
18
]
. It was therefore natural to ask whether the well-posedness by noise results in
[
18
]
could be generalised to the stochastic transport equation (
1
).

The well-posedness and regularisation properties that we show for (
1
) can be motivated by the observation that by (formally) rewriting (
1
) in Itô form and (formally) computing the expectation, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{K}(t,x)]+\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{K}(t,x)]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{K}(t,x)],

(2)

where
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{i}}^{2}
is a dissipative second order operator if some parabolicity conditions are met. We note that if
\xi_{i}:=e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n,
then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{i}}^{2}
is exactly the Laplacian operator
\Delta.

Although (
2
) suggests that transport noise could regularise solutions in (
1
), at least at the level of expectation, it is not clear whether this regularity would be transferred to pathwise solutions. However, we will prove that this is indeed the case.

1.2
Physical interpretation

From a physical viewpoint, a motivation for considering equation (
1
) resides in its importance in stochastic fluid modelling, where
it represents the advection of a locally conserved quantity
\mathbf{K}
(mathematically modelled as a differential
k
-form) along stochastic paths traversed by fluid particles. Indeed, as demonstrated in
[
8
]
, equation (
1
) is equivalent to the stochastic local conservation law

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{0}}\mathbf{K}(0,\cdot)=\int_{\Omega_{t}}\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot),\quad t\in[0,T],

(3)

where
\Omega_{0}
is any
k
-dimensional compact, orientable submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n},
and
\Omega_{t}:=\phi_{t}(\Omega_{0})
is the image of
\Omega_{0}
under a sufficiently smooth stochastic flow map
\phi_{t}
, represented by the equation

\displaystyle\phi_{s,t}(x)

\displaystyle=x+\int^{t}_{s}b(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{s}\xi_{i}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))\circ\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{i},

(4)

for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where we employed the notation
\phi_{t}:=\phi_{0,t}
(i.e. when the initial time is set to zero). Under the modelling philosophy of
[
22
]
,
\phi_{t}(x)
represents the stochastically perturbed Lagrangian trajectory of a fluid particle located initially at the point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We illustrate the equivalence between (
1
) and (
3
) with some examples. If
\mathbf{K}(t,x):=\rho(t,x)\mathrm{d}^{n}x
is the mass density of the fluid, where
\mathrm{d}^{n}x
is the standard volume element in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(which is a volume form, i.e.
k=n
), conservation of mass (
3
) implies that
\rho
satisfies the stochastic continuity equation with transport noise

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\rho(t,x)+\text{div}{(b(t,x)\rho(t,x))}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\text{div}{(\xi_{i}(t,x)\rho(t,x))}\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{i}=0.

(5)

Note that the above equation is exactly (
1
), since the Lie derivative of volume forms is given explicitly by
\mathcal{L}_{v}(\rho(t,x)\mathrm{d}^{n}x)=\text{div}{(\rho v)}\mathrm{d}^{n}x
. We may also consider the case when
\mathbf{K}(t,x):=u(t,x)
is a scalar, or a tuple of scalars
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
(this corresponds to
k=0
), which models physical quantities that make natural sense pointwise, such as the temperature or entropy per unit mass. In this case, the zero-dimensional manifold reduces to a single point
\Omega_{0}=\{x\}
and the conservation law (
3
) applied to each component of
u
becomes pointwise evaluation
u_{i}(0,x)=u_{i}(t,\phi_{t}(x)),

i=1,\ldots,n
. This implies that
u
satisfies the linear transport equation with transport noise

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u(t,x)+b(t,x)\cdot Du(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_{i}(t,x)\cdot Du(t,x)\circ\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{i}=0,

(6)

which coincides with (
1
) since the Lie derivative of scalar fields is given by
\mathcal{L}_{v}u=v\cdot Du
.

Yet another example arises in 3D ideal magnetohydrodynamics, where the advection of a magnetic field, interpreted geometrically as a two-form
B\cdot\mathrm{d}S
(i.e.
k=2,

n=3
) with
\text{div}{(B)}=0
(here,
\mathrm{d}S
represents the surface element), again obeys the conservation law (
3
) (conservation of magnetic flux) and therefore satisfies equation (
1
). In this case, the Lie derivative reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}(B\cdot\mathrm{d}S)=(-\text{curl}(v\times B)+v\text{div}{(B)})\cdot\mathrm{d}S=-\text{curl}(v\times B)\cdot\mathrm{d}S,

and (
1
) yields the stochastic vector advection equation with transport noise, studied for instance in
[
19
]
. We refer the readers to
[
23
]
for more instances of deterministic fluid models with advected quantities, and to
[
22
]
for their stochastic counterparts.

1.3
Overview of related results

The example SPDEs that we listed above have been the subject of several articles on the topic of well-posedness/regularisation by noise.
In the aforementioned work
[
18
]
, the authors proved well-posedness by noise for the stochastic linear transport equation (
6
) with
\xi_{i}:=e_{i},
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, providing the first example of a PDE that becomes well-posed under the presence of noise. In particular, they show using a characteristics-based argument that (
6
) (we have retained the same notation the authors employed) admits a unique global
L^{\infty}
-solution, strong in the probabilistic sense, under the assumptions that the drift vector field
b
is bounded, globally
\alpha
-Hölder continuous (uniformly in time, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
), and
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),

q>2
, in the sense of distributions. Moreover, there exist
classical
examples of vector fields
b
in the above conditions such that the deterministic transport equation is ill-posed.

In subsequent years following the publication of
[
18
]
, effort has been made to weaken the conditions on the drift
b
in the stochastic linear transport equation (
6
) such as in
[
32
]
, where only boundedness and measurability are assumed to construct solutions of Sobolev regularity. Standard PDE arguments (as opposed to characteristics-based ones) to prove the well-posedness by noise have also been developed in
[
5
]
, where the concept of renormalised solutions is exploited. Furthermore, in
[
14
]
, the transport noise has been shown to prevent the formation of singularities for a Sobolev-type class of initial conditions and drift
b
satisfying the so-called Ladyzhenskaya-Prodi-Serrin (LPS) condition.

Well-posedness by noise for the stochastic continuity equation (
5
) has also been shown in several works in the literature. For instance,
[
7
]
applies novel duality techniques to establish regularity and uniqueness results for the transport and continuity equations when
b
satisfies the LPS condition, sometimes considering integrability conditions on
\text{div}{(b)}
.
In addition,
[
34
]
employs an argument based on characteristics to show well-posedness in the case when
b
is random and meets certain integrability conditions. The work
[
21
]
furthermore shows well-posedness of
kinetic solutions
of the continuity equation with a general nonlinear noise.

We stress that
all
the above articles except
[
21
]
only treat the case
\xi_{i}:=e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n
, which substantially simplifies the equations. One of the goals of this article is to remove such a strong restriction on the diffusion vector fields when establishing well-posedness and regularisation by noise results.

Interestingly, the case corresponding to
k=2
,
n=3
in our general equation (
1
), known as the vector advection equation in 3D ideal magnetohydrodynamics, has also been considered in the literature, but only in the case
\text{div}(b)=0,

\xi_{i}:=e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n
. In particular, in
[
19
]
, a mechanism for preventing instantaneous singularities of the spatial supremum norm is demonstrated for a Hölder type divergence-free drift and in
[
16
]
, well-posedness by noise is shown by assuming some integrability conditions on a divergence-free drift vector field. We observe that, in particular, the results we establish in the present paper cover well-posedness and prevention of singularities in the above case for a drift with
nonzero divergence
.

The ultimate goal of research in well-posedness/regularisation by noise is to prove a meaningful result for nonlinear fluid PDEs such as the 2D Boussinesq equation, 3D Euler, or 3D Navier Stokes, for which the existence of global smooth solutions in the deterministic case remains an intriguing open problem. Local well-posedness of the 2D Boussinesq and 3D Euler equations with transport noise has already been established in
[
1
]
and
[
10
]
, respectively. Moreover,
[
3
]
generalises the latter well-posedness results by developing an abstract local theory in Hilbert spaces that includes fluid PDEs with transport noise. However, the results of these works do not shed any light on whether transport noise has a regularising effect on the deterministic solutions. An example of when noise of transport-type does indeed have a regularising effect is the class of stochastic Lagrangian-averaged equations introduced in
[
13
]
, where in the case of the 2D Boussinesq equation, global well-posedness can be demonstrated by exploiting the regularity of solutions to the parabolic system (
2
) (see
[
4
]
). Surprisingly, partial results of well-posedness by noise in the case of the 3D Navier-Stokes equation have also recently been established in
[
17
,
15
]
, where the added noise bears some similarity with the transport noise we consider here. While these works show promise that transport noise could help regularise solutions of deterministic nonlinear equations, it is also well-established that this is
not always
the case. For instance, in
[
2
]
, it is shown that transport noise is not sufficient to prevent formation of shocks in the inviscid Burgers’ equation.

1.4
Contributions

In this article, we extend the well-posedness/regularisation by noise results of
[
18
,
14
,
19
,
34
,
33
,
16
]
and several others to the general class of equations (
1
). In particular, we:

1.

Prove global existence of
L^{p}
-solutions, for any
p\geq 2,
strong in the probabilistic sense, with drift and diffusion vector fields satisfying Hypotheses
1
&
2
in Section
3
, and uniqueness when Hypothesis
2
is substituted by Hypothesis
3
. We also argue that Hypothesis
3
is sharp (in the sense that it is crucial to give a meaning to equation (
1
)) whenever
k\notin\{0,n\}
, and show that it can be substantially weakened in the case
k\in\{0,n\}
, recovering in particular the result in
[
18
]
. Moreover, we prove that the unique weak solution of (
1
) is continuous both in time and space whenever the initial datum is taken to be continuous.

2.

Demonstrate
two ill-posedness results of different nature
for equation (
1
) without stochastic noise (i.e.
\xi_{i}:=0,

i=1,\ldots,N
). More concretely, given
n\in\mathbb{N},

k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\},

p\geq 2,
and
q\in(1,2]
defined by the relation
1/q+1/p=1,
we show the following:

(a)

For all
\alpha\in(0,1)
if
n\geq 2
and
\alpha\in((q-1)/q,1)
if
n=1,
there exists a drift vector field
b_{\alpha}
satisfying Hypotheses
1
,
2
,
3
for which we construct an infinite family of
L^{p}
-solutions of equation (
1
) when
kp<n
(i.e., we establish nonuniqueness of solutions). Moreover, these solutions fail to be continuous in both time and space for initial datum of arbitrary regularity.

(b)

Whenever
k\notin\{0,n\}
and
\alpha\in((p-1)/p,1)
, there exists a different vector field
b_{\alpha}^{\prime}
satisfying the above hypotheses such that for certain smooth compactly supported initial data, nontrivial
L^{p}
-solutions exit the space
L^{\infty}_{loc}
at
any
positive time (i.e.,
instantaneous supremum norm inflation
occurs).

Thus, under fairly general conditions, the Lie transport noise term in (
1
) has the effect of (i) restoring the uniqueness of
L^{p}
-solutions and (ii) preventing instantaneous blow-up of the spatial supremum norm of the solutions. Since we apply characteristics-based techniques that exploit the geometric structure of equation (
1
), along the way we also prove some regularity properties of the flow (
4
) and an explicit representation for our
L^{p}
-solutions that may be of independent interest. To establish our results, we also prove an extension to
k
-form-valued stochastic processes of the Itô-Wentzell formula for the evolution of a time-dependent stochastic field along a semimartingale, which is equally of independent interest and has natural applications in fluid dynamics.

2
Preliminaries

We provide some important definitions and results that will be needed for our analysis. In particular, in Subsection
2.1
, we introduce bounded and uniform Hölder spaces of Euclidean functions defined on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In Subsection
2.2
, we discuss general notational aspects, in particular some geometric ones that will be employed in Subsection
2.3
. In Subsection
2.3
, we state some important definitions and results from the theory of stochastic processes. We also introduce weak concepts of the Lie derivative that will be used in the paper.

Throughout this paper, all the spaces of functions depending on time and space are understood in the Lebesgue sense (i.e. as spaces of equivalence classes, where two functions are identified if they coincide for almost every (“a.e.”)
(t,x)
). We stress this only throughout this introduction.

2.1
Euclidean Hölder spaces

Let
\alpha\in(0,1).
We say that a bounded Borel-measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is (globally)
\alpha
-Hölder continuous if

\displaystyle[f]_{\alpha}:=\sup_{x\neq y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha}}<\infty.

We denote the space of
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions by
C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

In the limiting case
\alpha=1
, we say that
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is (globally) Lipschitz continuous if the Lipschitz seminorm

\displaystyle[f]_{\text{Lip}}:=\sup_{x\neq y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert}<\infty.

We denote the space of bounded
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions by
C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
which is a Banach space endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{C_{b}^{\alpha}}:=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+[f]_{\alpha}.

This definition can be extended straightforwardly to vector-valued functions, in which case we employ the notation
C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}).
For
k\in\mathbb{N},
we define
C^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to be the space of
C^{k}
-functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, such that
D^{l}f
for all
l
with
\lvert l\rvert=k
are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous.
Similarly, we define
C^{k+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to be the Banach space of functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
having bounded continuous derivatives of order
i=0,\ldots,k,
and
k
-th derivatives of class
C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
The corresponding norm is defined by

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{C_{b}^{k+\alpha}}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\lVert D^{i}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+[D^{k}f]_{\alpha}.

Again, this definition can easily be extended to vector-valued functions. Let
T>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We define the space of bounded
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions uniformly in time
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\right)
to be the set of
bounded Borel-measurable functions
f:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with

\displaystyle[f]_{\alpha,T}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{x\neq y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{\lvert f(t,x)-f(t,y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha}}<\infty.

This is a Banach space with respect to the norm
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{b}^{\alpha},T}:=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}+[f]_{\alpha,T}.
Naturally, we denote by
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}}^{m})\right)
the space of vector fields
f:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
having components of class
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\right)
.

Moreover, for
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},
we say that
f\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C_{b}^{k+\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
if all spatial derivatives of order
i
are in
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\right)
,
i=0,1,\dots,k
. The corresponding norm is defined as

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{C_{b}^{k+\alpha},T}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\lVert D^{i}f\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}+[D^{k}f]_{\alpha,T}.

This definition can be extended straightforwardly to functions of multiple components, in which case we employ the notation
L^{\infty}([0,T];C_{b}^{k+\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m})).
Finally, we denote by
C_{b}([0,T];C_{b}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
the functions of class
C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{k+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

All the spaces of functions introduced above can be defined similarly when
T=\infty,
in which case we substitute
[0,T]
by
[0,\infty)
in the notations.

2.2
Notations

2.2.1
Differential geometry notations

Since we will be dealing with SPDEs with solutions in the space of tensor-valued stochastic processes, it is important that we clarify some of the notations we will use throughout the paper. For completeness, we have also included supplementary materials in Appendices
9
and
10
, addressed towards readers unfamiliar with tensor algebra and calculus, where all the geometric objects introduced below are defined. We stress that the geometric function spaces below are understood as spaces of equivalence classes, where two tensor fields are identified if they coincide for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

We shall denote by
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
the
(r,s)
-tensor bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and in particular, we denote by
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
the tangent and cotangent bundles of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively (note that these correspond to the cases
(r,s)=(1,0)
and
(r,s)=(0,1),
respectively).
In this article, we will mostly work with
k
-forms, which are the alternating tensors of type
(r,s)=(0,k).
We employ the notations
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the
k
-th exterior power of the tangent and cotangent bundle, respectively.
k
-forms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are defined as sections of
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
(i.e. maps
\mathbf{K}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfy the section property
\pi\circ\mathbf{K}=\text{id}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\pi:\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the canonical projection).

We denote the space of smooth sections of the tensor bundle
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
if they are assumed to have compact support. However, in the special cases of the tangent and cotangent bundles, we typically follow the notations conventionally employed in differential geometry, that is, we denote the space of smooth sections of
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and the space of smooth sections of
\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We also denote the space of smooth
k
-vector fields (that is, the space of smooth sections of
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
) by
\mathfrak{X}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and the space of smooth
k
-forms by
\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Moreover, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote using angle brackets
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(s,r)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the natural pairing between
(r,s)
and
(s,r)
-tensors, and by round brackets
(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the canonical bundle metric on
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
induced by the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Appendix
9
for the definition of the natural pairing and bundle metric).

In general, we adopt the notation
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
to denote Banach sections of tensor bundles
2
2

2

This notation shall not to be confused with the Borel sigma-algebra associated with a metric space
E
, which we will denote by
\mathcal{B}(E)
.
, where
\mathfrak{B}
is to be replaced with any Banach function space, for example
\mathfrak{B}:=L^{p},C^{m}_{b},W^{m,p}
(see Appendix
10
for precise definitions). For convenience, we also include the exception
\mathfrak{B}:=C^{m},
which is a Fréchet space. In particular, for
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
p\in[1,\infty],
we denote the Sobolev norm (induced by the canonical bundle metric) of a tensor
\mathbf{K}\in W^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
by
\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{W^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})}
or
\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{W^{m,p}}
for simplicity. For example, the space of Sobolev
k
-forms will be denoted
W^{m,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and its corresponding Sobolev norm denoted as above.
The case
\mathfrak{B}:=L^{2}
gives a Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where we denote its associated inner product (induced by the canonical bundle metric) employing double angle brackets
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{2}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},
for
\mathbf{K}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
R>0,
we denote by
B(x,R)
the open ball with center
x
and radius
R.
For a tensor
\mathbf{K}\in W^{m,p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
we denote its norm on
B(x,R)
by
\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{W^{m,p}_{x,R}}
, or
\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{W^{m,p}_{R}}
if
x=0.
We also denote the space of distribution-valued tensor fields by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is equipped with the weak-
*
topology. Given a mollifier
\rho_{\epsilon}
and
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
we denote its mollification by
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}.
Finally, we denote the pull-back and push-forward of a tensor field
\mathbf{K}
by a flow of diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{s,t}
by
\varphi_{s,t}^{*}\mathbf{K}
and
(\varphi_{s,t})_{*}\mathbf{K},
respectively, and its Lie derivative along a vector field
b
by
\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}
. The divergence of a vector field
b
with respect to the Euclidean volume form is denoted by
\text{div}{(b)}
and its curl (when
n=3
) by
\text{curl}{(b)}.

Since we are primarily interested in tensor fields that are random and time-dependent,
\mathbf{K}
may further be parameterised by time
t\in[0,T]
and a probability element
\omega\in\Omega
. Therefore, we also consider mixed Banach spaces of
k
-forms. For example, given Banach function spaces
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2},\mathfrak{B}_{3}
, we say that a tensor field
\mathbf{K}
belongs to the mixed Banach space
\mathfrak{B}_{1}\left(\Omega;\mathfrak{B}_{2}\left([0,T];\mathfrak{B}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)\right)
if

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{K}\|_{\mathfrak{B}_{1,\omega},\mathfrak{B}_{2,t},\mathfrak{B}_{3,x}}:=\left\|\left\|\|\mathbf{K}\|_{\mathfrak{B}_{3,x}}\right\|_{\mathfrak{B}_{2,t}}\right\|_{\mathfrak{B}_{1,\omega}}<\infty,

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{B}_{i,\bullet}}
denotes the norm in the Banach space
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
and the subscripts
\omega,t,x
indicate whether this norm is computed in the probability space, time, or physical space, respectively. In the special case
\mathfrak{B}_{1}=\mathfrak{B}_{2}=\mathfrak{B}_{3}=\mathfrak{B}
, we adopt the notation
\mathfrak{B}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the corresponding mixed Banach space, whose norm we denote simply by
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathfrak{B}}_{\omega,t,x}}
. For example, when
\mathfrak{B}:=L^{p}
, we obtain
L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, whose associated norm is
\|\mathbf{K}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega,t,x}}=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/p}
. Moreover, if the latter integral is only defined on
B(0,R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the physical space, we adopt the notation
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{\omega,t,R}}
. In the special case
p=2
, we also have the corresponding inner product
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,x}}
in the full space and
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}
its restriction to
B(0,R)
.

2.2.2
Miscellaneous notations

Below, we introduce some of the other notations we employ throughout the paper shall any confusion arises.

•

We understand
\mathbb{N}
to be the natural numbers excluding zero.

•

We denote by
e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n
the canonical basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n},
that is,
e_{1}=(1,0,0,\ldots,0)
,
e_{2}=(0,1,0,\ldots,0)
, etc.

•

\text{id}_{X}
represents the identity function on a set
X.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
R>0,
we denote by
\overline{B}(x,R)
the closure of the open ball
B(x,R)
, and by
S(x,R)
its boundary.

•

In general, we will employ the notation
\overline{A}
for the closure of a set
A
with respect to a topology (which will be clear from the context) and
A^{c}
for its complement.

•

For a matrix
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
or a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we denote by
\lvert P\rvert
(respectively
\lvert x\rvert
) the
l_{2}
-norm of
P
(respectively
x
). In the matrix case, this corresponds to the Frobenius norm and in the vector case, the Euclidean norm. We also denote by
\text{tr}{(P)}
the trace of a matrix
P
and
\text{Adj}{(P)}
its adjugate matrix.

•

Given a measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},
we denote the characteristic function of
A
by
\mathcal{X}_{A},
and we assume by convention
\mathcal{X}_{\emptyset}:=0
(understood as a function), where
\emptyset
is the empty set.

•

Given two functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
taking values in the real line, we write
F_{1}\lesssim F_{2}
if there exists a positive constant
C
such that
F_{1}\leq CF_{2}.
Such constant may differ from line to line. In this article, we make it clear which variables the constant in
\lesssim
depends on if it is not evident from the context.

•

Let
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a
C^{\infty}
-function such that
0\leq\rho(x)\leq 1,

\rho(x)=\rho(-x),

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x=1,
such that it is supported on
B(0,1)
and
\rho(x)=1,

x\in B(0,1/2).
For any
\epsilon>0,
we define
\rho^{\epsilon}
by
\rho^{\epsilon}(x):=\epsilon^{-n}\rho(x/\epsilon).
For a function
f\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{m}),
its mollification
\rho^{\epsilon}*f
will be denoted in short by
f^{\epsilon}.

•

We denote by
D
the gradient of a real-valued function or, in general, the Fréchet derivative of a vector-valued function, and by
D^{m}
its
m
-th Fréchet derivative, where
m\in\mathbb{N}
. These may be understood in the distributional sense, which will be clear from the context. Throughout this article, whenever we apply
D^{m}
to a function (or distribution) depending on both time and space, it will normally be understood as derivatives in the spatial component and will otherwise be clarified.

•

We denote
(r,s)
-tensors in
bold font
, with the exception of the cases
(r,s)=(1,0)
and
(r,s)=(0,1)
, corresponding to vectors and covectors, respectively. This is to avoid saturating the paper with bold font symbols. Tensor components are denoted by
T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}
as discussed in Appendix
9
. In particular, for differential
k
-forms we use the notation
K_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}
.

2.3
Results from stochastic analysis

Finally, we introduce some key results from stochastic analysis that our proofs depend on. Throughout the paper, we assume that we are working with a fixed filtered probability space
(\Omega,(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfying the usual conditions, together with an
N
-dimensional Brownian motion
W_{t}=(W_{t}^{1},\ldots,W_{t}^{N})
adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t}.
We denote by
(\mathcal{F}_{s,t})_{s,t}
the completion of the sigma-algebra generated by
W_{r}-W_{u},

s\leq u\leq r\leq t,
for
0\leq s<t\leq T
. We might consider the case
T=\infty.
In this section, we also consider a different Brownian motion
B_{t}=(B_{t}^{1},\ldots,B_{t}^{N})
adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t}
.

2.3.1
Stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms

Let
b
and
\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
be vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfy Hypothesis
1

(i)
below (see all our assumptions in Section
3
). We are interested in working with solutions
\phi_{s,t}
of equation (
4
), which in Itô form reads

\displaystyle\phi_{s,t}(x)=x+\int_{s}^{t}\left(b+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_{i}\cdot D\xi_{i}\right)(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\xi_{i}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{i},

(7)

for a.e.
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The case
s=0
will be of special interest to us, in which case (
7
) becomes

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\left(b+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_{i}\cdot D\xi_{i}\right)(s,\phi_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{i}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{i},

(8)

for a.e.
0\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where we denoted
\phi_{t}:=\phi_{0,t}
. Equation (
7
) turns out to define a “stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms” under certain regularity assumptions on the coefficients
[
29
]
. To explain precisely what we mean by this, we introduce the following definition found in
[
18
]
, only slightly generalised to the class
C^{m}
(see also
[
9
,
29
]
).

Definition 1

(Stochastic flow of
C^{m}
-diffeomorphisms)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
(i.e. at least
m\geq 1).
An
\mathcal{F}_{s,t}
-measurable random field
\phi_{s,t}:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n},

0\leq s\leq t\leq T
solving equation (
7
) is called a stochastic flow of
C^{m}
-diffeomorphisms if
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. (it can be modified in a set of measure zero in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that) the following properties are satisfied:

•

\phi_{s,r}(x)=\phi_{t,r}(x)\circ\phi_{s,t}(x),
for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq r\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

•

\phi_{t,t}(x)=x,
for all
0\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

•

\phi_{s,t}
is a
C^{m}
-diffeomorphism, for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T.
Moreover, the spatial derivatives
D^{i}\phi_{s,t}(x),

D^{i}\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(x)
are continuous for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
i=0,\ldots,m
.

For
0\leq t\leq s\leq T,
we set
\phi_{s,t}:=\phi_{t,s}^{-1}.

For
\alpha\in(0,1),
we define a stochastic flow of
C^{m+\alpha}
-diffeomorphisms to be a stochastic flow of
C^{m}
-diffeomorphisms such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
D^{m}\phi_{s,t}(x)
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous,
uniformly
in
(s,t)
.
Below, we also introduce the notion of a
C^{m+\alpha}
-semimartingale, found in
[
29
]
. Note that when we talk about semimartingales
F_{t}(x),

t\in[0,T],

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that depend on both time and space, it is implied that the process
(F_{t}(x))_{t\in[0,T]}
is a semimartingale for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 2

(
C^{m+\alpha}
-semimartingale
[
29
]
)
.

A continuous local martingale
M_{t}(x),

t\in[0,T],

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
with covariance
Q(t,x,y):=[M_{\cdot}(x),M_{\cdot}(y)]_{t},

t\in[0,T],

x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
is said to be a
C^{m+\alpha}
-local martingale if (i)
M_{t}
and
Q(t,\cdot,\cdot)
have modifications that are
C^{m+\alpha}
-processes,
and (ii) the processes
D^{l}_{x}M_{t}(x)
are continuous local martingales with covariance
D^{l}_{x}D^{l}_{y}Q(t,x,y),
for all multi-indices with
\lvert l\rvert\leq m
.
A continuous semimartingale
F_{t}(x)=A_{t}(x)+M_{t}(x)
expressed as the sum of a continuous local martingale and a continuous process of bounded variation is said to be a
C^{m+\alpha}
-semimartingale if (i)
A_{t}
is a continuous
C^{m+\alpha}
-process such that the processes
D^{l}_{x}A_{t}(x)
have bounded variation, for all
\lvert l\rvert\leq m
, and (ii)
M_{t}
is a
C^{m+\alpha}
-local martingale.

A classical assumption to guarantee that equation (
7
) defines a stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms is the following.

Assumption 1

(Classical regularity of the flow equation)
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
b\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{m+\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})),

\xi_{i}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{m+\beta+1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N,
be deterministic vector fields. Then (as a particular case of Theorem 4.7.3 in
[
29
]
and the results in
[
28
]
), (
7
) admits a unique solution
\phi_{s,t}
which is an adapted
C^{m+\beta^{\prime}}
-semimartingale and a stochastic flow of
C^{m}
-diffeomorphisms for any
\beta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta).

2.3.2
Itô-type formulas for
k
-form-valued processes

Next, we present a useful result, which comprises a generalisation of Itô-type formulas for tensors derived by Kunita in
[
27
]
, and the classical Itô-Wentzell formula. We call this the Kunita-Itô-Wentzell formula for
k
-form-valued processes (advected by a stochastic flow of diffeomorphisms). We stress that this result is
not in the literature
and thus needs to be proven, which we do in Appendix
8.4
.

Theorem 1

(Kunita-Itô-Wentzell (KIW) formula for
k
-form-valued processes)
.

Let
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
\mathbf{K}:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
k
-form-valued semimartingale satisfying
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

1.

\mathbf{K}(t,x)
is continuous for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

2.

\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot)
is of class
C^{2}
for all
t\in[0,T].

Moreover, let
\mathbf{K}
have the explicit form

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(t,x)=\mathbf{K}(0,x)+\int^{t}_{0}\mathbf{G}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int^{t}_{0}\mathbf{H}_{i}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{i},

where
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

1.

\int^{T}_{0}\left(\lvert\mathbf{G}(t,x)\rvert+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\lvert\mathbf{H}_{i}(t,x)\rvert^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t<\infty,
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

2.

\mathbf{G}(t,\cdot)
and
\mathbf{H}_{i}(t,\cdot)
are of class
C^{1}
for all
t\in[0,T],

and
W_{t}^{i}
are independent Brownian motions,
i=1,\ldots,M
. Let
\phi_{t}
be an adapted solution of equation (
8
) with
b,

\xi_{j},

j=1,\ldots,N
satisfying Hypothesis
1

(i)
and Hypothesis
3
with
q=1
, assumed to be a stochastic flow of
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., the
k
-form-valued process
\mathbf{K}
pulled back by the flow
\phi_{t}
satisfies the following

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}(t,x)=\mathbf{K}(0,x)+\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathbf{G}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathbf{H}_{i}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{i}\\
&+\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{j}\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\phi^{*}_{s}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}\mathbf{H}_{i}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}[W^{i}_{\cdot},B^{j}_{\cdot}]_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\phi^{*}_{s}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(9)

for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Moreover, let Assumption
1
be verified with
m=3
and
\beta\in(0,1)
.
Then we have the following formula for the push-forward by the flow

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}(t,x)=\mathbf{K}(0,x)+\int^{t}_{0}(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{G}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int^{t}_{0}(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{H}_{i}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{s}\\
&-\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{b}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}](s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}](s,x)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{j}\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{G}_{i}](s,x)\,\mathrm{d}[W^{i}_{\cdot},B^{j}_{\cdot}]_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{j}}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}](s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(10)

Remark 1
.

Note that for formula (
9
), in the case
k=0
Hypothesis 3 is not needed since
\mathcal{L}_{b}f=b\cdot Df
(and hence no spatial derivatives of
b
are involved). In the case
k=n,
Hypothesis 3 can be substituted by
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
since
\mathcal{L}_{b}(\rho\mathrm{d}^{n}x)=\text{div}{(\rho b)}\mathrm{d}^{n}x
.

Finally, we introduce weak notions of Lie derivative on the Euclidean space (see Appendix
9.4
for the definition of a Lie derivative). In the following definitions, we let
q,p\in[1,\infty]
be two constants that satisfy
1/q+1/p=1.

Definition 3

(Sobolev Lie derivative)
.

Let
\mathbf{K}\in W^{1,p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
b\in W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We define the weak Lie derivative of
\mathbf{K}
with respect to
b
and denote it by
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{w}\mathbf{K}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
through the global coordinate expression (
132
) (Appendix
9.4
), where the derivatives of
\mathbf{K}
and
b
therein are now understood in the weak (Sobolev) sense. Its
L^{2}
-adjoint operator
(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{w})^{*}\mathbf{K}
can be easily computed and verified to have the coordinate expression (
133
). Throughout this paper, we will mostly omit the subscript “
w
”, since it will be clear from the context.

Definition 4

(Distributional adjoint Lie derivative and Lie derivative)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}\in W^{1,p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
b\in L^{q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We define the distributional adjoint Lie derivative of
\mathbf{\Theta}
with respect to
b
(and denote it by
(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
) as the functional that assigns to any test function
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
the expression that would result from (
133
) integrated by parts against
\mathbf{\Gamma}
if
b
was smooth enough, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\right](\mathbf{\Gamma})\\
&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[b^{l}\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\frac{\partial\Gamma^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}}{\partial x_{l}}+b^{l}\frac{\partial\left(\Theta^{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\Gamma^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\right)}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 148.0pt+\cdots+b^{l}\frac{\partial\left(\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1},l}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\Gamma^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\right)}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}\right](x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[b^{j_{1}}\frac{\partial\left(\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{l,j_{2},\ldots,j_{s}}\Gamma^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\right)}{\partial x_{l}}+\cdots+b^{j_{s}}\frac{\partial\left(\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s-1},l}\Gamma^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}\right)}{\partial x_{l}}\right](x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x,\end{split}

(11)

for any
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where as usually we assumed summation over repeated indices. If we impose the extra assumption that
(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}
is represented by a function of class
L^{q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
(as in Hypothesis
2
), we can define the distributional Lie derivative via the formula

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{L}_{b}^{dist}\mathbf{K}\right](\mathbf{\Theta}):=\langle\langle\mathbf{K},(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}.

(12)

Remark 2
.

The RHS of (
11
) and (
12
) are well-defined by Hölder’s inequality. We also note that (
11
) simplifies significantly in the case when
\Theta
is a
k
-form since the last line vanishes, which is the only case we are interested in in this article. For the necessary geometric background, we point the reader to Appendices
9

\&

10
.

Remark 3
.

Throughout the paper, we will extensively use the distributional adjoint Lie derivative (it is necessary for defining the notion of
L^{p}
-weak solutions of equation (
1
) as we do in Definition
6
), so to avoid cumbersome notation (and since it will be clear from the context), we will normally write
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}
instead of
(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*})^{dist}
.

3
Assumptions and main results

We note that since we will be working in spaces of equivalence classes of functions (
L^{p},W^{k,p},C^{k},
etc), our functions in time and space are defined up to a set of measure zero. Also, our identities in time and space make natural sense almost everywhere, so when it is clear from the context, “a.e.” will be omitted. The product sigma-algebras will always be considered to be completed.

Let
r,l\geq 1.
We say that a vector field
b
is of class
L^{l}([0,T];W^{1,r}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
if for every
R>0,

\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}b\in L^{l}([0,T];W^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).

3.1
Assumptions

To establish our main results, we will consider different hypotheses on the drift and diffusion vector fields of equation (
1
). In general, we will understand that
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
are fixed unless indicated otherwise. The first hypothesis will be needed to treat the flow equation (
7
).

Hypothesis 1
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1).

(i)

b\in L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C^{\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
,
\xi_{i}\in C_{b}([0,T];C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N.

(ii)

There exist
0<c<C<\infty
such that the uniform parabolicity condition

\displaystyle c\lvert v\rvert^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{r=1}^{N}\xi^{i}_{r}(t,x)\xi^{j}_{r}(t,x)v_{i}v_{j}\leq C\lvert v\rvert^{2},\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad v\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(13)

is met.

The following hypothesis is needed to establish the existence of solutions.

Hypothesis 2
.

Fix
q>1.

(i)

For every test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}),

(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\in L^{q}_{loc}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(ii)

If
k\notin\{0,n\},
there exists
C\in\mathbb{R}
such that
C\leq\text{div}(b)(t,x),
uniformly in
(t,x),
where
\text{div}(b)
is understood in the sense of distributions.

Remark 4
.

It can be checked that Hypothesis
2

(i)
always holds if
b\in L^{q}([0,T];W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. However, in general Hypothesis
2

(i)
does not require the existence of Sobolev derivatives of
b
. For example, if
k=0,
Hypothesis
2

(i)
is equivalent to
b\in L^{q}_{loc}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{q}_{loc}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
in the sense of distributions.

To prove a general uniqueness result, we will employ the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 3
.

Fix
q\geq 1.

Db\in L^{q}_{loc}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
.

At first sight, Hypothesis
3
might appear to be a fairly strong assumption. However, it is not difficult to show that for
k\notin\{0,n\},

Hypothesis
2

(i)
actually implies Hypothesis
3
. In other words, the condition we impose for uniqueness
is weaker
than the ones for existence. We note that Hypothesis
2

(i)
is
absolutely necessary
to give rigorous meaning to the definition of weak solutions in (
15
), as explained in Remark
2
. In the case
k\in\{0,n\},
such implication does not hold and we will provide sharper conditions for uniqueness, at the expense of increasing
q
. This is done in Theorem
5
further below.

Remark 5
.

Throughout this paper, whenever we enforce any of Hypotheses
1
,
2
, or
3
, we will assume that
\alpha
and
q
are fixed.

3.2
Notions of solution

We first note that the solutions we consider are global in time. For questions regarding the geometric notation that we employ, we point the reader to Subsection
2.2
and Appendix
10
. Sometimes we will choose the notation
\mathbf{K}_{t}(x):=\mathbf{K}(t,x)
for the solution of (
1
) or simply
\mathbf{K}
at our convenience.

Definition 5

(Classical solution)
.

Let
b\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\xi_{i}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N
. A classical solution to equation (
1
) is a
k
-form-valued process

\mathbf{K}
adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t}
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\mathbf{K}
has trajectories of class
C([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and it holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)-\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{K}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{s}\\
&\hskip 110.0pt-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{K}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}s=0,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{split}

(14)

Remark 6
.

Equation (
14
) can be understood as an equality in
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{k}}
(holding, of course, for almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As explained at the beginning of the section, we normally omit this).

Definition 6

(Weak
L^{p}
-solution)
.

Let
p\geq 2
. Moreover, enforce Hypothesis
2

(i)
on the drift vector field with
q
defined by the relation
1/q+1/p=1
, and
\xi_{i}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N.
We say that a
k
-form-valued process
\mathbf{K}
satisfies equation (
1
) weakly in the
L^{p}
-sense if:

•

\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
which is the Banach space of
k
-form-valued stochastic processes with norm
3
3

3

This space can be identified with
L^{p}\left(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{k}}\right)
.

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}_{s}(x)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}s\right]^{1/p}<\infty.

•

For any test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the process
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
has an
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted continuous modification
4
4

4

By a continuous modification, we mean that there exists a stochastic process in the same equivalence class of functions that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. has continuous paths.
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 50.0pt+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi_{i}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0,\end{split}

(15)

understood as an equality in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).

Remark 7
.

We note that Hypothesis
2

(i)
is the weakest possible condition on the drift
b
to make sense of solutions of (
1
) without assuming spatial regularity of
\mathbf{K},
since it is the weakest condition that ensures that
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b})^{dist}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is well-defined. Also, we observe that it is natural to ask for
(
15
)
to be satisfied as an equality in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]),
since
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}).
It is equivalent to imposing that (
15
) holds for almost every
(\omega,t)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
We remind that
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
stands for the
L^{2}
-inner product of tensor fields, which we briefly introduced in Subsection
2.2
and defined in detail in Appendix
10
.

Remark 8
.

The term
-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi_{i}}\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s
in equation (
15
) does not indicate that the equation has a parabolic nature (see Remark 14 in
[
18
]
).

Remark 9
.

Equation (
15
) can be naturally reformulated in terms of distributions as

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{t}-\mathbf{K}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}^{dist}_{b}\mathbf{K}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}^{dist}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{K}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}^{dist}_{\xi_{i}}\mathcal{L}^{dist}_{\xi_{i}}\mathbf{K}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s=0,

where
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is to be understood as the distribution
\mathbf{K}_{t}(\mathbf{\Theta}):=\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.3
Main results

The main goals of this paper are establishing global well-posedness of (
1
), showing that this fails consistently in the deterministic case, and demonstrating that the noise in (
1
) also prevents the instantaneous norm blow-up taking place in the deterministic case. For this, we first prove the following result of existence, uniqueness, and regularity of the flow of equation (
7
).

Theorem 2

(Regularity of the stochastic flow)
.

Consider equation (
7
) with drift and diffusion vector fields satisfying Hypothesis
1
. Then we have the following:

1.

For every
s\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
equation (
7
) has a unique continuous adapted solution
(\phi_{s,t}(x))_{t\in[s,T]}
. Moreover,
\phi_{s,t},

0\leq s\leq t\leq T,
is a stochastic flow of
C^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}
-diffeomorphisms (see Definition
1
), for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha).

2.

Let
\{b^{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty},\{\xi_{i}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}\subset L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\right),

i=1,\ldots,N
be sequences of vector fields such that
b^{j}\rightarrow b
and
\xi_{i}^{j}\rightarrow\xi_{i}
in
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
,
i=1,\ldots,N,

j\rightarrow\infty.
Let
\{\phi^{j}_{s,t}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be the corresponding sequence of associated stochastic flows. Then for any
r\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}|\phi_{s,t}^{j}(x)-\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert^{r}\right]=0,

(16)

\displaystyle\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}^{j}(x)\rvert^{r}\right]<\infty,

(17)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}^{j}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert^{r}\right]=0.

(18)

3.

Let
\widetilde{r}(x):=\sqrt{1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r\geq 2
be any constant such that
\alpha r>n
. Then for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha-n/r)
and
\epsilon>n/r
, there exists a
C^{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
-modification of
[\tilde{r}]^{-\epsilon}D\phi_{s,t}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\big\lvert[\widetilde{r}(x)]^{-\epsilon}D\phi_{s,t}(x)-[\widetilde{r}(y)]^{-\epsilon}D\phi_{s,t}(y)\big\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{r}\,\right]<\infty.

(19)

Since Theorem
2
establishes the existence and uniqueness of a stochastic flow of
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms, we are in place to state our main theorems in full rigor.

Theorem 3

(Existence of stochastic solutions)
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, let Hypotheses
1

\&

2
hold with
q
defined by the relation
1/q+1/p=1
. Then equation (
1
) has a global, strong in the probabilistic sense,
L^{p}
-solution (i.e. a solution in the sense of Definition
6
) with initial condition
\mathbf{K}_{0}
. Moreover, we can explicitly construct this solution via the formula
\mathbf{K}_{t}(x):=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x),
where we remind the readers that
(\phi_{t})_{*}
denotes the push-forward of the flow
\phi_{t},
assumed to be a solution of the flow equation (
8
).

The following theorem guarantees uniqueness of solutions.

Theorem 4

(Uniqueness of stochastic solutions)
.

In the conditions of Theorem
3
, substitute Hypothesis
2
by Hypothesis
3
. Let
\mathbf{K}^{1}
and
\mathbf{K}^{2}
be two
L^{p}
-solutions of (
1
) with initial condition
\mathbf{K}_{0}
. Then for every
t\in[0,T],
we have
\mathbf{K}^{1}_{t}=\mathbf{K}^{2}_{t}
(the equality is understood naturally as an equality in
L^{p}
).

As mentioned earlier, for
k\notin\{0,n\},
Hypothesis
2

(i)
implies Hypothesis
3
. Hence existence implies uniqueness (although we do not need Hypothesis
2

(ii)
for uniqueness) and Hypothesis
3
is sharp since it is the minimal condition necessary to provide a well-defined notion of weak solutions. In the case
k\in\{0,n\},
the hypotheses in Theorem
4
can be reduced substantially. Indeed:

Theorem 5

(Improved uniqueness for
k\in\{0,n\}
)
.

Theorem
4
holds in the case
k=0
when Hypothesis
3
is replaced by
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
r>2
, and in the case
k=n
when Hypothesis
3
is replaced by
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
r>2q,
where
q
is defined by the relation
1/q+1/p=1
. We stress here that
\text{div}{(b)}
is understood in the sense of distributions.

We will also conclude the following regularity result for our solutions.

Corollary 6

(Continuity of the stochastic solutions)
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in\left(C\cap L^{p}\right)(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, let Hypotheses
1
,
2
,
3
hold with
q
defined by the relation
1/q+1/p=1
. Then the unique global
L^{p}
-solution of (
1
) has a continuous modification (i.e. there is a
k
-form-valued process in the same equivalence class that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. is of class
C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
).

Finally, we show that in the deterministic case, the linear Lie transport equation is ill-posed in
L^{p}
due to nonuniqueness of solutions, and that instantaneous blow-up of the supremum norm takes place.

Theorem 7

(Ill-posedness in the deterministic case)
.

In the deterministic case, i.e.
\xi_{i}:=0,

i=1,\ldots,N,
neither Theorem
4
nor Corollary
6
are true. In particular, for
n\in\mathbb{N},

k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\},
and
p\geq 2
with
q
defined by
1/q+1/p=1,
there exist:

1.

An autonomous vector field
b_{\alpha}
of class
(C_{b}^{\alpha}\cap W^{1,q}_{loc})(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
if
n\geq 2
and for any
\alpha\in((q-1)/q,1)
if
n=1,
such that if
kp<n,
there exists
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
(and hence also of class
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
) and a parametric family of different solutions of class
L^{p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
to equation (
85
) with drift
b_{\alpha}
and initial condition
\mathbf{K}_{0}.
Moreover, such solutions are discontinuous in both time and space.

2.

An autonomous divergence-free vector field
b^{\prime}_{\alpha}
of class
(C_{b}^{\alpha}\cap W^{1,q}_{loc})(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
\alpha\in((p-1)/p,1),
such that in the case
k\notin\{0,n\},
there exists
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
for which the
L^{p}
-solution
\mathbf{K}_{t}
to the deterministic equation with drift
b_{\alpha}^{\prime}
and initial condition
\mathbf{K}_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B(0,\epsilon)}\lvert\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)\rvert=\infty,

for any
0<t\leq T
and
\epsilon>0.

4
Regularity of the stochastic flow

This section is devoted to establishing Theorem
2
. In order to show Items
1

\&

2
, we extend the proof of the flow regularity result in
[
18
]
to allow for our more general type of noise. The proof in
[
18
]
is based on the Itô-Tanaka trick and sharp parabolic estimates. We encounter some extra difficulties and show representations of some specific solutions of parabolic equations with coefficients having low regularity in time. To establish Item
3
, we exploit the extra regularity properties of the flow that arise as a result of the Itô-Tanaka trick, to perform certain estimates on the flow equation, which guarantee the validity of certain uniform estimates in
[
30
]
. We note that the existence and uniqueness of strong path-wise solutions to a general class of SDEs containing (
7
) was established in
[
37
]
, which also provide the first example in the literature of well-posedness by noise in the ODE case.

We proceed to demonstrate Theorem
2
. Since our proofs of Items
1

\&

2
comprise a natural extension of the strategy in
[
18
]
, the latter reference can be checked for completeness. For simplicity, for fixed
s\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we occasionally employ the notation
X_{t}:=\phi_{s,t}(x),

t\in[s,T]
. Without loss of generality, we only treat the case
N=1
here (i.e. we only consider one diffusion vector field
\xi=(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{n})
). Incorporating more noise terms is a straightforward extension.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Proof of Item
1
.
The essence of our argument is as follows: we show that the solution to equation (
7
) is unique and can moreover be expressed as a composition of functions which are regular enough (in fact, they are remarkably regular taking into account our assumptions on the drift and diffusions!) to guarantee
C^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}
-smoothness. More concretely, for fixed
s\in[0,T),

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we prove that the process
\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x)):=\phi_{s,t}(x)+\psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x)),

t\geq s,
where
\psi_{\lambda}
is a solution of a parabolic equation on the extended domain
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
depending on a parameter
\lambda\geq 1
, solves a flow equation of the type (
7
) with new coefficients
\widetilde{b},

\widetilde{\xi}
enjoying more regularity than the original ones. Moreover, this equation admits a smooth enough flow of diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{s,t}
(this technique is called the Itô-Tanaka trick or Zvonkin transform. See
[
38
]
for one of the original motivations). By applying results treating the existence, uniqueness, and regularity of solutions of parabolic equations, we can show that
\psi_{\lambda}
enjoys enough regularity.

Moreover, we provide an implicit representation for
\psi_{\lambda}
, which we employ to conclude that
\Psi_{\lambda}
enjoys desired regularity and is invertible if
\lambda
is large enough. Finally, we prove that the regularity of
\Psi_{\lambda}
and
\varphi_{s,t}
is inherited by the flow
\phi_{s,t},
since
\phi_{s,t}=\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s,t}\circ\Psi_{\lambda}
. In order to facilitate the understanding of our argument, we divide this proof into five steps. In Step 1 and Step 2, we treat the regularity of the functions
\psi_{\lambda}
and
\Psi_{\lambda}
, whereas Step 3 is devoted to the Itô-Tanaka trick and the improved regularity of the flow.

Step 1.

First, we extend
b
and
\xi
to the whole real time line
[0,\infty)
by simply defining

\displaystyle b(t,x):=b(T,x),

\displaystyle\xi(t,x):=\xi(T,x),\quad t>T,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By Hypothesis
1
, we have

\displaystyle b\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})),\quad\xi\in C_{b}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{2+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).

For
\lambda\geq 1,
consider the parabolic equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)+L\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)-\lambda\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)=-b(t,x),\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(20)

with

\displaystyle L\psi_{\lambda}(t,x):=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{j}(t,x)\xi^{k}(t,x)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)+(b^{k}(t,x)+\xi^{j}(t,x)\partial_{j}\xi^{k}(t,x))\partial_{k}\psi_{\lambda}(t,x),

(21)

where summation over repeated indices is assumed. The notion of a solution to equation (
20
) is not standard, since
b
is only of class
L^{\infty}
in time instead of continuous. Following
[
26
]
, we prescribe that a function
\psi_{\lambda}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the space
L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{2+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
is a solution to (
20
), if it satisfies the integral equation

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)-\psi_{\lambda}(s,x)=\int_{s}^{t}\big[-L\psi_{\lambda}(r,x)+\lambda\psi_{\lambda}(r,x)-b(r,x)\big]\mathrm{d}r,

(22)

for
0\leq s\leq t
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. From this, it follows that
\psi_{\lambda}
is Lipschitz continuous in time and hence differentiable in time a.e. for fixed
x.
By Theorems 2.4
\&
2.5 in
[
26
]
(set
f:=-b.
It is easy to check that Hypotheses 2.1
\&
2.2 in
[
26
]
are met in the present context), there exists a unique solution
\psi_{\lambda}
to (
20
) in the Banach space
L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{2+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, which moreover, satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\lVert\psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)\rVert_{C_{b}^{2+\alpha}}\lesssim\sup_{t\geq 0}\lVert b(t,\cdot)\rVert_{C_{b}^{\alpha}},

where the constant in
\lesssim
only depends on the parabolicity constants (
13
),
\alpha,
and
n.

Step 2.
Next, we introduce the function
\Psi_{\lambda}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, defined as
\Psi_{\lambda}(t,x):=x+\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)
. By means of the following lemma (originally appearing in
[
18
]
. See Lemma 6), we can establish that
\Psi_{\lambda}
enjoys higher regularity than
\psi_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 8
.

For
\lambda
large enough, the condition

\displaystyle\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}<1

(23)

is met, and the following properties are satisfied:

1.

Uniformly in
t\in[0,\infty),

\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)
has bounded first and second spatial derivatives, and
D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}
is globally
\alpha
-Hölder continuous.

2.

For any
t\in[0,\infty),

\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a diffeomorphism of class
C^{2}.

3.

Uniformly in
t\in[0,\infty),

\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)
has bounded first and second derivatives.

In order to apply Lemma
8
to our function
\psi_{\lambda},
we need to show that for large enough
\lambda
,
\psi_{\lambda}
satisfies condition (
23
). For this, we prove the following intermediate result.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\lambda>0
and
\widetilde{f}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).
Then the parabolic system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{\lambda}(t,x)+L_{0}v_{\lambda}(t,x)-\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x)=\widetilde{f}(t,x),\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(24)

where
(L_{0}v)(t,x):=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{j}(t,x)\xi^{k}(t,x)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}v(t,x)
, has a unique solution
v_{\lambda}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{2+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).
Moreover, there exists a fundamental solution
p(t,x;s,y),

0\leq t\leq s,

x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the operator
\partial_{t}+L_{0}
, such that
v_{\lambda}
can be constructed via the explicit formula

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}(t,x):=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathrm{exp}\left((-\lambda(s-t))\right)p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(25)

Furthermore,
p
is infinitely differentiable in space and there exist constants
\epsilon,C>0
such that for
i,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
with
0\leq i<2m,
we have

\displaystyle\lvert D^{i}p(t,x;s,y)\rvert\leq C(s-t)^{-(n+i)/2m}\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{-\epsilon\lvert x-y\rvert^{2m}}{s-t}\right)^{1/(2m-1)},

(26)

for all
0\leq t<s
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

By Theorems 2.4
\&
2.5 in
[
26
]
, it is known that (
24
) admits a unique solution of class
L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{2+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. However, the explicit representation (
25
) does not follow immediately. We start by assuming that
\widetilde{f}\in C_{b}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).
If
\lambda=0,
Lemma
9
is a classical result (See Theorem 2 in Chapter 9 of
[
20
]
and Theorem 5 in the same chapter for the adjoint system. Conditions (A1), (A2), (A3) therein are clearly met under our assumptions). We claim that for
\lambda>0,

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}(t,x):=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(27)

solves (
24
). Indeed, taking the time derivative on both sides of (
27
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}v_{\lambda}(t,x)&=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}\partial_{t}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad+\lambda\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

(28)

\displaystyle=\text{exp}{(\lambda t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}p(t,x;s,y)(\text{exp}{(-\lambda s)}\widetilde{f}(s,y))\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s+\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x),

(29)

where we used the Leibniz rule in (
28
) and took into account that
\lim_{s\rightarrow t^{+}}p(t,x;s,y)=0
, which follows from (
26
) with
i=0
. Now, define

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}(t,x):=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}p(t,x;s,y)\text{exp}{(-\lambda s)}\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s,

which is a solution to the PDE

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\lambda}(t,x)+L_{0}u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\text{exp}{(-\lambda t)}\widetilde{f}(t,x),

by the classical result
[
20
]
(Theorem 2 in Chapter 9). Then, we have

(
29
)

\displaystyle=\text{exp}{(\lambda t)}\,\partial_{t}u_{\lambda}(t,x)+\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x)

(30)

\displaystyle=\text{exp}{(\lambda t)}\left(-L_{0}u_{\lambda}(t,x)+\text{exp}{(-\lambda t)}\widetilde{f}(t,x)\right)+\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x),

where we have again used Leibniz rule in (
30
) to take the time derivative outside the integrals. Hence

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{\lambda}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\text{exp}{(\lambda t)}(-L_{0}u_{\lambda}(t,x)+\text{exp}{(-\lambda t)}\widetilde{f}(t,x))+\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x)

\displaystyle=-L_{0}v_{\lambda}(t,x)+\lambda v_{\lambda}(t,x)+\widetilde{f}(t,x),

for
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
so we have proven our claim that the representation (
27
) solves (
24
).

Now we treat the general case
\widetilde{f}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).
For
\epsilon>0,
consider the time mollification of
f
(formally,
f
first needs to be extended by zero to the whole real line, then mollified and restricted), which we denote
\widetilde{f}^{\epsilon}\in C_{b}([0,\infty);C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).
From the first part of the proof, we know that

\displaystyle v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(t,x):=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}^{\epsilon}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

solves (
24
) with
\widetilde{f}
substituted by
\widetilde{f}^{\epsilon}.
By the dominated convergence theorem (take into account the mollifier properties, which in particular yield
\lVert\widetilde{f}^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\leq\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}
), we also obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(t,x)\rightarrow\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}s=v_{\lambda}(t,x),

for
t\geq 0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
pointwise. Now, fix a test function
\theta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Then we must have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)-v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(s,\cdot),\theta(\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=\int_{s}^{t}\left(-\langle v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(r,\cdot),(L_{0}^{*}\theta)(r,\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}}+\lambda\langle v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}(r,\cdot),\theta(\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\widetilde{f}(r,\cdot),\theta(\cdot)\rangle_{L^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}r,\end{split}

(31)

for
0\leq t<s
, where we have denoted by
L_{0}^{*}
the
L^{2}
-adjoint operator of
L_{0}.
Hence, taking limits in (
31
) by means of the bounded convergence theorem (use the fact that
v^{\epsilon}_{\lambda}\rightarrow v_{\lambda}
a.e.), we conclude that
v_{\lambda}
satisfies (
24
) weakly. It can also be checked that

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{3}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))\subset L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C^{2+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))

by taking into account its integral representation (
27
) and using property (
26
), so it is indeed a solution in the sense (
22
). Since (
24
) has one and only one solution, it must be (
25
). The proof is now complete.
∎

Step 3.

We can now show that
\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\rightarrow 0,
as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty.
By setting

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(t,x):=-b(t,x)-\left(b^{k}(t,x)+\xi^{j}(t,x)\partial_{j}\xi^{k}(t,x)\right)\partial_{k}\psi_{\lambda},

(32)

then by Lemma
9
, we must have the following implicit formula

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)=\int_{t}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\text{exp}{(-\lambda(s-t))}p(t,x;s,y)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

for the solution to the parabolic system (
20
).
From estimate (
26
) with
i=1
(consider the change of variables
u=s-t)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lvert D\psi_{\lambda}(t,x)\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\lambda u}u^{-(n+1)/2}\text{exp}\left(\frac{-\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}}{u}\right)\widetilde{f}(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\lambda u}u^{-1/2}\text{exp}\left[-\lvert z\rvert^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda u}u^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}u=\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r}r^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\lVert\widetilde{f}\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}}{\lambda^{1/2}},

where we have made the change of variables
y-x=z\sqrt{u}
and
r=\lambda u
. We stress that the constants
\lesssim
in the inequalities above are independent of
\lambda,
since inequality (
26
) only involves the fundamental solution of (
20
). Therefore, there exists a constant
C>0
that is independent of
\lambda
, such that

\displaystyle\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L_{t,x}^{\infty}}\leq C\lambda^{-1/2}\|\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}.

Hence from (
32
), we have the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\leq C\lambda^{-1/2}\left(\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}+\left(\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}+\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\right)\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\right),

which, after minor rearranging, gives us

\displaystyle\left(1-C\lambda^{-1/2}\left(\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}+\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\right)\right)\lVert D\psi_{\lambda}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\leq C\lambda^{-1/2}\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}.

(33)

From the last inequality (
33
), it follows that by choosing
\lambda
large enough, one can obtain the bound (
23
), ensuring that Lemma
8
can be invoked.

Step 4.

We now detail the most important step in proving Item
1
, which is to carry out the so-called Itô-Tanaka trick. Define the vector fields

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}(t,y):=\lambda\psi_{\lambda}(t,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,y))+\frac{1}{2}\left(\xi\cdot D\xi\right)(t,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,y)),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}(t,y):=\xi(t,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,y))D\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,y)),

and consider for
s\in[0,T],

y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
the following SDE in Itô form:

\displaystyle Y_{t}=y+\int_{s}^{t}\widetilde{b}(u,Y_{u})\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{s}^{t}\widetilde{\xi}(u,Y_{u})\,\mathrm{d}W_{u},\quad t\in[s,T].

(34)

We claim that equation (
34
) is equivalent to the characteristic equation (
7
) in the following sense: if
\phi_{s,t}(x)
solves (
7
), then
Y_{t}:=\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x))
solves (
34
) with
y=\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)
. This can be shown by applying Itô’s Lemma to the function
\Psi_{\lambda}
. However, in doing so, one needs to be careful since this function does not enjoy enough regularity in time to satisfy the assumptions of the classical Itô’s Lemma. To this end, we use the following weak-in-time version of Itô’s Lemma, established in
[
18
]
(see Lemma 3).

Lemma 10
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\Psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{2+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),
satisfying

\displaystyle\Psi(t,x)-\Psi(s,x)=\int_{s}^{t}v(r,x)\,\mathrm{d}r,\quad 0\leq s\leq t,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

with
v\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})).
Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a stochastic process of the form

\displaystyle X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\xi(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},

where
b,\xi:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\xi(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\geq 0,

is an Itô process for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\Psi(t,X_{t})

\displaystyle=\Psi(0,X_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\left(v+(D\Psi)^{T}b\right)(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\text{tr}{\left(\xi^{T}\left(H\Psi\right)\xi\right)}(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left((D\Psi\right)^{T}\xi)(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\geq 0,

where
H\Psi
denotes the (spatial) Hessian matrix of
\Psi
.
