Remark 10
.

Lemma
10
can be extended in a straightforward manner to allow for multiple diffusion terms and applied to functions
\Psi\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);C_{b}^{2+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
component-wise.

Fix
s\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Applying Lemma
10
to
X_{t}:=\phi_{s,t}(x)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi_{\lambda}(t,X_{t})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)+\int_{s}^{t}v(r,X_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{s}^{t}\left((D\Psi_{\lambda})^{T}\left(b+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\xi\right)\right)(r,X_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\left(\text{tr}{[\xi^{T}(H_{X}\Psi_{\lambda})\xi]}\right)(r,X_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{s}^{t}((D\Psi_{\lambda})^{T}\xi)(r,X_{r})\,\mathrm{d}W_{r},

where
t\geq s
and

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=\lambda\psi_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\psi_{\lambda}-\left(b^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{i}\partial_{i}\xi^{j}\right)\partial_{j}\psi_{\lambda}-b,

\displaystyle\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}\right)^{T}\left(b+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\xi\right)

\displaystyle=b+b^{j}\partial_{j}\psi_{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\xi+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{i}\partial_{i}\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\psi_{\lambda},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\left(\xi^{T}\left(H_{X}\Psi_{\lambda}\right)\xi\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\psi_{\lambda},

which yields (
34
). We note that
\widetilde{b},\widetilde{\xi}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C_{b}^{1+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and by the classical results in
[
29
]
, equation (
34
) has a unique strong solution which defines a flow of
C^{1+\alpha^{\prime}}
-diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{s,t}
,
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,
for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha).
As in
[
18
]
, owing to the regularity shown for
\psi_{\lambda}
in Step 1 and for
\Psi_{\lambda}
in Step 2, and using the fact that
\phi_{s,t}=\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s,t}\circ\Psi_{\lambda},
we conclude that
\phi_{s,t}
enjoys the extra regularity specified in Theorem
2
and the solution to (
7
) is unique, which proves Item
1
.

Proof of Item
2
.
The argument follows almost verbatim the techniques in
[
18
]
, so we omit it.

Proof of Item
3
.

The following lemma can be derived as a special case of Corollary 5.3 in
[
30
]
, where Hölder properties of flows of SDEs with stochastic coefficients are analysed. Our lemma is obtained by setting
l=0,

\delta=\alpha,

X_{s}:=\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert,
for
s\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
applying Corollary 5.3 in
[
30
]
with these constants, and then taking suprema in
0\leq s\leq T.
We note that the inequality shown in Corollary 5.3 in
[
30
]
is also valid for the suprema since their constant
N
is independent of
X
and our constant
C
in Lemma
11
is independent of
s.

∎

Lemma 11
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
r\geq 2
be a constant satisfying
\alpha r>n
. Moreover, assume that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert^{r}\right]\leq C,

(35)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}(y)\rvert^{r}\right]\leq C\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha r},\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(36)

Then for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha-n/r)
and
\epsilon>n/r
, property (
19
) holds.

Hence, in order to establish (
19
), it suffices to verify estimates (
35
) and (
36
), which we claim to be true in our setting. Since the proof of this claim is technical and lengthy, we include it in Appendix
8.1
.

Remark 11
.

We note that Items
2

\&

3
in Theorem
2
also hold for the inverse flow
\phi_{s,t}^{-1}
. Indeed, by using the flow properties (and formal Stratonovich notation for simplicity), we observe that

\displaystyle\phi_{s,t}(\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(y))=\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(y)+\int_{s}^{t}b(r,\phi_{s,r}(\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(y)))\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\xi_{i}(r,\phi_{s,r}(\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(y)))\circ\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{i},

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\phi_{s,t}^{-1}(y)=y-\int_{s}^{t}b(r,\phi_{r,t}^{-1}(y))\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\xi_{i}(r,\phi_{r,t}^{-1}(y))\circ\mathrm{d}W_{r}^{i},

where
0\leq s\leq t,

y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
so for fixed
t\in[0,T],
the inverse flow satisfies a backward SDE with a sign change in the drift and diffusion coefficients and hence enjoys similar properties.

We present the following important consequence of Theorem
2
(
3
).

Corollary 12
.

Let
R>0
and
r\geq 1.
We have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B(0,R)}\lvert\phi_{t}^{n}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0,

(37)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B(0,R)}\lvert D\phi_{t}^{n}(x)-D\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0.

(38)

Finally, we state a remarkable result shown in
[
18
]
(see Theorem 11) that translates easily into our context.

Theorem 13
.

Let the drift and diffusion vector fields satisfy Hypothesis
1
and let
r>q\geq 2
. If
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
J\phi\in L^{q}([0,T];W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})).

Proof.

The result follows by a simple modification of the proof of Theorem 11 in
[
18
]
(note that the employed parabolic estimates in Sobolev spaces also hold for our general system by Theorem 9, Chapter 7 in
[
24
]
).
∎

In the following result, we show that if the drift and diffusion vector fields of (
7
) are divergence-free
in the sense of distributions
, the stochastic flow constructed in Theorem
2
is a.s. volume-preserving. This is proven in
[
18
]
in the case
\xi_{i}:=e_{i},

i=1,\ldots,n,
and we follow exactly the same argument.

Theorem 14
.

Let
b
and
\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
satisfy Hypothesis
1
. Assume that
\text{div}{(b)}=\text{div}{(\xi_{i})}=0,

i=1,\ldots,N,
in the sense of distributions. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the flow of (
7
) is volume-preserving, i.e.
J\phi_{s,t}(x)=1
, for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Proof.

We assume without loss of generality that
N=1.
Let
\{b^{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of vector fields such that
\text{div}{(b^{j})}=0,

b^{j}\rightarrow b
in
L^{\infty}\left([0,T];C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
,
j\rightarrow\infty
(these can be constructed as in the proof of Lemma 9 in
[
18
]
). Let
\{\phi^{j}_{s,t}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be the corresponding sequence of associated stochastic flows (with fixed diffusion vector field
\xi
). Applying Theorem II.3.1 in
[
28
]
, we obtain
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
J\phi^{j}_{s,t}(x)=1
, for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix
s\in(0,T]
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.
By taking into account (
18
), there existence a subsequence
\{D\phi_{s,t}^{j_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
(depending on
(s,x)
) such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\lvert D\phi_{s,t}^{j_{k}}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert^{2}\rightarrow 0,\quad j_{k}\rightarrow\infty.

Since
J\phi
is well-defined, we must have
J\phi_{s,t}(x)=1
. The proof is complete.
∎

5
Existence of stochastic solutions

In this section, we prove our main theorem of existence of solutions to equation (
1
), namely Theorem
3
. Only sometimes throughout the rest of this paper (when the notation becomes especially convoluted in a proof), the
L^{r}
norms of measurable objects defined on the measure spaces
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}),

([0,T],\lambda([0,T]),\lambda),
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\lambda(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\lambda),

where
\lambda(\cdot)
represents the Lebesgue sigma-algebra and
\lambda
the Lebesgue measure, may be denoted by
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{r}_{\omega}},

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{r}_{t}},
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{r}_{x}},
respectively. We may also use notations of the type
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{r}_{x},L^{q}_{t}}
for the mixed norms in the product spaces.

5.1
Existence for regular drift and initial condition

First, we establish the existence of weak solutions in the case when the drift vector field
b
is smooth in space. Then we extend these results to the case when
b
is only
\alpha
-Hölder continuous and satisfies Hypothesis
2
by means of a limiting argument.

Proposition 15

(Existence of classical solutions for regular drift and initial condition)
.

Enforce Assumption
1
with
m=3
and
\beta\in(0,1),
and let
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then there exists a strong solution to equation (
1
) of the form
\mathbf{K}_{t}(x):=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)
, where
\phi_{t}
is the flow of (
8
).

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, we assume without loss of generality that
N=1
. By Assumption
1
with
m=3
,
\phi_{t}
is a stochastic flow of
C^{3}
-diffeomorphisms.

Applying Theorem
9
, we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)=\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)-\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{b}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}](x)\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}](x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 130.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}[(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}](x)\,\mathrm{d}s,

for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Hence
\mathbf{K}_{t}
solves (
1
) in the sense of Definition
5
. For this, we note that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\mathbf{K}
has paths of class
C([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, since by definition,

\displaystyle((\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=(K_{0})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)

(39)

holds in global coordinates, where
\psi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}
is the inverse flow and summation over repeated indices is assumed.
∎

Next, we prove the existence of weak solutions of the linear transport of
k
-forms (
1
) when the drift vector field
b
is smooth in space and the initial condition is of low regularity. First we establish some useful preliminary lemmas. In the following, we let
J\phi
represent the
determinant
of the Jacobian matrix of a diffeomorphism
\phi
.

5.2
Auxiliary results

Lemma 16
.

Given a
k
-form
\mathbf{K}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\phi
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\phi_{*}\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}(y),\lvert J\phi(y)\rvert\left(\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(y)\right)^{\flat}\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y,

where
\sharp
and
\flat
are the sharp and flat operators, respectively (see Appendix
9
for the definitions).

Remark 12
.

Since we are only using the Euclidean metric, for a
k
-form with expression
\mathbf{\Theta}(x)=\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}}(x)
, the corresponding
k
-vector field
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}=\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x),

and similarly for a
k
-vector field
\mathbf{V}(x)=V^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)(\partial/\partial x_{i_{1}})(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge(\partial/\partial x_{i_{k}})(x)
, the corresponding
k
-form
\mathbf{V}^{\flat}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{\flat}=V^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}}(x),

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and we assume summation over repeated indices as usually.

Proof.

By standard tensor identities, we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{*}\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x))=\left<\phi_{*}\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(x)\right>=\left<\mathbf{K}(\phi^{-1}(x)),\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(\phi^{-1}(x))\right>.

Hence, by the change of coordinates
y=\phi^{-1}(x)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\phi_{*}\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left<\mathbf{K}(\phi^{-1}(x)),\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(\phi^{-1}(x))\right>\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left<\mathbf{K}(y),\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(y)\right>\lvert J\phi(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}(y),\lvert J\phi(y)\rvert(\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(y))^{\flat}\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y.

∎

Remark 13
.

When changing coordinates in Lemma
16
, we observe that the term
\lvert J\phi(y)\rvert(\phi^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(y))^{\flat}
appears. For simplicity in our future calculations, we will often denote it by
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}.

We introduce a lemma that we will employ several times.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\phi_{t}
be a flow of
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. we have the following properties

1.

\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}
(see previous remark for the notation used) has compact support, uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
.

2.

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
R>0
such that the support of
\mathbf{\Theta}
is contained in the ball
B(0,R)
. Since
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., the map
\phi_{\cdot}^{-1}(\cdot)
is continuous in both time and space (and noting that the image of a compact set under a continuous map is compact), we can choose
R^{*}:\Omega\rightarrow(0,\infty]

5
5

5

More concretely,
R^{*}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T],x\in B(0,R)}\lvert\phi_{t}^{-1}(x)\rvert,
which is measurable and hence a random variable.
such that
R^{*}<\infty

a.s.
and

\displaystyle\{\phi_{t}^{-1}(y):\hskip 2.84544ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544pty\in B(0,R)\}\subset B(0,R^{*}).

(40)

This implies that the support of
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}
is contained in
B(0,R^{*})
, which proves
1
. We observe that by definition, we have the explicit expression (assume summation over repeated indices)

\displaystyle(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(\phi_{t}(x))\cdots\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(\phi_{t}(x))

(41)

for the pull-back of the
k
-vector field
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}
in global coordinates. Since
\phi_{t}
is a flow of
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms, both its (first order) derivatives and the derivatives of the inverse flow are
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. continuous in time and space. Hence
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}
is uniformly bounded on
B(0,R^{*})
, since every continuous function on a compact set reaches a maximum.
6
6

6

We note that in our case, since we consider the Euclidean metric, the
k
-vector field
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}
has the same coordinate expression as
\mathbf{\Theta}
, and the
k
-form
(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}
has the same coordinate expression as
\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}.

We can therefore conclude
2
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\phi_{t}
be a stochastic flow of
C^{2}
-diffeomorphisms. Moreover, assume that the drift vector field
b
satisfies Hypothesis
2

(i)
and
\xi\in C([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

1.

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)
has compact support, uniformly in
t\in[0,T],
and
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{q}}
is of class
L^{q}([0,T])
.

2.

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)
has compact support, uniformly in
t\in[0,T],
and
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}
is of class
L^{\infty}([0,T])
.

3.

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}}(t)
has compact support, uniformly in
t\in[0,T],
and
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}
is of class
L^{\infty}([0,T]).

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}
be supported on
B(0,R).
By Hypothesis
2

(i)
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}
is well-defined, and therefore

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t,x)=\lvert J\phi_{t}(x)\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta})^{\sharp})^{\flat}(x),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

is also well-defined. Hence, by the arguments in the proof of Lemma
17

1
, it must be supported on
B(0,R^{*})
, where
R^{*}
is defined as in (
40
). By a similar argument, one can check that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}}(t)
are well-defined (take into account the regularity assumed on
\phi_{t}
and
\xi
). Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)
is supported on
B(0,R^{*})
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}}(t)
is supported on
B(0,R^{**}),
where
R^{**}:\Omega\rightarrow(0,\infty]
satisfies
R^{**}<\infty

a.s.
and is chosen such that

\displaystyle\{\phi_{t}^{-1}(y):\hskip 2.84544ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544pty\in B(0,R^{*})\}\subset B(0,R^{**}).

Moreover, by applying Hölder’s inequality, we can obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t)\rVert_{L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim\lVert J\phi_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert D\psi_{t}\rVert^{k}_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}}},

(42)

where we have employed the notation
\psi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}
and used the explicit formula for the pull-back of a
k
-vector field (
41
). Since
R^{*}<\infty
a.s., we have a.s.
\lVert J\phi_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]),

\lVert D\psi_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]),
and by Hypothesis
2

(i)

\lVert\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{q}([0,T]),
so (
42
) is of class
L^{q}([0,T])
and we conclude
1
. For
2
, the argument is similar by noting that a.s.,
\lVert\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
holds. Finally, for
3
, observe that

\displaystyle\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}}(t)\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\lVert J\phi_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{**}}}\lVert D\psi_{t}\rVert^{k}_{L^{\infty}_{R^{**}}}\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{**}}},

(43)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t,x)=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}(\lvert J\phi_{t}\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta})^{\sharp})^{\flat})(x).
By taking into account the definition of the pull-back of a vector field (
41
) and of the adjoint Lie derivative (
133
), we observe that
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}}
is expressed in coordinates as a sum that only contains products where at most two partial derivatives of the flow
\phi_{t},
its inverse
\psi_{t},
and
\xi
are involved. By our assumptions, these are locally uniformly bounded in space and time, so for
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\omega
such that
R^{**}(\omega)<\infty,

\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}}(t,x)
must be uniformly bounded in space and time. Therefore (
43
) is a.s. of class
L^{\infty}([0,T])
and the proof is complete.
∎

A final short lemma will be needed.

Lemma 19
.

Let Hypothesis
1
and Hypothesis
2

(ii)
hold and
\phi_{t}
be the unique stochastic flow of
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms defined by (
8
). Given
\epsilon>0,
let
b^{\epsilon}
and
\xi_{i}^{\epsilon},

i=1,\ldots,N
be the mollifications of
b
and
\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N,
respectively, and
\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}
be the flow of (
8
) with drift vector field
b^{\epsilon}
and diffusion vector fields
\xi_{i}^{\epsilon},

i=1,\ldots,N
. Then, for any
R>0
and
r\geq 2
, we have the following

\displaystyle\sup_{\epsilon>0}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[J\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)]^{-r}\right]<\infty,

(44)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[J\phi_{t}(x)]^{-r}\right]<\infty.

(45)

Proof.

We assume without loss of generality that
N=1.
Let
r\geq 2.
For
\epsilon>0,
define
f^{\epsilon}_{t}(x):=[J\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)]^{-r},

f_{t}(x):=[J\phi_{t}(x)]^{-r},
and

\displaystyle g_{t}^{\epsilon}(x):=\text{exp}{\left(-r\int_{0}^{t}\text{div}\,b^{\epsilon}(s,\phi^{\epsilon}_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}s-r\int_{0}^{t}\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}(s,\phi^{\epsilon}_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right)},

for
t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We note that
f^{\epsilon}
and
f
are well-defined since
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
J\phi^{\epsilon},

J\phi>0
because
\phi_{t}^{\epsilon},

\phi_{t}
(respectively) are orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms for all
t\in[0,T]
. By classical results, we know that for a.e.
(\omega,t,x)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\log J\phi_{t}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\text{div}\,b^{\epsilon}(s,\phi^{\epsilon}_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}(s,\phi^{\epsilon}_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},

so
\text{log}\,f^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=\text{log}\,g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)
and hence
f^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)=g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)
. Moreover, by the mollifier properties and Hypothesis
2

(ii)
, we have
-\text{div}\,b^{\epsilon}\leq-C.
Indeed

\displaystyle-\text{div}\,b^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}-\text{div}\,b(t,x-y)\rho^{\epsilon}(y)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}-C\rho^{\epsilon}(y)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle=-C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(y)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y=-C,

for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Also, since
D\xi\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
by Hypothesis
1

(i)
, and taking into account the mollifier properties, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that the uniform bound
\lvert\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}\rvert\leq C^{\prime}
holds. Hence, by setting
X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x):=-\int_{0}^{s}\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}(u,\phi_{u}^{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}
for
s\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}g_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\text{exp}{\left(r\lvert C\rvert T+r\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\text{exp}{\left(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{r}},\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(46)

where the constant in the last inequality above is independent of
\epsilon.
Applying Lemma
10
, the diffusion coefficient of the process
\text{exp}{(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x))}
is
\text{exp}{(X_{u}^{\epsilon}(x))}\,\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}(u,\phi_{u}^{\epsilon}(x)),
and therefore its quadratic variation can be calculated and bounded as

\displaystyle\left[\text{exp}{\left(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)}\right]_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\text{exp}{(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x))}\,\text{div}\,\xi^{\epsilon}(s,\phi_{s}^{\epsilon}(x))\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(C^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{0\leq u\leq s}\text{exp}{(X_{u}^{\epsilon}(x))}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Hence by Burkholder-Davis-Gundy and Hölder’s inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\text{exp}{\left(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{r}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left[\text{exp}{\left(X_{s}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)}\right]^{r/2}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq u\leq s}\text{exp}{(X_{u}^{\epsilon}(x))}^{r}\right]\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where the constants in the inequalities above are independent of
\epsilon.
By applying Grönwall’s inequality, we find that (
46
) is finite, independently of
x,
so by taking suprema in time and space we obtain (
44
).

To prove (
45
), we first note that by taking into account (
38
), we obtain that for any
R>0,

J\phi^{\epsilon}\rightarrow J\phi
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times B(0,R);\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0,
and hence there exists a subsequence (which we call again
\epsilon
) such that for a.e.
(\omega,t,x)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

f_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\rightarrow f_{t}(x)
when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0.
Moreover, since
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
f
is continuous in time and space, then for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
the map
f_{\cdot}(x)
is continuous and hence reaches a maximum
f_{t^{*}_{x}}(x)=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}f_{t}(x)
.
Hence by applying the bound (
46
) with
t=T
and invoking Fatou’s Lemma, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}f_{t}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{t^{*}_{x}}(x)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}f_{t^{*}_{x}}^{\epsilon}(x)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}g_{t^{*}_{x}}^{\epsilon}(x)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}g_{t^{*}_{x}}^{\epsilon}(x)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{divergence}}}{{\lesssim}}\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\text{exp}{\left(X_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{r}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{Fatou})}}{{\lesssim}}\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\text{exp}{\left(X_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{r}}\right].

Finally, by (
44
) we have
\liminf_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\text{exp}{\left(X_{t}^{\epsilon}(x)\right)^{r}}\right]<\infty
independently of
x.
The proof is complete.
∎

5.3
Existence for regular drift and weak initial condition

Proposition 20

(Existence of
L^{p}
-solutions for regular drift and weak initial condition)
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
b,

\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
satisfy Assumption
1
with
m=3
and
\beta\in(0,1)
and Hypothesis
2

(ii)
. Then there exists an
L^{p}
-solution to equation (
1
) of the form
\mathbf{K}_{t}(x):=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x),
where
\phi_{t}
is the flow of (
8
).

Proof.

Step 1.
\mathbf{K}
satisfies (
1
) weakly.
We assume without loss of generality that
N=1
. Consider the mollified initial datum
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}:=\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathbf{K}_{0}
. By the embedding
L^{p}_{loc}\subset L^{q}_{loc}
for
p\geq q
and the mollifier properties,
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{0},

\epsilon\rightarrow 0,
strongly in
L^{r}_{loc},
for any
r\in[1,p],
and therefore also weakly. Moreover, by Proposition
15
,
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\epsilon}(x):=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}(x)
is a strong solution to (
1
) with initial datum
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}
and must satisfy the equation weakly. Hence, for any test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
we have
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0pt-\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

for all
t\in[0,T].
We also note that
\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is a.s. continuous in time, since from Theorem
2
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\psi_{t}
and its spatial derivatives are continuous in
(t,x).
We analyse term-by-term convergence in the last equality for fixed
t\in[0,T]
.

•

By the weak convergence
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{0}
in
L^{2}_{loc}
, the second term on the left-hand side converges as
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rightarrow\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}.

•

We claim that the first term on the left-hand side converges a.s. as

\displaystyle\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rightarrow\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}(x)=\lvert J\phi_{t}(x)\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}(x)
as explained in Lemma
16
. For this, note that a.s.

\displaystyle\lvert\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{0},\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rvert\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{0}\rVert_{L^{1}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\rightarrow 0,

where we have taken into account the following facts (i)
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}
has compact support contained in
B(0,R^{*}),
uniformly in
t\in[0,T],
where
R^{*}<\infty
a.s., (ii)
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}<\infty
(use Lemma
17

1
&
2
for (i) & (ii)), and (iii)
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{0}
in
L^{1}(B(0,R^{*}),\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

•

By the bounded convergence theorem, the first term on the right-hand side converges a.s. as

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0pt\rightarrow\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

First note that for such a regular drift
b,
it is easy to check that Hypothesis
2

(i)
holds (our assumptions on
b
are substantially stronger) so we can apply Lemma
18

1
. We claim that a.s. we have

\displaystyle\lvert\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{0},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rvert\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{0}\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{p}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}}}\rightarrow 0,

pointwise in
s\in[0,T],
since (i) from the mollifier properties
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{0}
in
L^{p},
(ii)
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)
is supported on
B(0,R^{*})
, uniformly in
s\in[0,T],
(iii)
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)
is of spatial class
L^{q}
(use Lemma
18

1
for (ii) & (iii)). Moreover, we have the following bound in
L^{1}([0,T]).

\displaystyle\lvert\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rvert\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{p}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rVert_{L^{q}}\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}\rVert_{L^{p}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}(s)\rVert_{L^{q}},

since
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{q}}\in L^{q}([0,T])
from Lemma
18

1
.

•

The Itô-correction term converges a.s. as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

by similar arguments as the ones for the last term by taking into account Lemma
18

3
.

•

Finally, by the stochastic dominated convergence theorem, the martingale term converges as

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}\rightarrow\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle(\phi_{s})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s},

in probability (apply the previous strategies using Lemma
18

2
).

Step 2.
\mathbf{K}
is of class
L^{p}
.
We observe that by definition

\displaystyle((\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=(K_{0})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x),

(47)

in global coordinates, where
\psi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}
. Hence, by considering the change of variables
x=\phi_{t}(y),
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}_{0}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{pk}\lvert J\phi_{t}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}_{0}(y)\rvert^{p}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{2pk}\mathrm{d}t\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\lvert J\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}^{n}y

(48)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{2pk}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert J\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{K}_{0}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{2pk}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(49)

where we have used Tonelli’s theorem to change the order of integration and Hölder’s inequality in the space
\Omega\times[0,T]
in (
48
), and taken into account that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert J\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\right]\lesssim\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\lesssim\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\right]+\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}(y)-D\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2}\right],

by (
17
) & (
18
) , and the fact that
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
in (
49
). To conclude, we show that (
49
) is finite. For this, we first note that
D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))=[D\phi_{t}(y)]^{-1},
for
t\in[0,T]
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
where
[D\phi_{t}(y)]^{-1}
denotes the inverse of the Fréchet matrix
D\phi_{t}(y).
Therefore, we have
D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))=[J\phi_{t}(y)]^{-1}\text{Adj}(D\phi_{t}(y))
and

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{2pk}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[J\phi_{t}(y)]^{-4pk}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert\text{Adj}(D\phi_{t}(y))\rvert^{4pk}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[J\phi_{t}(y)]^{-4pk}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{t}(y)\rvert^{4pk(n-1)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[J\phi_{t}(y)]^{-4pk}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty,

where we have applied Hölder’s inequality, taken into account that all the terms in
\text{Adj}(D\phi_{t}(y))
are sums of products of
(n-1)
components of the matrix
D\phi_{t}(y),
and applied (
18
). Finally, the term in the last line is finite due to (
45
).
∎

5.4
Existence for weak drift and initial condition

We proceed to reduce the regularity on the drift and establish our main existence theorem.

Theorem 21

(Existence of
L^{p}
-solutions for weak drift and initial condition)
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
b,

\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
satisfy Hypotheses
1

\&

2
with
q
such that
1/q+1/p=1
. Then there exists an
L^{p}
-solution to (
1
).

Proof.

For simplicity, we treat the case
N=1.
First, we take the mollified vector field
b^{\epsilon}:=\rho^{\epsilon}*b
and consider equation (
1
) with drift vector field
b^{\epsilon}
instead of
b
. Let
\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}
be the smooth flow of (
8
) with drift vector field
b^{\epsilon}
and define
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}_{t}(x):=(\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)
, which is a weak solution of (
1
) by Proposition
20
. By imitating the argument in Step 2 of the last proposition, considering the change of coordinates
x=\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(y)
and taking into account properties (
17
) and (
44
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}_{t}(x)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C<\infty,

(50)

where the constant
C
depends on
p
but is independent of
0<\epsilon<1.
Hence there exists a subsequence
\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow 0
and a
k
-form-valued stochastic process
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon_{n}}
converges weakly to
\mathbf{K}
in
L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and, without loss of generality, also in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Indeed, bound (
50
) holds in
L^{2}
for
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon_{n}}
by following Step 2 in Proposition
20
with
p=2
and applying Hölder’s inequality in space with conjugate exponents
p^{\prime}=p/2
and
q^{\prime}=p/(p-2)
in (
48
). Hence, a new subsequence converging weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be subtracted by iteration. Its weak limit must again be
\mathbf{K}
. This means that

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon_{n}}_{t}(x)-\mathbf{K}_{t}(x),\mathbf{F}(\omega,t,x)\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,x}}\rightarrow 0,\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

for any
\mathbf{F}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. From now on, we shall write
\epsilon
instead of
\epsilon_{n}
for the sake of simplicity. We fix a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Note that the process
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is adapted by definition and converges to
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]),
from the weak convergence of
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is adapted and the set of adapted processes in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
is closed (and therefore weakly closed),
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is adapted.
Taking into account this last fact, it is not difficult to show that
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is also adapted.

Moreover, by Hölder’s inequality and using the fact that
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
together with Hypothesis
2

(i)
, we have
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\in L^{1}(\Omega\times[0,T]),
so its time integral is well defined. It can also be checked that
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is of class
L^{1}(\Omega\times[0,T]),
so their time integrals are well-defined. Moreover,
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
.

We know that
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
satisfies equation (
15
), and hence it also satisfies it weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
We claim that this property is transferred in the weak limit, so
\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
also satisfies (
15
), weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
We include the proof of this claim in Appendix
8.2
to prevent this proof from becoming excessively convoluted. Therefore we may pass to the weak
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
-limit in the equation for
\mathbf{K}
and (
15
) holds for almost every
\left(\omega,t\right)\in\Omega\times[0,T]
. The proof is complete.
∎

Until now, we have constructed a solution to our equation but we have not proven that there exists a solution with the particular form
(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x).

To show this, we require the following technical lemma.

Lemma 22
.

Let
b
and
\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
satisfy Hypothesis
1
and
\{b^{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of drift vector fields such that
b^{m}\rightarrow b
in
L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})),

m\rightarrow\infty.
Moreover, let
\{\phi_{t}^{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
be the corresponding sequence of stochastic flows solving (
7
) with drift vector fields
b^{m}
and fixed diffusion vector fields
\xi_{i},

i=1,\ldots,N
. Let
r\geq 2
such that
\alpha r>(n+1)/2^{k+1}
and
R>0.
Then for any multi-vector field
\mathbf{U}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact support on
B(0,R),
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\left((\phi_{t}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U}(x)-(\phi_{t})^{*}\mathbf{U}(x)\right)^{r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\rightarrow 0,\quad m\rightarrow\infty,

(51)

where
R^{*}(m)<\infty
a.s. is chosen such that

\Big\{(\phi_{t}^{m})^{-1}(y):\hskip 2.84544ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544pty\in B(0,R)\Big\}\cup\Big\{\phi_{t}^{-1}(y):\hskip 2.84544ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544pty\in B(0,R)\Big\}\subset B(0,{R}^{*}(m)),

and we assume
B(0,R^{*}(m))\subset B(0,R^{\prime})

7
7

7

Note that this is guaranteed for fixed
m,
but
R^{\prime}
would depend on
m,
so this assumption is important.
with deterministic
R^{\prime}<\infty,
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}.

Since this lemma is quite technical, we prove it in Appendix
8.3
. The following proposition concludes this existence section.

Theorem 23
.

In the conditions of Theorem
21
, there exists an
L^{p}
-solution to (
1
) of the form
\mathbf{K}_{t}(x):=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x),
where
\phi_{t}
is the flow of (
8
).

Proof.

Step 1.
First we assume that
\mathbf{K}_{0}
is supported on
B(0,R).
We will show that we can choose a sequence converging to a weak solution of (
1
), which we denote by
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
for convenience, such that for every test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}-(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rightarrow 0,
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
From this, we can conclude that
\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
satisfies equation (
15
) for almost every
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T].
Since by the flow properties
(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}
is adapted and a.s. continuous, it is a solution in the sense prescribed in Definition
6
.

Indeed, we select a convergent (sub)sequence
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
by applying the same argument as we did previously in the proof of Theorem
21
. Fix a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which we assume without loss of generality to be supported on
B(0,R)
. We claim that
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
converges to
\langle\langle(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).

Fix a test function
f\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
Let us adopt the notation
\|\cdot\|_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R}}
to denote the
L^{r}
-norm of a tensor on the set
\Omega\times[0,T]\times B(0,R)
. Recalling that
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}_{t}=(\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}
, where
\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}
is the stochastic flow of (
1
) with
b^{\epsilon}
, the mollified drift, instead of
b
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\epsilon}-(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}_{0},\lvert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}\rvert((\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}-\lvert J\phi_{t}\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}_{0},\lvert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}\rvert((\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}-\lvert J\phi^{\epsilon}_{t}\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}_{0},\lvert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}\rvert((\phi_{t})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}-\lvert J\phi_{t}\rvert(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}\lVert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}\,(((\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}-(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat})\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}\lVert(J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}-J\phi_{t})(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}},

where we took into account Lemma
16
in the second line and Hölder’s inequality in the last step. We check that the last lines above converges to zero. First we note

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}_{R}}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t}}<\infty.

Moreover, choosing constants
r\geq 2
such that
2r\alpha>(n+1)/2^{k+1}
and
s>1
satisfying
1/r+1/s=1
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}(((\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}-(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat})\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}\leq\lVert J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{2s}_{\omega,t,R}}\lVert(\phi_{t}^{\epsilon})^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}-\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}\rVert_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R}}\rightarrow 0,

where we have used (
38
) to control the Jacobian determinant term together with Lemma
22
to obtain convergence of the second term.
By applying similar techniques we can also derive

\displaystyle\lVert\left(J\phi_{t}^{\epsilon}-J\phi_{t}\right)(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\rVert_{L^{2}_{\omega,t,R}}\rightarrow 0.

Note that the hypothesis in Lemma
22
is verified with
R^{\prime}=R
(the hypothesis would not hold had we not assumed that
\mathbf{K}
is compactly supported, so this was crucial). Hence, our argument is complete in the case when
\mathbf{K}_{0}
has compact support.

Step 2.
Now we treat the general case
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
without restriction on the support. We choose
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
B(0,1)
such that
\zeta(x)=1,

x\in B(0,1/2)
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N},
let
\mathbf{K}_{0}^{m}(x):=\mathbf{K}_{0}\left(x\right)\zeta(x/m),
which is compactly supported. By taking Step 1, we can construct a corresponding solution
\mathbf{K}^{m}
with initial datum
\mathbf{K}^{m}_{0}.
Therefore we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{\mathbf{K}}_{0}^{m},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 50.0pt-\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(52)

t\in[0,T],
for any test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We know from the first part of our proof that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t}(x)=(\phi_{t})_{*}(\mathbf{K}_{0}(\cdot)\zeta(\cdot/m))(x)=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)\zeta(\phi_{t}^{-1}(x)/m),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where we have employed the property
(\phi_{t})_{*}(\mathbf{K}f)(x)=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}(x)(\phi_{t})_{*}f(x)
and taken into account that
(\phi_{t})_{*}(x)=f(\phi_{t}^{-1}(x))
for smooth scalar functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. It is clear that a.s., the sequence
\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t}
converges to
\mathbf{K}_{t}:=(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}
pointwise in
(t,x)
. We want to show that
\mathbf{K}_{t}
also satisfies (
52
). To this end, we first fix a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
B(0,R)
. As in the proof of Lemma
17
, we choose a random variable
R^{*}:\Omega\rightarrow(0,\infty]
, which is finite a.s., such that

\displaystyle\{\phi_{t}^{-1}(x):\hskip 2.84544ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84544ptx\in B(0,R)\}\subset B(0,R^{*}).

Let
\omega
belong to the set of probability one such that
R^{*}(\omega)<\infty
and set
R^{\prime}(\omega):=\max(R,R^{*}(\omega))
. Then we have for
\mathbb{N}\ni m>\lfloor 2R^{\prime}(\omega)\rfloor+1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)\zeta(\phi_{t}^{-1}(x)/m)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x)=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}_{t}(x),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Therefore
\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t}(x)=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}\mathbf{K}_{t}(x).

We conclude that for
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\omega,
if
m\equiv m(\omega)>\lfloor 2R^{\prime}(\omega)\rfloor+1,
we always have
\mathbf{K}^{m}=\mathbf{K}
and hence the terms
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,
and
\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s
in (
52
) become
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s
respectively, which implies a.s. convergence. To finish the argument, we claim that

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}\rightarrow\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}

in probability,
t\in[0,T]
. For this, it suffices to establish

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right]\rightarrow 0,

(53)

where we have used Itô’s isometry and employed the fact that square mean convergence implies convergence in probability. Claim (
53
) can be verified by applying the dominated convergence theorem in the space
\Omega\times[0,T]
to the sequence of functions
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
, where the hypotheses are satisfied since (i) clearly, a.s.
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle^{2}_{L^{2}}\rightarrow 0,
(ii) by using Hölder’s inequality, we have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}^{m}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L_{x}^{2}}^{2},

where we have taken into account that
\lvert\mathbf{K}^{m}_{t}(x)\rvert\leq\lvert\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)\rvert
and have used that
\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
. Moreover, (iii)
\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L_{x}^{2}}^{2}\in L^{1}(\Omega\times[0,T])
by usual strategies as in the bound for
\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{K}(t,x)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]
in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
20
.
∎

6
Uniqueness of stochastic solutions

6.1
Uniqueness with weak differentiability of the drift

This subsection is devoted to establishing our general uniqueness result (Theorem
4
). Let us assume we are under the hypotheses in Theorem
4
. For convenience, we employ the notation
\mathbf{K}(t,x)
for the solution of (
1
). For simplicity, we treat the case
N=1
. Due to the linearity of the equation, it is sufficient to show that if
\mathbf{K}(0,\cdot)=0
, then
\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot)=0
a.s., for every
t\in[0,T]
.

First, fix
\epsilon>0
and consider the
smooth-in-space

k
-form-valued process
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x):=(\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot))(x)
, which must satisfy
8
8

8

Stochastic Fubini’s theorem can be applied to equation (
54
) to switch spatial and temporal integration by a similar (but simpler) argument to the one we employ to justify its use in the proof of Theorem
4
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x)=-\int^{t}_{0}(\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}(\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&\hskip 90.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(\rho^{\epsilon}*(\mathcal{L}_{\xi})^{2}\mathbf{K})(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s=0,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{split}

(54)

where
\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K},

\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K},

(\mathcal{L}_{\xi})^{2}\mathbf{K}
are understood as Lie derivatives
in the sense of distributions
, owing to the low spatial regularity of
\mathbf{K}
(see Definition
4
). We note that (
12
) is well-defined since by Hölder’s inequality and Hypothesis
2

(i)
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{\omega,t,x}}\lVert\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{t,x}}.

Since all the terms in (
54
) are now smooth in space, we can apply the KIW formula for
k
-forms (Theorem
1
(
9
)) to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x)=-\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int^{t}_{0}\phi^{*}_{s}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}[W_{\cdot},W_{\cdot}]_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\phi^{*}_{s}\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(55)

for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Note that the hypotheses of Theorem
1
are satisfied since:

•

By the properties of mollifiers,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}
is continuous in time (indeed, see equation (
54
)) and smooth in space, whereas
(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}
and
((\mathcal{L}_{\xi})^{2}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}
are just smooth in space.

•

By taking into account the property
D_{y}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)=-D_{x}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)
and applying the definition of mollification of distribution-valued
k
-forms (see Definition
12
), one can check that
(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(t,x)
is the smooth time-dependent
k
-form

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}(x-y)\mathbf{K}(t,y)b^{l}(t,y)-\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\mathbf{K}(t,y)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(t,y)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\left(\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(t,y)K_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,y)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 130.0pt\left.+\cdots+\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(t,y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(t,y)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y.

Let
\rho^{\epsilon}
be supported on
B(0,R)
. Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}(x-y)\mathbf{K}(t,y)b^{l}(t,y)-\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\mathbf{K}(t,y)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(t,y)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\left(\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(t,y)K_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,y)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\left.\left.+\cdots+\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(t,y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},i_{k}}(t,y)\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}(x-y)+\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\right)\lVert\mathbf{K}(\cdot,y)\rVert_{L^{p}_{t}}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\left(\lVert b(\cdot,y)\rVert_{L^{q}_{t}}+\lVert Db(\cdot,y)\rVert_{L^{q}_{t}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\lvert\left\lvert\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}\right\rvert\right\rvert_{L^{\infty}}+\lVert\rho^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\right)\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{t,x}}\left(\lVert b\rVert_{L^{q}_{B(x,R)}}+\lVert Db\rVert_{L^{q}_{B(x,R)}}\right)<\infty,

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
where we have taken into account Hypotheses
1

(i)

\&

3
.

•

By a similar argument, one can also check that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left\lvert\big((\mathcal{L}_{\xi})^{2}\mathbf{K}\big)^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right\rvert+\big|(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(t,x)\big\rvert^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(56)

Therefore, (
55
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x)&=\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{split}

(57)

where

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}:=\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}-(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon},

(58)

denotes the commutator between the linear operators
\mathcal{L}_{b}
and
\rho^{\epsilon}*(\cdot),
generalising the DiPerna-Lions commutator appearing in
[
11
,
31
]
, and

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}-2\mathcal{L}_{\xi}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}+(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{\xi}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}

(59)

is the Stratonovich-to-Itô correction of the commutator (a double commutator). A novelty of this paper consists in showing that the commutators (
58
) & (
59
) satisfy a DiPerna-Lions type estimate, and converge weakly to zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. This is a-priori far from being obvious since they contain terms which at first glance seem too singular. We remark that in the special case
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}=\xi\cdot\nabla
, a similar estimate for the double commutator was obtained in
[
35
]
, Proposition 3.4.

We will show that (
57
) vanishes weakly as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, thus establishing uniqueness of solutions. To treat commutators (
58
) & (
59
), we establish generalisations to
k
-forms of the classical DiPerna-Lions commutator estimates (see
[
11
,
31
,
18
]
). Our first lemma will be applied to treat the first commutator (
58
).

Lemma 24
.

For
p\geq 2
, given a
k
-form
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, a vector field
b\in W^{1,q}_{loc}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),
and a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
B(0,R)
for some
R>0
, for
0<\epsilon<1
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{K},b,\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\leq C\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R+1}},

(60)

Moreover,
\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
converges to zero in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

We split the proof in two steps, where we first prove the commutator estimate (
60
) and then prove the
L^{1}
-convergence of
\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
to zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Step 1.
By the definition of the commutator, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left((\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x.\end{split}

(61)

The first integral on the left-hand side becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(x),\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K^{\epsilon}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x,\end{split}

(62)

where the coordinate expression for the adjoint Lie derivative reads (see (
133
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)&=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}}(b^{l}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x))+\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\\
&\quad+\cdots+\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x).\end{split}

(63)

Hence by definition of tensor mollification (Definition
11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:commutator-first-term}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}}(b^{l}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\left.+\cdots+\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Bigg(\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)b^{l}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0pt+\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\left.+\cdots+\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)\right)\Bigg)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x.

For the second integral in (
61
), we take into account the property
D_{y}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)=-D_{x}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)
and apply the definition of mollification of distribution-valued
k
-forms (see Definition
12
), obtaining

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left((\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K})^{\epsilon}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}(y),\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}(\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathbf{\Theta})(y)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Bigg(\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial x_{l}}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)b^{l}(y)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt-\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Bigg)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Bigg(\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\cdots+\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)\Bigg)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x.

Combining the above expressions, we derive

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\epsilon^{-n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{l}}\rho\left(\frac{y-x}{\epsilon}\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{b^{l}(y)-b^{l}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\Bigg(\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)-\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)

(64)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt-\cdots-\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)-\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Bigg)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt=:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I_{1}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I_{2}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x.

(65)

Applying Young’s convolution inequality (take into account
0<\epsilon<1
and that
\mathbf{\Theta}
has support in
B(0,R)
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I_{2}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R+1}},

(66)

where we have also employed the fact that
\lVert\rho^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{1}}=1
, independently of
\epsilon
.
To obtain an analogous estimate for the first integral in (
65
), we follow the proof of Lemma 2.3 in
[
31
]
.
First, we note that by Taylor’s theorem, we have

\displaystyle b^{l}(y)-b^{l}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}(y-x)\cdot Db^{l}\left(x+\lambda(y-x)\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda.

Hence

\displaystyle I^{\epsilon}_{1}(x)=\epsilon^{-n}\int_{B(x,\epsilon)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{l}}\rho\left(\frac{y-x}{\epsilon}\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{b^{l}(y)-b^{l}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle=\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{l}}\rho\left(w\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\left(\int_{0}^{1}w\cdot Db^{l}\left(x+\lambda\epsilon w\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w,

and therefore

\displaystyle\lvert I^{\epsilon}_{1}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert D\rho\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\int_{B(0,1)}\int_{0}^{1}\lvert Db\left(x+\lambda\epsilon w\right)\rvert^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w\right)^{1/q}.

Finally, by observing that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\int_{0}^{1}\lvert Db\left(x+\lambda\epsilon w\right)\rvert^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w=(\lvert Db\rvert^{q}*\chi^{\epsilon})(x),

where
\chi^{\epsilon}(z):=\int^{1}_{0}\frac{1}{(\epsilon\lambda)^{n}}\mathcal{X}_{B(0,\epsilon\lambda)}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
satisfies
\lVert\chi^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{1}_{\epsilon}}=\text{meas}(B(0,1))
, and applying Hölder’s inequality in space, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}\lvert I^{\epsilon}_{1}(x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\lesssim\lVert D\rho\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\int_{B(0,R)}(\lvert Db\rvert^{q}*\chi^{\epsilon})(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert D\rho\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert Db\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}\lVert\chi^{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^{1}_{\epsilon}}^{1/q}\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R+1}},

(67)

where we have made use of Young’s convolution inequality in the second line. Note that none of the constants that appear in the inequalities thus far depend on
\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{K},b,\epsilon.
This proves our bound (
60
).

Step 2.
We now show the
L^{1}
-convergence of
\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
. Taking into account the properties of mollifiers and applying the dominated convergence theorem (use bound (
66
)), we obtain

\displaystyle I^{\epsilon}_{2}\rightarrow\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b)}

in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. Next, for any
R>0
, taking into account the density of
C^{\infty}_{0}
functions in the space
W^{1,q}_{R+1}
, we consider a sequence of
C^{\infty}_{0}
-vector fields
\{b_{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}
such that
b_{\delta}\rightarrow b
in
W^{1,q}_{R+1}
. Define

\displaystyle I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]

\displaystyle:=\epsilon^{-n}\int_{B(x,\epsilon)}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{l}}\rho\left(\frac{y-x}{\epsilon}\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(y)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle=\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial w_{l}}\left(w\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w.

We claim that the convergence

\displaystyle I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]\rightarrow\left(\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\int_{B(0,1)}w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{j}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial w_{j}}(w)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w=-\left(\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\text{div}{(b_{\delta}(x))},

(68)

holds in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, where the last equality was obtained by integrating by parts, and using
\frac{\partial w_{i}}{\partial w_{j}}=\delta_{ij}
together with
\int_{B(0,1)}\rho(w)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w=1
.
To show this convergence, we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]-\left(\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\int_{B(0,1)}w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{j}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial w_{l}}(w)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w\right\rvert\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{\Theta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\|D\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\int_{B(0,1)}\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 190.0pt-w_{i}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w,

where we used Fubini’s theorem to switch the order of integration. Clearly, if we can show that the integral above vanishes in the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, then our claim follows. To this end, we apply the triangle inequality and Hölder’s inequality to get the following bound on the inner integral

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)-w_{i}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B(0,R)}\big\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\big\rvert\left\lvert w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{B(0,R)}\big\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\big\rvert\left\lvert\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)-w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\big\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\big\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert^{q}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\big\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\big\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\times\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert\left(\frac{b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)-w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\right\rvert^{q}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle=:J_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)+J_{2}^{\epsilon}(w).

We first treat the term
J_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)
. Noting that
C_{0}^{\infty}
functions are dense in
L^{p}_{R}
, consider a sequence of
k
-forms
\{\mathbf{K}^{\eta}\}_{\eta>0}
of class
C_{0}^{\infty}
such that
\mathbf{K}^{\eta}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}
in
L^{p}_{R}
as
\eta\rightarrow 0
. Applying Minkowski’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(69)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}^{\eta}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}^{\eta}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}^{\eta}(x)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(70)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}^{\eta}(x)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\,\cdot+\epsilon w)-\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{R}}+\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\cdot)-\mathbf{K}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{R}}.

(71)

Now fix
\eta>0
and
w\in B(0,1)
. By the continuity of
\mathbf{K}^{\eta}
, we have the pointwise convergence
\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(x+\epsilon w)\rightarrow\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(x),
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(x+\epsilon w)\leq\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}\|_{L_{R+1}^{\infty}}<\infty
(take into account
0<\epsilon<1
), we have
\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\,\cdot+\epsilon w)-\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{R}}\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
by the dominated convergence theorem.
Thus

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(\int_{B(0,R)}\left\lvert K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon w)-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right\rvert^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:K-eta-inequality}}}{{\lesssim}}\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\,\cdot+\epsilon w)-\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{R}}+\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\|\mathbf{K}^{\eta}(\cdot)-\mathbf{K}(\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{R}}=0,

(72)

which yields the convergence
J_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

The convergence
J_{2}^{\epsilon}(w)\rightarrow 0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
also follows from the dominated convergence theorem. Indeed, by differentiability of
b_{\delta}
, we have that
(b^{l}_{\delta}(x+\epsilon w)-b^{l}_{\delta}(x))/\epsilon
converges pointwise to
w_{i}\frac{\partial b^{l}_{\delta}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)
(sum over repeated indices assumed) and its absolute value is furthermore bounded by
\|Db_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}
due to the mean value theorem, where we took into account
w\in B(0,1)
.

Furthermore, we have the bound
J_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)+J_{2}^{\epsilon}(w)\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{{R}+1}}\lVert Db_{\delta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{{R}+1}}
, which can be shown by following similar arguments as when showing the bound (
67
). This allow us to conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\left(J_{1}^{\epsilon}(w)+J_{2}^{\epsilon}(w)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}w\rightarrow 0

by the bounded convergence theorem, thus proving our claim.

Finally, by the linearity of
I_{1}^{\epsilon}
(in the
b
-argument) and using estimate (
67
), we have

\displaystyle\lVert I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b]+\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b)}\rVert_{L^{1}_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b]-I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]\rVert_{L^{1}_{R}}+\lVert I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]+\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b_{\delta})}\rVert_{L^{1}_{R}}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\lVert\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b)}-\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b_{\delta})}\rVert_{L^{1}_{R}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\left(\lVert b-b_{\delta}\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R+1}}+\lVert\text{div}{(b)}-\text{div}{(b_{\delta})}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\lVert I_{1}^{\epsilon}[b_{\delta}]+\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b_{\delta})}\rVert_{L^{1}_{R}}\rightarrow 0,

as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, followed by
\delta\rightarrow 0
(limits taken in this precise order, following arguments similar to (
72
). Note that the constant in the last inequality is independent of
\delta
). Therefore we have shown that
I_{1}^{\epsilon}+I_{2}^{\epsilon}\rightarrow-\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b)}+\left(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)\text{div}{(b)}=0
in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
∎

The following lemma will be applied to treat the double commutator (
58
).

Lemma 25
.

For
p\geq 2
, given a
k
-form
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, a vector field
\xi\in C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),
and a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
