supported on
B(0,R)
for some
R>0
. Then for
0<\epsilon<1
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{K},\xi,\epsilon
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 50.0pt\leq C\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D^{2}\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}+\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}\right).\end{split}

(73)

Moreover,
\left([\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
converges to zero in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

By a straightforward but lengthy calculation using the explicit formula (
63
) and the property that
D_{x}\rho(x-y)=-D_{y}\rho(x-y)
, we find

\displaystyle\left([\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\partial^{2}\rho}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{l}}(y-x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)(\xi^{l}(y)-\xi^{l}(x))(\xi^{m}(y)-\xi^{m}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\partial\rho^{\epsilon}}{\partial y_{l}}(y-x)\Bigg[K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Bigg(2\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)(\xi^{l}(y)-\xi^{l}(x))

\displaystyle\quad+\xi^{m}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{m}}(x)\right)+\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{m}}(x)(\xi^{m}(y)-\xi^{m}(x))\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-2\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\big(\xi^{l}(y)-\xi^{l}(x)\big)\Bigg(K_{m,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\cdots+K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},m}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\right)\Bigg)\Bigg]\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(y-x)\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Bigg[K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{m}}\left(\xi^{m}(y)\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-K_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Bigg[\left(\xi^{m}(y)\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{k}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{i_{1}}}(y)-\xi^{m}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{k}}{\partial x_{m}\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\right)+\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{m}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad-\cdots-K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(y)\Bigg[\left(\xi^{m}(y)\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{k}}{\partial y_{m}\partial y_{i_{k}}}(y)-\xi^{m}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{k}}{\partial x_{m}\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\right)+\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{m}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{m}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad+2K_{l,m,i_{3},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{1}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\right)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{2}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{2}}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle+\cdots+2K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-2},l,m}(y)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial y_{i_{k-1}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{k-1}}}(x)\right)\left(\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial y_{i_{k}}}(y)-\frac{\partial\xi^{m}}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x)\right)\Bigg]\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle=:I^{\epsilon}_{1}(x)+I^{\epsilon}_{2}(x)+I^{\epsilon}_{3}(x).

By Young’s convolution inequality, one can verify that for the third integral, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\lvert I^{\epsilon}_{3}(x)\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D^{2}\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}+\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}\right).

For the second integral, we apply a similar strategy as carried out in the proof of Lemma
24
to show that

\displaystyle\left\lvert I^{\epsilon}_{2}(x)\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D^{2}\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}+\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}\right).

For the first integral, using

\displaystyle\xi^{l}(y)-\xi^{l}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}(y-x)\cdot D\xi^{l}\left(x+\lambda(y-x)\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda,

we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I^{\epsilon}_{1}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\rho}{\partial y^{l}\partial y^{m}}\left(\frac{y-x}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt\times\left(\frac{\xi^{l}(y)-\xi^{l}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\left(\frac{\xi^{m}(y)-\xi^{m}(x)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle=\int_{B(0,R)}\int_{B(0,1)}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x+\epsilon\lambda w)D^{2}_{lm}\rho(w)\left(\int_{0}^{1}w\cdot D\xi^{l}\left(x+\epsilon\lambda w\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}w\cdot D\xi^{m}\left(x+\epsilon\lambda w\right)\mathrm{d}\lambda\right)\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}w\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I_{1}^{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert\lvert D\xi\rvert^{2}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}},

where
\chi^{\epsilon}(z):=\int^{1}_{0}\frac{1}{(\epsilon\lambda)^{n}}\mathcal{X}_{B(0,\epsilon\lambda)}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda
as in Lemma
24
and the constant in
\lesssim
is independent of
\epsilon
. Combining all of the above, we obtain (
73
). None of the constants appearing in this proof depend on
\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{K},\xi,\epsilon.
Finally,
L^{1}
-convergence is not difficult to prove.
∎

As a corollary of Lemmas
24

\&

25
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 26
.

Let
\phi
be a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism,
\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\geq 2
,
b\in W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\xi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
a test
k
-form with support on
B(0,R).
Then for any
0<\epsilon<1,
choosing
R^{*}<\infty
as in Lemma
17
(in this case,
R^{*}
is deterministic), we have

\displaystyle\bullet\quad\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\,\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\lesssim\|\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\|\mathbf{K}\|_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\|b\|_{W^{1,q}_{R^{*}+1}},

(74)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bullet\quad\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(x),\,\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\quad\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\|D^{2}\xi\|_{L^{q}_{R^{*}+1}}+\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D\xi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}+1}}\right),\end{split}

(75)

where the constants above are independent of
\mathbf{\Theta},\mathbf{K},b,\xi,\epsilon
. Moreover, both
\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
and
\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
converge to zero in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

Using Lemma
16
(note
\phi^{*}=(\phi^{-1})_{*}
), we get

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert,

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(x)=\lvert J\phi^{-1}(x)\rvert(\phi_{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(x))^{\flat}
. Applying Lemma
24
, we obtain the estimate (
74
). Likewise, the estimate (
75
) can be obtained by applying Lemma
25
. The
L^{1}
-convergence claimed at the end of this lemma also follows easily from Lemmas
24

\&

25
and estimates (
74
)
\&
(
75
).
∎

We are now ready to prove our uniqueness result.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

We showed earlier by applying the KIW formula that the pull-back of the mollified
k
-form-valued process
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
satisfies (
57
). We observe that in order to prove the uniqueness of solutions to equation (
1
), it suffices to show that
\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot)
is zero a.s. in the weak sense for all
t\in[0,T]
if
\mathbf{K}(0,\cdot)=0,
since
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\phi_{t}
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Fixing a test function
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
with support in
B(0,R),
taking into account (
1
), and using stochastic Fubini’s theorem to switch the spatial and time integrals, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(76)

We note that stochastic Fubini’s theorem can be applied in (
76
), since the uniform bounds (
74
)
\&
(
75
) together with Lemma
17
yield

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert b_{s}\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R^{*}+1}},

(77)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\quad\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D^{2}\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}+\lVert D\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\right),\end{split}

(78)

uniformly in
\epsilon
, where
R^{*}<\infty
a.s. is chosen such that

\displaystyle\left\{\phi_{t}^{-1}(x),\hskip 2.84526ptt\in[0,T],\hskip 2.84526ptx\in B(0,R)\right\}\subset B(0,R^{*}),

where a.s., we know that
\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
. Moreover, by the definition of solution we have
\lVert\mathbf{K}_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\in L^{p}([0,T])
a.s., and by Hypothesis
3
, we have
\lVert b_{\cdot}\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R^{*}+1}}\in L^{q}([0,T])
, so the RHS of
(
77
)
is a.s. in
L^{1}([0,T])
by a standard application of Hölder’s inequality. We now check term-by-term convergence as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
in (
76
) for fixed
t\in[0,T]
.

•

The term before the equality. We claim that a.s.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,y),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}(y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y

\displaystyle\rightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\mathbf{K}(t,y),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}(y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{K}(t,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x.

For this, we note that a.s.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,y)-\mathbf{K}(t,y),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}(y))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\leq\lVert\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)-\mathbf{K}(t,\cdot)\rVert_{L^{1}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{t}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\rightarrow 0,

pointwise in
t,
since
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
converges to
\mathbf{K}
in
L^{1}_{loc}.

•

The first term after the equality.
By Corollary
26
(
74
), we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert b_{s}\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R^{*}+1}},

where a.s.
\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
,
\lVert\mathbf{K}_{\cdot}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\in L^{p}([0,T]),
and
\lVert b_{\cdot}\rVert_{W^{1,q}_{R^{*}+1}}\in L^{q}([0,T])
. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem we obtain a.s.

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}s=0.

•

The double commutator term in the last line. By (
75
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}\lVert\mathbf{K}_{s}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D^{2}\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}+\lVert D\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D\xi_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\right).

It is easy to show that the bound is in
L^{1}([0,T])
, and hence by the dominated convergence theorem we have a.s.

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}s=0.

•

The martingale term in the last line. Similarly, by the stochastic dominated convergence theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left\lvert\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi_{s}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right\rvert=0

in probability.

∎

6.2
Uniqueness without weak differentiability of the drift

This subsection is devoted to proving Theorem
5
.

Lemma 27
.

Let
p\geq 2,

\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
b\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
B(0,R)
for some
R>0
.

1.

If
k=0
and
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\qquad\leq 2\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\left(\lVert\text{div}{(b)}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R+1}}\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L_{R}^{\infty}}+\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R}}\right).\end{split}

(79)

2.

If
k=n
then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\leq 2\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R}}.

(80)

Moreover, in all cases
\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
converges to zero in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

By following our proof of Lemma
24
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}\left(x-y\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(b^{l}(x)-b^{l}(y)\right)\frac{\partial\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}\left(x-y\right)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\left(\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\Bigg(\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\\
&\qquad-\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)-\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\\
&\qquad-\cdots-\left(\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial y_{l}}(y)-\frac{\partial b^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(y)\Bigg)\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x.\end{split}

(81)

Inequality (
79
) follows by observing that all the terms in (
81
) except for the one in the first line cancel, and applying Young’s convolution inequality. (
80
) can be established by similar inspection mechanisms. To establish the
L^{1}
-convergence, we follow similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma
24
.
∎

Corollary 28
.

Let
\phi
be a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism with inverse
\psi,

\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
b\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported on
B(0,R).

1.

If
k=0
and
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
for any
r>2,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert\text{div}{(b)}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert J\psi\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{\infty}}+\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert D\psi\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}+\lVert DJ\psi\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}}}\right)\right),\end{split}

(82)

2.

if
k=n
and
\text{div}{(b)}\in L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
for any
r>2q,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\\
&\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert D\psi\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}}}+\lVert DJ\psi\rVert_{L^{2q}_{R^{*}}}\right)\lVert DJ\phi\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{2q}}\lVert D\psi\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{\infty}},\end{split}

(83)

where, as usually,
R^{*}
is chosen such that
\{\phi^{-1}(x),\hskip 2.84526ptx\in B(0,R)\}\subset B(0,R^{*})
and the constants in
\lesssim
do not depend on
\phi,\mathbf{K},b,\epsilon.
Moreover, in all cases
\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right)
converges to zero in
L^{1}
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

We first establish (
82
). Using Lemma
16
and (
79
) in Lemma
27
, we get

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R^{*}+1}}\left(\lVert\text{div}{(b)}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{\infty}}+\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R^{*}+1}}\lVert D\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R^{*}}}\right).

Since
k=0,
we have
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(y)=\lvert J\psi(y)\rvert\mathbf{\Theta}(\psi(y))
, which is supported on
B(0,R^{*})
by Lemma
17
. Finally, observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lVert D\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{q}}&\leq\lVert[D\psi]^{T}(D\mathbf{\Theta}\circ\psi)J\psi\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{q}}+\lVert(\mathbf{\Theta}\circ\psi)DJ\psi\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{q}}\\
&\leq\lVert D\psi\rVert^{2}_{{L_{{R^{*}}}^{\infty}}}\lVert D\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{q}}+\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{\infty}}\lVert DJ\psi\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{q}},\end{split}

(84)

where we have taken into account Theorem
13
.
To show (
83
), applying Lemma
16
and (
80
) in Lemma
27
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\mathbf{\Theta}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K}(x),\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{K}\rVert_{L^{p}_{R+1}}\lVert b\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{R+1}}\lVert D\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L^{q}_{R}}.

Since
k=n,
we have
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}(y)=\lvert J\psi(y)\rvert(\phi_{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp}(y))^{\flat},
with
(\phi_{*}\mathbf{\Theta}^{\sharp})(y)=\mathbf{\Theta}(\psi(y))\lvert(J\phi)(\psi(y))\rvert.
By the same argument in (
84
) and using Hölder’s inequality, we have the control

\displaystyle\lVert D\widetilde{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{q}}\leq\left(||D\psi||^{2}_{{L_{{R^{*}}}^{\infty}}}\lVert D\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{2q}}+\lVert\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{\infty}}\lVert DJ\psi\rVert_{L_{{R^{*}}}^{2q}}\right)\lVert DJ\phi\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{2q}}\lVert D\psi\rVert_{L_{R^{*}}^{\infty}},

where we have taken into account Theorem
13
. Finally, it is not difficult to show
L^{1}
-convergence of
\left(\phi^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{b},\rho^{\epsilon}*]\mathbf{K},\mathbf{\Theta}\right).

∎

Finally, by following the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
4
in Section
6
, we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to conclude Theorem
5
.

7
Ill-posedness of the deterministic equation

This section is devoted to establishing Theorem
7
. Without noise, (
1
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)=0,\\
&\mathbf{K}_{0}(\cdot)=\mathbf{K}_{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{split}

(85)

We start by noting that the natural equivalent of Definition
6
in the deterministic case is the following.

Definition 7

(Deterministic weak
L^{p}
-solution)
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
b\in W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
1/q+1/p=1.
We say that a
k
-form-valued time-dependent function
\mathbf{K}
satisfies equation (
85
) weakly in the
L^{p}
-sense if

•

\mathbf{K}\in L^{p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}).

•

For any test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the function
\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is continuous (a.e.) and (
15
) with
\xi_{i}=0,

i=1,\ldots,N
is satisfied.

7.1
Nonuniqueness of deterministic solutions

During the following arguments, we choose
T>1.
If
T\leq 1,
the solutions to the characteristic ODE (
88
) below are exactly the same but restricted. Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
R>0
. Consider the autonomous vector field

\displaystyle b_{\alpha,R}(x)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\frac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}(\min\{\lvert x\rvert,R\})^{\alpha},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(86)

where we assumed the convention
\frac{0}{0}=0.
A straightforward computation shows that
b_{\alpha,R}\in C^{\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover,
b_{\alpha,R}\in W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
q\in[1,2]
when
n\geq 2
and when
n=1,\alpha\in((q-1)/q,1)
, since its partial derivatives behave like
1/\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}
near zero. The associated characteristic ODE reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\phi_{t}(x)=b_{\alpha,R}(\phi_{t}(x)),\\
&\phi_{0}(x)=x,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{split}

(87)

First of all, notice that by means of expressing the equation in terms of the re-scaled variables

y=x/R
and
\tau=t/R^{1-\alpha}
, it suffices to consider the case
R=1
without loss of generality. Thus, to simplify notation, from now on we set
R=1
and denote
b_{\alpha}:=b_{\alpha,1}
. Hence (
87
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\phi_{t}(x)=b_{\alpha}(\phi_{t}(x)),\\
&\phi_{0}(x)=x.\end{split}

(88)

For
x\neq 0
, it can be verified that the unique solution to (
88
) has the explicit form

\phi_{t}(x)=\begin{cases}\phi^{0}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 0\leq t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\leq 1,\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\\
\phi^{1}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}(t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}-1)+1\right),\quad t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}>1,\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\\
\phi^{2}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(\frac{t}{1-\alpha}+\lvert x\rvert\right),\quad\lvert x\rvert>1,\end{cases}

(89)

for
t\in[0,T].
Moreover, one can check that for every
t\in[0,T],

\phi_{t}
maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
continuously and bijectively onto the set
\mathcal{A}_{t},
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{B}(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}),\quad 0\leq t\leq 1,\\
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{B}(0,\frac{t}{1-\alpha}-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}),\quad t>1.\end{cases}

We have assumed the convention
\overline{B}(0,0):=\{0\}.
The solution
\phi
in (
89
) is of class
C^{\infty}
in both time and space everywhere except on the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{1}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}:\lvert x\rvert=(1-t)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{2}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert x\rvert=1\right\}.

Nevertheless, we know that
\phi
is at least continuous on
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}:=\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{2}
. Let us first show that
\phi_{t}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},\mathcal{A}_{t}),
for all
t\in[0,T]
. To that purpose, by computing the first order (spatial) partial derivatives of
\phi_{t}
, we find that

\frac{\partial\phi^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)=\begin{cases}\left(t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}\dfrac{x_{i}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2-\alpha}}+(t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha})^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\dfrac{\delta_{ij}\lvert x\rvert-\frac{x_{i}}{\lvert x\rvert}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}},\quad 0\leq t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\leq 1,\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\\
\dfrac{\delta_{ij}\lvert x\rvert-\frac{x_{i}}{\lvert x\rvert}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}\left(\dfrac{t-1+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}+1\right)+\dfrac{x_{i}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2-\alpha}},\quad t+\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}>1,\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\\
\dfrac{\delta_{ij}\lvert x\rvert-\frac{x_{i}}{\lvert x\rvert}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}\left(\dfrac{t}{1-\alpha}+\lvert x\rvert\right)+\dfrac{x_{i}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}},\quad\lvert x\rvert>1,\end{cases}

for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. It is easy to check that all partial derivatives coincide on
\mathcal{S}^{\phi}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\phi^{0})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial(\phi^{1})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x),\quad(t,x)\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{1},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\phi^{1})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial(\phi^{2})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x),\quad(t,x)\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\phi^{0})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial(\phi^{2})^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x),\quad(t,x)\in\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{1}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\phi}_{2}.

Similarly, it can be verified that for fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\},

\phi_{\cdot}(x)\in C^{1}([0,T])
. Therefore, we have shown that
\phi\in C^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}).

Furthermore, by means of the Inverse Function Theorem or by direct computation, one can also check that the inverse of
\phi_{t}
, denoted by
\psi_{t}:=\phi^{-1}_{t}
, is also of class
\psi\in C^{1}\left(\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\{t\}\times\mathcal{A}_{t}\right);\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\right).
Moreover, the explicit form of
\psi
is

\psi_{t}(x)=\begin{cases}\psi^{0}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}-t\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\quad 0\leq t<1,\\
\psi^{1}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left((1-\alpha)\lvert x\rvert+\alpha-t\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 1<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1+\frac{t}{1-\alpha},\quad 0<t<1,\\
\psi^{2}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left((1-\alpha)\lvert x\rvert+\alpha-t\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad\frac{t-\alpha}{1-\alpha}<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1+\frac{t}{1-\alpha},\quad t\geq 1,\\
\psi^{3}_{t}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(\lvert x\rvert-\frac{t}{1-\alpha}\right),\quad\lvert x\rvert>1+\frac{t}{1-\alpha},\quad t>0.\end{cases}

(90)

Consequently,
\psi
is of class
C^{\infty}
in time and space everywhere except on the singular sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\psi}_{1}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert x\rvert=1\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\psi}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert x\rvert=1+\frac{t}{1-\alpha}\right\}.

For
x=0
, the ODE (
88
) has infinitely many solutions of the form

\displaystyle\varphi^{v}_{t}=\begin{cases}vt^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 0\leq t\leq 1,\\
v\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}(t-1)+1\right),\quad t>1,\end{cases}

where
v
is any unitary vector, i.e.
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\lvert v\rvert=1
. Moreover, fixing
t_{0}
such that
0<t_{0}\leq t\leq T
, we can construct via translation a shifted family of solutions

\displaystyle\varphi^{v}_{t-{t_{0}}}=\begin{cases}v\left(t-t_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 0\leq t-t_{0}\leq 1,\\
v\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}(t-t_{0}-1)+1\right),\quad t-t_{0}>1.\end{cases}

By expressing uniquely any element
x\in B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}
in the form
x=t_{0}v,
where
t_{0}\in(0,t]
and
\lvert v\rvert=1
, we can define a diffeomorphism
\Phi_{t}:B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}\rightarrow B(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})\backslash\{0\}
via the relation
t_{0}v\mapsto\varphi^{v}_{t-{t_{0}}}.
More explicitly, we have

\Phi_{t}(x)=\begin{cases}\Phi_{t}^{0}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(t-\lvert x\rvert\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 0\leq t-\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert<t,\\
\Phi_{t}^{1}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}(t-\lvert x\rvert-1)+1\right),\quad t-\lvert x\rvert>1,\quad\lvert x\rvert>0,\quad t>1,\end{cases}

for
t\in(0,T]
and
x\in B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}
. One can check that
\Phi:\bigcup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(\{t\}\times B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
is of class
C^{\infty}
in time and space everywhere except on the singular set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\Phi}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[1,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert x\rvert=t-1\right\}.

The inverse of
\Phi_{t}
, denoted by
\Psi_{t}:=\Phi_{t}^{-1}
, can be computed explicitly as

\Psi_{t}(x)=\begin{cases}\Psi_{t}^{0}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(t-\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\right),\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad 0<t\leq 1,\\
\Psi_{t}^{1}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(t-\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\right),\quad 0<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1,\quad t>1,\\
\Psi_{t}^{2}(x)=\dfrac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left((\alpha-1)\lvert x\rvert-\alpha+t\right),\quad 1<\lvert x\rvert\leq 1+\frac{t-1}{1-\alpha},\quad t>1,\end{cases}

(91)

with singular set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\Psi}:=\left\{(t,x)\in[1,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lvert x\rvert=1\right\}.

One can check that
\Phi
is of class
C^{1}\left(\bigcup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(\{t\}\times B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}\right);\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\right)
and its inverse
\Psi
is of class
C^{1}\left(\bigcup_{t\in(0,T]}\left(\{t\}\times B(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})\backslash\{0\}\right);\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\right).

Now, we will construct explicit locally in space strong solutions to the transport equation (
1
) with vector field
b_{\alpha}
. In the next step, we will employ this to construct
L^{p}
-solutions to (
1
) (i.e. in the sense of Definition
7
). Since the aim of the first part of this section is showing that equation (
1
) is ill-posed in
L^{p}
spaces and not developing a deterministic theory, we will assume for simplicity that the support of the initial datum
\mathbf{K}_{0}
is contained in
B(0,1/2),
and we will choose
T\leq 1-1/2^{1-\alpha}
. The general case works similarly, although the computations become more involved. Before stating an important result, let us first introduce new notation that will simplify the exposition of the arguments hereafter.

We define the time dependent open sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}:=B(0,1)\backslash\overline{B}(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}),

where
t\in[0,T]
. Observe that
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}
is the open set where
\psi_{t}^{0}
is defined (see (
90
)). Similarly, the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}:=\begin{cases}B(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})\backslash\{0\},\quad 0<t\leq T,\\
\emptyset,\quad t=0,\end{cases}

is exactly the open set where

\Psi^{0}_{t}
is defined.

With this notation at hand, we present the following result:

Proposition 29
.

Let
\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Gamma}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
be supported on
B(0,1/2)
and
0<T\leq 1-1/2^{1-\alpha}
. Then the
k
-form-valued function defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(x)=\begin{cases}(\phi^{0}_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}(x),&x\in\mathcal{A}^{0}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
(\Phi^{0}_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}(x),&x\in\mathcal{B}^{0}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
0,&\textit{elsewhere},\end{cases}

(92)

solves the Lie transport equation (
1
) strongly on the open sets
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{t},

\mathcal{B}^{0}_{t},

t\in[0,T].

Proof.

Before starting the computation, we first note that by taking into account the regularity shown for
\psi
and
\Psi
in the previous steps, the
k
-form-valued function
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
defined in (
92
) is of class
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\in C^{\infty}\left(\bigcup_{t\in(0,T)}\left(\{t\}\times(\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}\cup\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0})\right);\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).
We have to verify that the following identity holds

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(x)+\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}(x)=0,

(93)

for
x\in\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}\cup\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}
and
t\in(0,T).
To that purpose, we divide the arguments into two parts.

Step 1:
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
verifies the equation strongly on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
.

Using the fact that
\phi^{0}_{t}
(
89
) is a smooth local flow, we can define the Lie derivative in terms of the local flow as
\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\right\rvert_{s=0}(\phi_{s}^{0})^{*}\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}
on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}
. A standard differential geometry manipulation shows that indeed
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}
solves (
93
) strongly on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
.

However, showing that
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
solves (
93
) strongly on
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T),
requires a different argument since
\Phi^{0}_{t}
is not a local flow (in particular, the semigroup property fails). To bypass this issue, we provide a direct proof in the next step.

Step 2:
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
verifies the equation strongly on
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
.

Before starting with the proof, we derive a useful evolution equation for
\Psi^{0}_{t}
. To simplify the notation, we will just write
\Phi_{t}
instead of
\Phi^{0}_{t}
and
\Psi_{t}
instead of
\Psi^{0}_{t}
(this is actually correct by (
91
)). Recalling that
\Psi_{t}(\Phi_{t}(y))=y,
for any
y\in B(0,t)\backslash\{0\}
we infer that

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi_{t}(\Phi_{t}(y))&=\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}}{\partial t}(\Phi_{t}(y))+\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(\Phi_{t}(y))\frac{\partial\Phi_{t}^{i}}{\partial t}(y)\\
&=\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}}{\partial t}(\Phi_{t}(y))+\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(\Phi_{t}(y))b_{\alpha}^{i}(\Phi_{t}(y)),\end{split}

(94)

t\in(0,T),
where we have used the chain rule and the fact that
\Phi_{t}
solves (
88
). Setting
x=\Phi_{t}(y)
and rearranging the terms slightly we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi_{t}(x)=-b_{\alpha}^{i}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(x),\quad x\in\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in(0,T).

(95)

We are ready to verify that (
93
) holds. We write the left-hand side of (
93
) in coordinates as

\displaystyle((\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=

\displaystyle\,\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x),

(96)

x\in\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T).
Applying identity (
95
) on the right hand side of (
96
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial t}((\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=-\frac{\partial\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}}{\partial y_{l}}(\Psi_{t}(x))b_{\alpha}^{r}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi^{l}_{t}}{\partial x_{r}}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{l}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\\
&\quad-\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\left(\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{r}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi^{i_{1}}_{t}}{\partial x_{r}}(x)+b_{\alpha}^{r}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi^{i_{1}}_{t}}{\partial x_{r}x_{j_{1}}}(x)\right)\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{2}}}{\partial x_{j_{2}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\\
&-\cdots-\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k-1}}}{\partial x_{j_{k-1}}}(x)\\
&\hskip 150.0pt\times\left(\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{r}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi^{i_{k}}_{t}}{\partial x_{r}}(x)+b_{\alpha}^{r}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi^{i_{k}}_{t}}{\partial x_{r}x_{j_{k}}}(x)\right),\end{split}

(97)

for
x\in\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
. Using the explicit formula for Lie derivatives (
132
), we also compute the right-hand side of (
93
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}[(\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}])_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=b_{\alpha}^{l}(x)\frac{\partial\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}}{\partial y_{r}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\Psi^{r}_{t}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\\
&\quad+\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\left(b_{\alpha}^{l}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}\partial x_{l}}(x)+\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{l}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\right)\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{2}}}{\partial x_{j_{2}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\\
&+\cdots+\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\Psi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k-1}}}{\partial x_{j_{k-1}}}(x)\\
&\hskip 150.0pt\times\left(b_{\alpha}^{l}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}\partial x_{l}}(x)+\frac{\partial\Psi_{t}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{l}}{\partial x_{j_{k}}}(x)\right),\end{split}

(98)

x\in\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
.
Comparing the expressions (
97
) and (
98
), we clearly see that

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}((\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma})_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)=(\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}[(\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}])_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}}(x)

holds for
x\in\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}
and
t\in(0,T)
. The proof is now complete.
∎

We now show the existence of
L^{p}
-solutions (in the sense of Definition
7
) to (
1
) with a restriction on the dimension. More precisely, we will show the following result.

Proposition 30
.

Let
kp<n,

\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Gamma}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
be supported on
B(0,1/2),
and
0<T\leq 1-1/2^{1-\alpha}
. Then the
k
-form-valued function
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
defined in (
92
) complemented with the initial datum
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{0}:=\mathbf{K}_{0}
is an
L^{p}
-solution of the Lie transport equation (
1
) in the sense of Definition
7
.

Proof.

We split the argument into three steps.

Step 1. Demonstrating that
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\in L^{p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

It is easy to check that
(\phi^{0}_{\cdot})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}\in L^{p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
since
\mathbf{K}_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\psi^{0}\in C^{1}(\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\{t\}\times\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}\right);\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}),
where we recall that
\psi^{0}_{t}:=(\phi^{0}_{t})^{-1}
. We claim that
(\Phi^{0}_{\cdot})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}\in L^{p}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Indeed, by noting that
\Psi^{0}_{t}(x)=\frac{x}{\lvert x\rvert}\left(t-\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\Psi^{0}_{t})^{i}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)=\frac{\delta_{ij}\lvert x\rvert-\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}(t-\lvert x\rvert^{1-\alpha})-(1-\alpha)\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{\lvert x\rvert^{2-\alpha}},\quad t\in(0,T],\quad x\in\mathcal{B}^{0}_{t},

for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

We observe that the partial derivatives
\frac{\partial(\Psi^{0}_{t})^{i}}{\partial x_{j}}
behave at worst like
1/\lvert x\rvert
near zero. Therefore, using the explicit formula (
39
) for the push-forward, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{B(0,1)}\left((\Phi^{0}_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}(x)\right)^{p}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{\Gamma}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\left\lvert\int_{B(0,1)}\lvert x\rvert^{-kp}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right\rvert<\infty,

where we used the hypothesis
kp<n
.

Step 2.
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}
satisfies equation (
1
) in weak form.

We have to check that

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0,\quad t\in[0,T].

(99)

By observing that
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is supported on
B(0,1),
(
99
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{s}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0,

for
t\in[0,T],
where we recall that
\mathcal{X}_{\emptyset}=0
(and hence
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}^{0}}=0
) as assumed by our convention in Notations (Section
2.2
). We will first prove

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{s}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0.

(100)

By noting that
\partial\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}=S(0,1)\cup S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})
and applying the Reynolds transport theorem, we observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}&=\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\partial_{t}(K^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
&+\int_{S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})}(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)v_{t}(x)\cdot n(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{t},\end{split}

(101)

where
\mathrm{d}S_{t}
is the surface element,
v_{t}(x)
is the velocity of the boundary
S(0,r^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})
at
r=t
and
n(x)
is the outward pointing unit normal to
S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})
at the point
x\in S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})
. We also assumed summation over repeated indices as usual. For the first term after the equality in (
101
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\partial_{t}(K^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x=-\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{K}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

since by Proposition
29
,
\mathbf{K}_{t}
satisfies (
1
) strongly on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0},

t\in(0,T)
. By observing that

\displaystyle v_{t}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}n(x)\right)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}t^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}n(x),

we rewrite the second term after the equality in (
101
) as

\displaystyle\int_{S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})}(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)v_{t}(x)\cdot n(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{t}

\displaystyle\quad=t^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}(1-\alpha)^{-1}\int_{S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})}(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{t}.

Now we compute
\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
by integration by parts

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}(K^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x){(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\Theta)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt=\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}(K^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\left(-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}}\big(b_{\alpha}^{l}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\big)+\Theta_{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 180.0pt\left.+\cdots+\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},l}(x)\frac{\partial b_{\alpha}^{i_{k}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt=\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\mathcal{L}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\text{div}\left(b_{\alpha}(x)(K^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x,

(102)

where we have used the coordinate expression of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}
(see (
133
)). By the divergence theorem and explicit expression (
86
) of
b_{\alpha}
, we obtain

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}\text{div}\left(b_{\alpha}(x)(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})}b_{\alpha}(x)\cdot n(x)(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{t}

\displaystyle\quad=-t^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}(1-\alpha)^{-1}\int_{S(0,t^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})}(K_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\Theta_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{t}.

By putting together all the previous calculations, we can conclude that (
100
) holds. Finally, one can apply the same techniques to prove

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{0}}\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b_{\alpha}}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s=0,

for
t\in[0,T].
We conclude that (
99
) holds.

Step 3.
\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is weakly continuous.

Fix a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}}(\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad t\in[0,T].

By the regularity of
\phi_{t}
on
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}
and due to the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}
varies smoothly with
t,
it is not difficult to show that
\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{0}}(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is continuous in
t\in[0,T]
. Since
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}
varies smoothly with
t
and we have a
L^{p}_{loc}
control of
(\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}
on
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0},
it can also be shown that
\langle\langle\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{0}}(\Phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
is continuous in
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

7.2
Instantaneous blow-up of deterministic solutions

Let
n\geq 2.
Moreover, let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
R>0.
We define
b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime}
by the relations

\displaystyle(b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime})^{1}(x)=0,

\displaystyle(b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime})^{2}(x)=(\min\{\lvert x_{1}\rvert,R\})^{\alpha},

\displaystyle(b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime})^{i}(x)=0,\quad i=3,\ldots,n,

where
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and we note that the constant
R
has been introduced to obtain boundedness. One can check that
b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime}\in C_{b}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap W^{1,q}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
provided that

\displaystyle\alpha\in((q-1)/q,1).

(103)

The associated characteristic ODE is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\phi_{t}(x)=b_{\alpha,R}^{\prime}(\phi_{t}(x)),\\
&\phi_{0}(x)=x,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{split}

(104)

From now on we choose
R=1
without loss of generality as in the previous subsection and employ the notation
b_{\alpha}^{\prime}
. The solution to (
104
) can be explicitly computed to be

\displaystyle(\phi^{1}_{t})(x)=x_{1},

\displaystyle(\phi^{2}_{t})(x)=x_{2}+\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{\alpha}t,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert\leq 1,

\displaystyle(\phi^{2}_{t})(x)=x_{2}+t,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert>1,

\displaystyle(\phi^{i}_{t})(x)=x_{i},\quad i=3,\ldots,n,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover, the inverse can be easily verified to be given by

\displaystyle(\psi^{1}_{t})(x)=x_{1},

\displaystyle(\psi^{2}_{t})(x)=x_{2}-\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{\alpha}t,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert\leq 1,

\displaystyle(\psi^{2}_{t})(x)=x_{2}-t,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert>1,

\displaystyle(\psi^{i}_{t})(x)=x_{i},\quad i=3,\ldots,n,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi^{i}_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)=1,

for all
i=1,\ldots,n
(no sum assumed over repeated indices) and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi^{2}_{t}}{\partial x_{1}}(x)=\begin{cases}-\text{sgn}(x_{1})\alpha\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{\alpha-1}t,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert<1,\\
0,\quad\lvert x_{1}\rvert>1,\end{cases}

where
sgn
is the sign function. All the other partial derivatives
\frac{\partial\phi_{t}^{i}}{\partial x_{j}}
(i.e. when
i\neq j
and
(i,j)\neq(2,1)
) are zero. Let
k\notin\{0,n\}
and choose the initial datum

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{0}:=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,1)}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{k+1},

which is trivially of class
L^{p}
. By direct computation (or simply by integrating the equations), it can be checked that the solution to the deterministic Lie transport equation (
85
) with vector field
b_{\alpha}^{\prime}
must be given by
(\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0}
as expected.

We also have (take into account the formula for the push-forward (
47
))

\displaystyle(\mathbf{K}_{t})_{1,3,\ldots,k+1}(x)=((\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0})_{1,3,\ldots,k+1}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{B(0,1)}\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{2}}{\partial x_{1}}(x)\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{3}}{\partial x_{3}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{k}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{k+1}}{\partial x_{k+1}}(x)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{X}_{B(0,1)}\text{sgn}(x_{1})\alpha\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{\alpha-1}t.

Hence, for every
\epsilon,t>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B(0,\epsilon)}\left\lvert((\phi_{t})_{*}\mathbf{K}_{0})_{1,3,\ldots,k+1}(x)\right\rvert=\infty,

which clearly implies that for every
\epsilon,t>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B(0,\epsilon)}\lvert\mathbf{K}_{t}(x)\rvert=\infty.

Furthermore, we observe that the only other nonzero component of
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is
(K_{t})_{1,2,3,\ldots,k}(x)=1
(recall the above observation regarding the partial derivatives of
\psi_{t}
).
Therefore
\mathbf{K}
is of class
L^{p}
provided that
\alpha\in((p-1)/p,1)
. Moreover,
(p-1)/p\geq(q-1)/q
since
p\geq 2\geq q,
so in this case, condition (
103
) is met. It is also clear that
\mathbf{K}_{t}
is weakly continuous in time, so it a solution to (
85
) in the sense of Definition
7
.

Finally, note that
\mathbf{K}_{0}
can be easily modified to have
C^{\infty}_{c}
-regularity in a way that
\mathbf{K}_{t}
would still be a solution of (
104
). For example, choose
\mathbf{K}_{0}
such that (i)
\mathbf{K}_{0}:=\mathrm{d}x_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{k+1}
on
B(0,1/2),
(ii)
\mathbf{K}_{0}
vanishes outside
B(0,1),
(iii)
\mathbf{K}_{0}
is of class
C^{\infty},
and (iv)
(K_{0})_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}=0
when
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})\neq(2,3,\dots,k+1).
It is not difficult to show that formation of instantaneous singularities via the same mechanism persists in this case. This means that
regardless of the regularity of the initial datum
, instantaneous blow-up of the supremum norm can take place in the deterministic case.

Declarations

On behalf of all authors, the corresponding author states that there are no competing interests.

8
Technical proofs

8.1
Proof of estimates (
35
) and (
36
) in Lemma
11
(Step 4 of the proof of Theorem
2
)

Step 4.1.
For simplicity, we set
\xi:=1
, but our argument can easily be generalised to allow for general diffusion vector fields of class
L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{2+\alpha}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. To avoid confusion with the time integration variables, we employ the notation
p
instead of
r
for the number
r\geq 2
in Lemma
11
. We divide the proof into three steps because it involves long computations. Estimate (
35
) follows easily from (
16
), but we outline a self-contained proof here since the arguments and the result will be used in the next steps. Since for fixed
s\in[0,T],
the process
Y_{t}(x)=\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x)),

t\geq s,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies the modified SDE (
34
) and
\phi_{s,t}(x)=\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))
with
y=\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)
, we have the following equation for its (spatial) derivative

\displaystyle\begin{split}&DY_{t}(x)=D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)+\lambda\int^{t}_{s}D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x))DY_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\hskip 70.0pt+\int^{t}_{s}D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x))DY_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r},\end{split}

(105)

where
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To prove the first bound (
35
), we observe that
D\phi_{s,t}(x)=D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))DY_{t}(x)
and

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}(x)\rvert^{p}\right]\leq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,y)\rvert^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert DY_{t}(x)\rvert^{p}\right].

Since by Lemma
8
,
D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)
is bounded uniformly in time, we are only left to show the bound

\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert DY_{t}(x)\rvert^{p}\right]<\infty
, which can be proven directly from (
105
) by a standard argument using Grönwall’s lemma and Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, together with the fact that
D\psi_{\lambda},D\Psi_{\lambda},D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1},D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}
are bounded
uniformly in time
.

Step 4.2.
It is easy to check that the constants appearing in all the inequalities
\lesssim
in the rest of the proof are
independent of
s
, so we will not stress this again to avoid repetition. We claim that for fixed
\beta\in(0,1],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert\phi_{s,t}(x)-\phi_{s,t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta p}

(106)

holds for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To verify this, we first note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert\phi_{s,t}(x)-\phi_{s,t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))-\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(y))\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert Y_{t}(x)-Y_{t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right],

where we have taken into account that the Lipschitz seminorm
[\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)]_{\text{Lip}}
is bounded uniformly in time since
\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}))
by Lemma
8
. By using the modified SDE (
34
), we also have

\displaystyle\lvert Y_{t}(x)-Y_{t}(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert

\displaystyle+\lambda\int^{t}_{s}\lvert\psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-\psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\rvert\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle+\left\lvert\int^{t}_{s}\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\right)\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\rvert.

Employing the fact that
\psi_{\lambda}
and
\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}
are globally Lipschitz, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lvert Y_{t}(x)-Y_{t}(y)\rvert\leq\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert+C_{1}\int^{t}_{s}\lvert Y_{r}(x)-Y_{r}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\quad+\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\left\lvert\int^{u}_{s}\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\right)\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\rvert,\end{split}

(107)

where
C_{1}=\lambda\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[\psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)]_{\text{Lip}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)]_{\text{Lip}}
is a finite constant. Applying Grönwall’s inequality to (
107
), we get

\displaystyle\lvert Y_{t}(x)-Y_{t}(y)\rvert\leq\text{exp}(C_{1}T)\,\bigg(\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\left\lvert\int^{u}_{s}\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\right)\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\rvert\bigg).

By raising both sides to the power of
p
and applying the supremum in time, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\lvert Y_{u}(x)-Y_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\leq\text{exp}(p\,C_{1}T)\,\bigg(\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\\
&\quad+\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\left\lvert\int^{u}_{s}\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\right)\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\rvert^{p}\bigg).\end{split}

(108)

Now using Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, there exists a constant
C^{*}>0
(dependent on
p
) such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\left\lvert\int^{u}_{s}\left(D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x)))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y)))\right)\mathrm{d}W_{r}\right\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{*}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{s}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y))\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{p/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int^{t}_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq r}\lvert Y_{u}(x)-Y_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}r,

(109)

9
9

9

Explicitly,
C_{2}=C^{*}T^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[D\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)]_{\text{Lip}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}[\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)]_{\text{Lip}}\right)^{p}
.

where in the last line we have taken into account that
p\geq 2.
Hence, taking expectations in (
108
) and applying Grönwall’s inequality to the function

\displaystyle f(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\lvert Y_{u}(x)-Y_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right],\quad t\in[0,T],

(use inequality (
8.1
)), we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\lvert Y_{u}(x)-Y_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\leq C_{3}\lvert\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\beta p},

(110)

for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
where
C_{3}=\text{exp}[C_{2}T\,\text{exp}(p\,C_{1}T)]<\infty
and we have used the fact that the
\beta
-Hölder seminorm
[\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)]_{\beta}
is uniformly bounded in time.

Step 4.3.
Finally, we establish estimate (
36
). We do not write down the explicit constants in the remaining inequalities for simplicity. For this, employing a similar argument as in the previous steps, we start by noting that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\phi_{s,t}(x)-D\phi_{s,t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))DY_{t}(x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(y))DY_{t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))\rvert^{p}\lvert DY_{t}(x)-DY_{t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert DY_{t}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(y))\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert DY_{t}(x)-DY_{t}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]

(111)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2},

(112)

where we have applied Hölder’s inequality in the last line and have taken into account the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert DY_{t}(x)\rvert^{2p}\right]
is uniformly bounded in
s
as shown at the beginning of this proof. The last term can be treated easily as

\displaystyle\eqref{horrible2}\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq t\leq T}\lvert Y_{t}(x)-Y_{t}(y)\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha p},

where we have used (
110
) with
\beta=\alpha
and the fact that
D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}
is globally Lipschitz uniformly in time. For the term (
111
), using (
105
) and defining

\displaystyle P_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=D\psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x))D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x)),

\displaystyle Q_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\phi_{s,t}(x))D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,Y_{t}(x)),

\displaystyle M_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=\int^{t}_{s}Q_{r}(x)DY_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r},

for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we obtain the following inequality

\displaystyle\lvert DY_{t}(x)-DY_{t}(y)\rvert\leq\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert+\lvert M_{t}(x)-M_{t}(y)\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda\int^{t}_{s}\lvert P_{r}(x)DY_{r}(x)-P_{r}(y)DY_{r}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\leq\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert+\sup_{s\leq r\leq t}\lvert M_{r}(x)-M_{r}(y)\rvert\\
&\qquad+\lambda\left(\int^{t}_{s}\lvert P_{r}(x)\rvert\lvert DY_{r}(x)-DY_{r}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}r+\int^{t}_{s}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert\lvert P_{r}(x)-P_{r}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}r\right).\end{split}

(113)

By the same argument used in Step 4.2 to derive (
8.1
), we can apply Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\lvert M_{u}(x)-M_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\lesssim\int_{s}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq r}\lvert DY_{u}(x)-DY_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{t}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert Q_{r}(x)-Q_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right].

By substituting the last inequality in (
8.1
), taking the power of
p
, and applying supremum and expectation, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\eqref{horrible1}&\lesssim\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\\
&\quad+\int_{s}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq r}\lvert DY_{u}(x)-DY_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}r\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{t}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert Q_{r}(x)-Q_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\\
&\quad+\int^{t}_{s}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq r}\lvert P_{u}(x)\rvert^{p}\lvert DY_{u}(x)-DY_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}r\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert DY_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert P_{u}(x)-P_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right],\end{split}

(114)

where we have also used Hölder’s inequality and Tonelli’s theorem to switch the domains of integration in the last line. By applying Grönwall’s lemma in (
114
) (these techniques are similar as the ones in Step 4.2), we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq t}\lvert DY_{u}(x)-DY_{u}(y)\rvert^{p}\right]\lesssim\Bigg(\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert^{p}

(115)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert P_{r}(x)-P_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert Q_{r}(x)-Q_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\Bigg)

(116)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\times\text{exp}\left(T+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq u\leq T}\lvert P_{r}(x)\rvert^{p}\right]\mathrm{d}s\right).

(117)

The term in (
117
) is clearly controlled. The term after the inequality in (
115
) can be controlled easily as

\displaystyle\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,x)-D\Psi_{\lambda}(s,y)\rvert^{p}\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha p}.

For the first term in (
116
), we apply Hölder’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert P_{r}(x)-P_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert P_{r}(x)-P_{r}(y)\rvert^{2p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq T}\lvert D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))\rvert^{2p}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{t}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{t}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\lvert D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))-D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq T}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\!\!

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq T}\lvert D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))-D\psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha p},

where in the last inequality, we have employed (
106
) and (
110
) with
\beta=\alpha,
together with the fact that
D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1},D\psi_{\lambda}
are globally Lipschitz uniformly in time.
For the second term in (
116
), we get the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{s}\lvert DY_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\lvert Q_{r}(x)-Q_{r}(y)\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{s}\lvert Q_{r}(x)-Q_{r}(y)\rvert^{2p}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq t}\lvert D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(x))-D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(r,Y_{r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}\!\!

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq t}\lvert D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(x))-D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}(r,\phi_{s,r}(y))\rvert^{2p}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq t}\lvert Y_{r}(x)-Y_{r}(y)\rvert^{2\alpha p}\right]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leq r\leq t}\lvert\phi_{s,r}(x)-\phi_{s,r}(y)\rvert^{2\alpha p}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha p},

where in the fourth line, we employed the fact that the
\alpha
-Hölder seminorms
[D\Psi_{\lambda}^{-1}(t,\cdot)]_{\alpha},[D^{2}\Psi_{\lambda}(t,\cdot)]_{\alpha}
are bounded uniformly in time and in the last line, we applied (
106
) and (
110
) with
\beta=1
. Our proof is complete.

8.2
Proof of the claim in the last paragraph of the proof of Proposition
21

We fix a test
k
-form
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and check term by term convergence in the following equation

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}_{\cdot},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{0},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\int^{\cdot}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b^{\epsilon}}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s-\int^{\cdot}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{\cdot}_{0}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
.

•

The first term on the left-hand side converges as

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}_{\cdot},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\rightarrow\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{\cdot},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}},

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0,
by definition due to the weak convergence of
\mathbf{K}^{\epsilon}
.

•

The Stratonovich-to-Itô correction term in the second line. We claim that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).
Indeed, we observe that the mapping
h\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}h\,\mathrm{d}s
is linear continuous from the space of adapted processes of class
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
into itself, and therefore also weakly continuous. Hence
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]).

•

The diffusion term on the right-hand side.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s},

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
, since the mapping
h\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}h\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}
is linear continuous from the space of adapted processes of class
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
into itself.

•

The drift term on the right-hand side. We check that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b^{\epsilon}}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
. To this purpose, one can verify that for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left(\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b^{\epsilon}}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}-\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}s\,f(\cdot,t)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\lvert\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert

(118)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lvert\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b^{\epsilon}}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}-\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert.

(119)

The term (
118
) converges to zero by applying the dominated convergence theorem to the function
F_{1}^{\epsilon}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]
. For this, we note that by defining the test function
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}(\omega,s,x):=f(\omega,t)\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}(x)
,
10
10

10

It should be understood that
b
depends on
s
.
we have

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\epsilon}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]=\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega,s,x}}\rightarrow 0,

by the weak convergence of
\mathbf{K}_{t}^{\epsilon}
to
\mathbf{K}_{t}
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By choosing
R
such that
\mathbf{\Theta}
has support on
B(0,R),
we also have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega,s,x}}\right\rvert\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}(\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s})\rVert_{L^{p}_{s,x}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rVert_{L^{q}_{s,x}}\lvert f(\cdot,t)\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{X}_{B(0,R)}(\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{s})\rVert_{L^{p}_{\omega,s,x}}\|\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\|_{L^{q}_{s,x}}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert f(\cdot,t)\rvert\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert f(\cdot,t)\rvert\right]<\infty,

where we have taken into account the uniform bound (
50
), Hypothesis
2

(i)
, and the embedding
L^{p}_{loc}\subset L^{2}_{loc},

p\geq 2
. Since
f\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T])\subset L^{1}(\Omega\times[0,T]),
we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert f(\cdot,t)\rvert\right]\in L^{1}([0,T]).
Similarly, the term (
119
) also converges to zero by applying the dominated convergence theorem to
F_{2}^{\epsilon}(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\langle\langle\mathbf{K}_{s}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{L}_{b^{\epsilon}}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}-\mathcal{L}_{b}^{*}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}f(\cdot,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right].
The proof is now complete.

8.3
Proof of Lemma
22

We prove our result by induction on
k
(i.e., the degree of the multivector field
\mathbf{U}
).

Step 1.

First consider the base case
k=1
(i.e.
\mathbf{U}
is a vector field). Employing the notation
\psi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}
as usually, the pullback of
\mathbf{U}
has the global coordinate expression

\displaystyle(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{U})^{i}(x)=U^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i}}{\partial y_{j}}(\phi_{t}(x)),\quad i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(120)

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\lVert(\phi_{\cdot}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U}(\cdot)-(\phi_{\cdot})^{*}\mathbf{U}(\cdot)\rVert^{r}_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert\mathbf{U}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert^{r}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert\mathbf{U}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-\mathbf{U}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mathbf{U}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{r}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

(121)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad+\lVert D\mathbf{U}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{r}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{2r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}\\
&\hskip 50.0pt\times\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{2r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}.\end{split}

(122)

We now proceed to prove that the term (
121
) converges to zero. Dealing with the term (
122
) is easy since by standard arguments, we have the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{2r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}(y)\rvert^{2r}\lvert J\psi_{t}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}^{4r}}^{r}\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}^{2n}}^{n/2}<\infty,

and taking into account (
37
), we obtain convergence to zero. Hence, it suffices only to establish the convergence of (
121
). We observe

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

(123)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

(124)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right].

(125)

Changing coordinates
y=\phi^{m}_{t}(x)
, we obtain convergence of the first term after the inequality as

(
124
)

\displaystyle=\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))-D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R)}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(y)-D\psi_{t}(y)\rvert^{r}\lvert J\psi_{t}^{m}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)-D\psi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|^{r}_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R}}\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2n}_{\omega,t,R}}^{n}\rightarrow 0,

where we have taken into account property (
38
) of the flow. Fix
\alpha^{\prime}\in(\frac{1}{4r},\alpha-\frac{n}{4r})
11
11

11

We can guarantee that
\alpha-\frac{n}{4r}>\frac{1}{4r}
