since we have imposed
r\alpha>(n+1)/4.
.
To establish the convergence of (
125
), we
set
\widetilde{r}(x):=\sqrt{1+|x|^{2}}
and observe that

\displaystyle\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert=\lvert\widetilde{r}(\phi_{t}(x))\widetilde{r}^{-1}(\phi_{t}(x))(D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x)))\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\widetilde{r}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert\Big(\left\lvert\widetilde{r}^{-1}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-\widetilde{r}^{-1}(\phi_{t}(x))D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert\big\lvert\widetilde{r}^{-1}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-\widetilde{r}^{-1}(\phi_{t}(x))\big\rvert\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\widetilde{r}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert\left(C_{1}(\omega,t)\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{\alpha^{\prime}}+C_{2}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert\right),

where

\displaystyle C_{1}(\omega,t):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert\widetilde{r}^{-1}(x)D\psi_{s,t}(x)-\widetilde{r}^{-1}(y)D\psi_{s,t}(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert^{\alpha^{\prime}}}

is finite
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
0\leq t\leq T
by property (
19
) of the flow with
\epsilon=1
and

\displaystyle C_{2}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\lvert\widetilde{r}^{-1}(x)-\widetilde{r}^{-1}(y)\rvert}{\lvert x-y\rvert}

is finite since
\widetilde{r}^{-1}
is globally Lipschitz. Hence, by applying Hölder’s inequality in the whole space, we get

\displaystyle\eqref{splitting2}=\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))-D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert\widetilde{r}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\left(C_{1}(\cdot,t)\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert\widetilde{r}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\left(C_{2}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert\right)^{r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\widetilde{r}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}C_{1}^{2r}(\cdot,t)\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{2r\alpha^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad+C_{2}^{2r}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x)))\rvert^{2r}\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{2r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\|\widetilde{r}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}[\text{(I)}+\text{(II)}].

By our usual arguments, we have the control

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{r}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert\widetilde{r}(y)\rvert^{2r}\lvert J\phi_{t}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{r}\|_{L^{\infty}_{R^{\prime}}}^{r}\|D\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{n}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{n/2}<\infty.

Moreover, for term (I) we have the convergence

\displaystyle\text{(I)}\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}C_{1}^{4r}(\cdot,t)\right]^{1/4}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert\phi_{t}^{m}(x)-\phi_{t}(x)\rvert^{4\alpha^{\prime}r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/4}\rightarrow 0,

where we have applied Hölder’s inequality, taken into account property (
19
) with
4r
instead of
r
to control the weighted Hölder norm of
C_{1},
and employed (
37
) to establish convergence of the second term (note that this can be done since
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{4r}
and therefore
4\alpha^{\prime}r\geq 1
). For the second term (II), we have

(II)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{\prime})}\lvert D\psi_{t}(y)\rvert^{4r}\lvert J\phi_{t}^{m}(x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/4}\|\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)-\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{4r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{8r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}\|D\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2n}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{n/4}\|\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)-\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{4r}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}^{r}\rightarrow 0,

where we have employed our usual strategies and the fact that
\|D\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot)\|_{L^{s}_{\omega,t,R^{\prime}}}
is uniformly bounded for any
s\geq 1
, by property (
17
) of the flow. This concludes the convergence (
51
) when
\mathbf{U}
is a vector field.

Step 2.
We now proceed to perform the induction step. Here, we only do the case
k=2
for the sake of communicating the idea more clearly; however, proving this in the general case is straightforward, albeit notationally convoluted. If
\mathbf{U}
is a two-vector field, we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{U})^{ij}(x)=U^{kl}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{j}}{\partial y_{l}}(\phi_{t}(x)),\quad i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(126)

Hence, for fixed
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},
we obtain

\displaystyle((\phi_{t}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U})^{ij}(x)-(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{U})^{ij}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}}{\partial y_{l}}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\left(U^{kl}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\frac{\partial(\psi_{t}^{m})^{i}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-U^{kl}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}(x))\right)

\displaystyle\quad+U^{kl}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi^{i}_{t}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}(x))\left(\frac{\partial(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}}{\partial y_{l}}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-\frac{\partial\psi^{j}_{t}}{\partial y_{l}}(\phi_{t}(x))\right).

(127)

Define

\displaystyle v^{l,i}(t,x):=U^{kl}(\phi_{t}(x))\frac{\partial\psi_{t}^{i}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}(x)),\qquad v_{m}^{l,i}(t,x):=U^{kl}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\frac{\partial(\psi_{t}^{m})^{i}}{\partial y_{k}}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x)).

Observe that for fixed index
l
, these are identical to the expression (
120
) of the pullback of a vector field that we considered in the previous step.
12
12

12

In the general
k
case, one can set
v
and
v_{m}
similarly so that they adopt the expression of the pullback of
(k-1)
-vector fields (for example, in the case
k=3
, they should take the form (
126
)).
We also define
v
and
v_{m}
to be the tuples
v:=(v^{1},\ldots,v^{n}),
where
v^{l}:=(v^{l,1},\ldots,v^{l,n}),
and
v_{m}:=(v^{1}_{m},\ldots,v^{n}_{m}),
where
v_{m}^{l}:=(v_{m}^{l,1},\ldots,v_{m}^{l,n}),
respectively, for
l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.
For every
l
, the sequence
(v_{m}^{l})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\left\lvert v_{m}^{l}(t,x)-v^{l}(t,x)\right\rvert^{2r}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\rightarrow 0,\quad m\rightarrow\infty,

(128)

by the inductive hypothesis. Notice that since
r>(n+1)/(\alpha 2^{k+1}),
we have
2r>(n+1)/(\alpha 2^{k}),
which satisfies our assumption at Step
k-1
.

Next, applying Cauchy-Schwartz inequality in (
8.3
) to get

\displaystyle\lvert(\phi_{t}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U})^{ij}(x)-(\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{U})^{ij}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert D(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert\lvert C_{m}^{i}(t,x)-C^{i}(t,x)\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\lvert C^{i}(t,x)\rvert\lvert D(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))-D\psi_{t}^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert,

where
C^{i}:=(\sum_{l}(v^{l,i})^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
C^{i}_{m}:=(\sum_{l}(v^{l,i}_{m})^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then applying the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{ij}\lvert A_{ij}+B_{ij}\rvert^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\sum_{ij}A_{ij}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Big(\sum_{ij}B_{ij}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

on the RHS (take
A_{ij}:=\lvert D(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert\lvert C_{m}^{i}(t,x)-C^{i}(t,x)\rvert
and
B_{ij}:=\lvert C^{i}(t,x)\rvert\lvert D(\psi_{t}^{m})^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))-D\psi_{t}^{j}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert
), we have

\displaystyle\lvert(\phi_{t}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U}(x)-\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{U}(x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert\lvert v_{m}(t,x)-v(t,x)\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\lvert v(t,x)\rvert\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert,

where the norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert
on
v
(likewise for
v_{m}
) is defined as

\displaystyle\lvert v(t,x)\rvert=\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}|v^{l}(t,x)\rvert^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v^{l,i}(t,x))^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

This yields

\displaystyle\lVert(\phi_{\cdot}^{m})^{*}\mathbf{U}(\cdot)-(\phi_{\cdot})^{*}\mathbf{U}(\cdot)\rVert^{r}_{L^{r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))\rvert^{r}\lvert v_{m}(t,x)-v(t,x)\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R^{*}(m))}\lvert v(t,x)\rvert^{r}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(\phi_{t}^{m}(x))-D\psi_{t}(\phi_{t}(x))\rvert^{r}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}.

Now using Hölder’s inequality, we obtain the following estimate for the first term

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\|v_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)-v(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}.

By our usual arguments, we have the control

\displaystyle\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int_{B(0,R)}\lvert D\psi_{t}^{m}(y)\rvert^{2r}\lvert J\psi_{t}^{m}(y)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y\,\mathrm{d}t\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\|D\psi^{m}_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{4r}_{\omega,t,R}}^{r}\|D\psi^{m}_{\cdot}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2n}_{\omega,t,R}}^{n/2}<\infty,

where we used property (
17
). Noting that

\displaystyle\lvert v_{m}(t,x)-v(t,x)\rvert^{2r}=\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\lvert v_{m}^{l}(t,x)-v^{l}(t,x)\rvert^{2}\right)^{r}\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{n}\lvert v_{m}^{l}(t,x)-v^{l}(t,x)\rvert^{2r},

we also get

\displaystyle\|v_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)-v(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|v_{m}^{l}(\cdot,\cdot)-v^{l}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\rightarrow 0,

where we used the inductive hypothesis (
128
) to deduce the convergence in the last line. Hence
I_{1}\rightarrow 0
.
Applying Hölder’s inequality, we also have

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|v(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))-D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\|U(\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}^{r}\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))-D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\rightarrow 0,

where we used that
\|D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}<\infty
by standard arguments and the convergence
\|D\psi_{\cdot}^{m}(\phi_{\cdot}^{m}(\cdot))-D\psi_{\cdot}(\phi_{\cdot}(\cdot))\|_{L^{2r}_{\omega,t,R^{*}(m)}}^{r}\rightarrow 0
holds by following similar arguments as in Step 1 of the proof, where we established convergence of (
123
). The same arguments hold in the general
k
case. This concludes our proof.

8.4
Proof of KIW formula for
k
-form-valued processes (Theorem
1
)

We first establish (
9
). We divide our proof into several steps.

Step 1. Setup and preparation.

For simplicity and without loss of generality, we choose
N=M=1
so that the only diffusion vector field in the flow equation (
8
) is
\xi=(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{n})
. Given a multi-vector field
\mathbf{U}=u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge u_{k}\in\mathfrak{X}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
we consider the following real-valued continuous
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-semimartingale

\displaystyle F_{t}(x):=\langle\phi^{*}_{t}\mathbf{K}(t,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle=K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x),

(129)

for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
In (
129
),
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
represents the dual pairing, we employed the notation

J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x):=\frac{\partial\phi^{i_{\alpha}}_{t}}{\partial x_{j_{\alpha}}}(x),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and we stress that summation over repeated indices
i_{\alpha}=1,\ldots,n,

\alpha=1,\ldots,k
is implied as usually.

Step 2. Equation for
F_{t}
in simple form.

We employ differential notation to avoid excessively convoluted formulas. Itô’s product rule, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))

\displaystyle\quad+K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}\left(\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{d}\left[K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(\cdot,\phi_{\cdot}(x)),\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(\cdot,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right]_{t},

where
[\cdot,\cdot]_{t}
denotes cross-variation. By applying the Itô-Wentzell formula to
K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))
and using the flow equation (
8
), one can check that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))=G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}t+H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+(b\cdot DK_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}t+(\xi\cdot DK_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+(\xi\cdot DH_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}[W_{\cdot},B_{\cdot}]_{t}+\frac{1}{2}(\xi\cdot D(\xi\cdot DK_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}))(t,\phi_{t}(x))\,\mathrm{d}t,

for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Remark 14
.

We note that
G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
and
H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
do not satisfy the assumptions of the classical Itô-Wentzell formula (Theorem 1.1. in
[
27
]
), since they are not continuous in time. However, as noted at the end of page 24 in
[
18
]
, Kunita’s theorems are stated for processes integrated with respect to continuous semimartingales, and hence the assumptions imposed are those necessary to treat semimartingales. In the present case, it is easy to prove that adaptability,
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
integrability in time of
G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
and
H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
(respectively), and regularity in space are enough. For instance, in
[
25
]
, the author provides an extension of the classical Itô-Wentzell formula that allows for drifts and diffusions
G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
and
H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
of such time regularity, and even for distribution-valued processes.

By Itô’s product rule, we also obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)=\sum_{p=1}^{k}\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}J^{i_{p}}_{j_{p}}(t,x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q=1\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}^{k}\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p,q}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(t,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x)u_{q}^{j_{q}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\left[J^{i_{p}}_{j_{p}}(\cdot,x),J^{i_{q}}_{j_{q}}(\cdot,x)\right]_{t}.

We differentiate (
8
) with respect to
x
to arrive at

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}J_{j}^{i}(t,x)

\displaystyle=D_{l}(b^{i}+(1/2)\xi^{m}D_{m}\xi^{i})(t,\phi_{t}(x))J_{j}^{l}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+D_{l}\xi^{i}(t,\phi_{t}(x))J_{j}^{l}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},

for
i,j=1,\ldots,n
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Step 3. Full equation for
F_{t}
.

By using all the formulas obtained in Step 2, one checks that for
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\omega
independent of
u,

F_{t}(x)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle F_{t}(x)-F_{0}(x)=

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\hat{G}^{1}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\hat{G}^{2}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}[W_{\cdot},B_{\cdot}]_{s}

\displaystyle\quad+\int^{t}_{0}\hat{H}^{1}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int^{t}_{0}\hat{H}^{2}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s},

where

\displaystyle\hat{G}^{1}_{s}(x):=G_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle+b^{l}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{k}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}b^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)J^{l}_{j_{p}}(s,x)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{l}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\xi^{m}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{lm}^{2}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{m}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{m}\xi^{l}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{p=1}^{k}\Bigg[\xi^{m}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}\xi^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{m}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}D_{l}\left[\xi^{m}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{m}\xi^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\right]K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)J^{l}_{j_{p}}(s,x)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q=1\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}^{k}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}\xi^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{m}\xi^{i_{q}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p,q}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)J^{l}_{j_{p}}(s,x)J^{m}_{j_{q}}(s,x)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x)u_{q}^{j_{q}}(x),

\displaystyle\hat{G}^{2}_{s}(x):=\xi^{l}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{k}H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}\xi^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)J^{l}_{j_{p}}(s,x)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x),

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{1}_{s}(x):=H_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x),

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{2}_{s}(x):=\xi^{l}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\prod_{\alpha=1}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{k}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))D_{l}\xi^{i_{p}}(s,\phi_{s}(x))\left(\prod_{\alpha\neq p}^{k}J^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}(s,x)u_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(x)\right)J^{l}_{j_{p}}(s,x)u_{p}^{j_{p}}(x).

Step 4. Conclusion.
By inspection and taking into account formula (
132
), we obtain that for
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\omega
independent of
\mathbf{U}:

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\hat{G}^{1}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi_{s}^{*}\mathbf{G}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 90.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\hat{G}^{2}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}[W_{\cdot},B_{\cdot}]_{s}=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi^{*}_{s}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{H}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}[W_{\cdot},B_{\cdot}]_{s},

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\hat{H}^{1}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi^{*}_{s}\mathbf{H}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\hat{H}^{2}_{s}(x)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}=\int^{t}_{0}\langle\phi_{s}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\mathbf{K}(s,x),\mathbf{U}(x)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}B_{s},\quad t\in[0,T],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where we remind
\mathbf{U}=u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge u_{k}.
Since
\mathbf{U}
was chosen arbitrarily, the proof of (
9
) is finished.
The proof of (
10
) follows similar steps by noting that

1.

(\phi_{t})_{*}=\psi_{t}^{*},
where
\psi_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}.

2.

Using the identity (
94
), one can show that the inverse flow satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\psi_{t}(x)&=\left(-b^{i}(t,x)\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)+\xi^{i}(t,x)\frac{\partial\xi^{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(t,x)\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)\right.\\
&\qquad\left.+\xi^{i}(t,x)\xi^{j}(t,x)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{t}}{\partial x_{j}\partial x_{i}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}t-\xi^{i}(t,x)\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\mathrm{d}B_{t},\end{split}

(130)

for
t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
where we recall that summation over repeated indices is implied.

3.

One can apply the Itô-Wentzell formula to evaluate
K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(t,x)
along
\psi_{t}(x)
using (
130
).

9
Background in tensor algebra and calculus

In this appendix, we provide some background on concepts from differential geometry that we use throughout this article. Although these concepts are naturally introduced on smooth manifolds, here we restrict ourselves to the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, since (1) we only need this setting for our analysis, (2) we avoid defining manifolds and dealing with the arising technicalities.

We first introduce the notion of tensor fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and discuss some operations defined on them, such as raising and lowering indices when each tangent space (defined later on) is equipped with an inner product (the metric tensor). Next, we present some common classes of tensor fields that arise naturally in physics, namely, the tensors of alternating types. We also discuss some of the common operations than can be performed on tensor fields, including the Lie derivative, which is a fundamental operator that we need in order to define our main equation. Whereas in the present discussion, we assume for simplicity that the tensor fields we work with are smooth, in the proceeding appendix we will introduce tensor fields of different regularity classes. This will be fundamental for our analysis.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space with basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\},
and
V^{*}
be its corresponding dual space with dual basis
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{n}\}
, defined via the relation
\left<e^{i},e_{j}\right>=\delta_{ij}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta (that is,
\delta_{ij}=1
when
i=j
and zero otherwise), where
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>:V^{*}\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the natural pairing
\left<\alpha,v\right>:=\alpha(v)
. For a contravariant vector
v\in V
, we express its components as
v=v^{i}e_{i}
using raised indices, and for a covariant vector (covector)
\alpha\in V^{*}
, we express its components as
\alpha=\alpha_{i}e^{i}
with lowered indices. We always assume Einstein’s convention of summing over repeated indices.

Definition 8
.

Given an
n
-dimensional vector space
V
and its dual
V^{*},
we define an
(r,s)
-tensor
\mathbf{T}
to be a multilinear map

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}:\underbrace{V^{*}\times\cdots\times V^{*}}_{r\text{ times}}\times\underbrace{V\times\cdots\times V}_{s\text{ times}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

Fixing a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
for
V
and
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{n}\}
for
V^{*}
, we may interpret tensors as vectors with basis
\{e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{r}}\otimes e^{i_{r+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{i_{r+s}}\}_{i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
, where

\displaystyle e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{r}}\otimes e^{i_{r+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{i_{r+s}}\left(e^{j_{1}},\ldots,e^{j_{r}},e_{j_{r+1}},\ldots,e_{j_{r+s}}\right)

\displaystyle:=\delta_{i_{1}}^{j_{1}}\cdots\delta_{i_{r}}^{j_{r}}\delta^{i_{r+1}}_{j_{r+1}}\cdots\delta^{i_{r+s}}_{j_{r+s}}.

We can express
\mathbf{T}
in coordinates as

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{r}}\otimes e^{i_{r+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{i_{r+s}},

where
T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}=\mathbf{T}\left(e^{i_{1}},\ldots,e^{i_{r}},e_{i_{r+1}},\ldots,e_{i_{r+s}}\right)
are its components for
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r+s}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Note that summation over repeated indices is assumed. We denote the space of
(r,s)
-tensors over
V
by
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{V}
.

Given two tensors
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{V}
and
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}_{V}
, we define the tensor product
\otimes
between these two tensors as the new tensor
\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r+r^{\prime},s+s^{\prime})}_{V}
given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}\otimes\mathbf{T}:=S^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}T^{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r^{\prime}}}_{j_{r^{\prime}+1},\ldots,j_{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}}\bigotimes_{a=1}^{r}e_{i_{a}}\otimes\bigotimes_{b=1}^{r^{\prime}}e_{j_{b}}\otimes\bigotimes_{c=r+1}^{r+s}e^{i_{c}}\otimes\bigotimes_{d=r^{\prime}+1}^{r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}}e^{i_{d}},

(131)

where for simplicity, we employed the notations
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}e_{i_{j}}:=e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{l}}
and
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}e^{i_{j}}:=e^{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{i_{m}}
for any integers
l,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

9.1
Smooth sections of tensor bundles

We wish to introduce the notion of tensor field, which is a function that assigns a tensor to every point in a manifold (in the present case, in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
). Tensors fields help formalise various objects of physical origin mathematically, such as Eulerian flow field, vorticity, and density.

First, we need to introduce the notion of tangent space at a point. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
The tangent space at
x,
denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n},
is an equivalence class of smooth curves
c:(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
c(0)=x
, where we say that two curves
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are equivalent if and only if
Dc_{1}(0)=Dc_{2}(0)
. One can check that this forms an
n
-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{R}
. We define the tangent bundle as the tuple
(\mathcal{T}{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}:=\{(x,v):x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,v\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
and
\pi
is the natural projection map
\pi:\mathcal{T}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is defined by
\pi(x,v)=x,
for all
(x,v)\in\mathcal{T}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Since
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a vector space, there exists a corresponding dual space, denoted
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and referred to as the cotangent space. We analogously define the cotangent bundle as the tuple
(\mathcal{T}^{*}{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}:=\{(x,\alpha):x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
and
\pi
is the corresponding natural projection.

Generalising further, we define the

(r,s)
-tensor bundle on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the tuple
(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:=\{(x,v):x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,v\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{(r,s)}\}
and
\pi
is the corresponding natural projection. For every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have
\pi^{-1}(x)=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{(r,s)}
, which we call the fibre of the bundle at
x
and we will denote more conveniently by
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We will often refer to
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
itself as the tensor bundle, omitting the projection map. Note that setting
(r,s)=(1,0)
, we recover the tangent bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n},
and setting
(r,s)=(0,1),
we recover the cotangent bundle
\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A tensor field is defined as a “section” of the tensor bundle, a concept which we introduce in the next paragraph.

A smooth section
s
of the tensor bundle
(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\pi)
is a smooth map
s:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
such that
\pi\circ s:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the identity map.
We define a smooth tensor field to be a smooth section of the tensor bundle. The space of smooth tensor fields will be denoted by
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, or alternatively
\Gamma(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, as commonly done in the differential geometry literature. As special cases, a smooth section of the tangent bundle is called a smooth vector field and a smooth section of the cotangent bundle is called a smooth differential one-form. As standardly done in the differential geometry literature, we denote the set of all smooth vector fields by
\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the set of all smooth differential one-forms by
\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Remark 15
.

In the Euclidean case, given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be canonically identified with the tangent space at zero
\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
via the following construction. Define the translation operator
L_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
L_{x}x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-x,
for
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Given a basis
\{[c_{1}],\ldots,[c_{n}]\}
for
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can construct a basis for
\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\{[L_{x}c_{1}],\ldots,[L_{x}c_{n}]\}
. This defines an isomorphism
\mathcal{T}_{x}L_{x}:\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
via the relation
\mathcal{T}_{x}L_{x}(\alpha_{1}[c_{1}]+\cdots+\alpha_{n}[c_{n}]):=\alpha_{1}[L_{x}c_{1}]+\cdots+\alpha_{n}[L_{x}c_{n}]
, for any
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, we can identify the tangent bundle
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the product space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which can further be identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
upon fixing a basis for
\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Similarly, one can identify the cotangent bundle
\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
via the adjoint of
\mathcal{T}_{x}L_{x}
with respect to the natural pairing, namely,
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{x}L_{x}:\mathcal{T}_{0}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and by fixing a dual basis for
\mathcal{T}_{0}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Putting the previous facts together, we can identify the tensor bundle
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the direct product space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

Remark 16
.

There is a canonical basis for
\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
due to the presence of the standard coordinate basis
e_{1}=(1,0,0\ldots,0)
,
e_{2}=(0,1,0,\ldots,0),
etc, on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is given by
\{[c_{1}],\ldots,[c_{n}]\}
, where
c_{1}(t)=(t,0,0\ldots,0)
,
c_{2}(t)=(0,t,0\ldots,0)
, etc. By Remark
15
above, this also generates a canonical basis
\{[L_{-x}c_{1}],\ldots,[L_{-x}c_{n}]\}
for
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n},
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 17
.

Owing to Remark
15
above, a tensor field on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be interpreted as a tensor-valued map
\mathbf{T}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, i.e., a function that assigns a tensor to every point in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In the cases
(r,s)=(1,0)
and
(r,s)=(0,1)
, the tensor fields simply become vector-valued maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Given an
(r,s)
-tensor field
\mathbf{T}
and an
(s,r)
-tensor field
\mathbf{S}
, we can define a natural pairing at each point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted by angle brackets
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(s,r)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, by

\displaystyle\left<\mathbf{T}(x),\mathbf{S}(x)\right>:=T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)S^{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}(x),

where sum over repeated indices is assumed.

Throughout the rest of this section, we assume sections to always be smooth.

9.2
Raising/lowering indices and the bundle metric

Consider a symmetric, positive definite, nondegenerate bilinear form
\gamma:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which is a
(0,2)
-tensor whose components are given by
\gamma_{ij}=\gamma(e_{i},e_{j})
. Fixing a vector
v\in V
, one can construct an associated covector
v^{\flat}\in V^{*}
through the relation
v^{\flat}(w)=\gamma(v,w),
for any
w\in V
. Let
\gamma^{\flat}:V\rightarrow V^{*}
be the map
\gamma^{\flat}:v\rightarrow v^{\flat},
for
v\in V
. This procedure is called “lowering the index”, since the components of
v^{\flat}
now have lower indices (
(v^{\flat})_{i}=\gamma_{ij}v^{j}
). We also introduce the inverse operation
\gamma^{\sharp}:=(\gamma^{\flat})^{-1}:V^{*}\rightarrow V
, which maps a covector
\alpha\in V^{*}
to a vector
\alpha^{\sharp}\in V
by
\alpha^{\sharp}=\gamma^{\sharp}(\alpha)
. This is called “raising the index” for similar reasons. One can then define a
(2,0)
-tensor
\gamma^{-1}:V^{*}\times V^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
\gamma^{-1}(\alpha,\beta):=\gamma(\alpha^{\sharp},\beta^{\sharp}),
whose components we simply denote by
\gamma^{ij}=\gamma^{-1}(e^{i},e^{j})
.

If
\mathbf{g}
is a smooth
(0,2)
-tensor field such that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbf{g}(x)
defines a symmetric, positive definite, nondegenerate bilinear form on the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we say that
\mathbf{g}
is a Riemannian metric or a metric tensor on
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Applying the above procedure on each fibre, we can construct the cometric tensor
\mathbf{g}^{-1}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{T}^{(2,0)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which defines a symmetric, positive definite, nondegenerate bilinear form on the cotangent spaces
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Combining the metric and cometric tensors, we can construct a metric on general tensor fields, defined as follows (see for instance
[
6
]
).

Definition 9
.

Given a metric tensor
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a bundle metric
\bar{\mathbf{g}}
on
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by

\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{x}(\mathbf{T}_{x},\mathbf{S}_{x}):=g_{i_{1}j_{1}}(x)\cdots g_{i_{r}j_{r}}(x)g^{i_{r+1}j_{r+1}}(x)\cdots g^{i_{r+s}j_{r+s}}(x)(T_{x})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}(S_{x})^{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}}_{j_{r+1},\ldots,j_{r+s}},

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and any
\mathbf{T}_{x},\mathbf{S}_{x}\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
g_{ij}
are the components of the metric tensor
\mathbf{g}
and
g^{ij}
are the components of the cometric tensor
\mathbf{g}^{-1}
. For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, this also equips each fibre
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a norm
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{x}:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, defined by

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{T}_{x}\rvert_{x}:=\sqrt{\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{x}(\mathbf{T}_{x},\mathbf{T}_{x})}.

The Euclidean space is canonically equipped with the Euclidean metric tensor
g_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij},
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta
13
13

13

This is operationally identical to the standard dot product employed in Euclidean geometry.
. In this case, the bundle metric simply becomes

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{x}(\mathbf{T}_{x},\mathbf{S}_{x})=(T_{x})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(S_{x})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}},

where sum over repeated indices is assumed regardless of whether the indices are up or down. For simplicity, when the base metric is just the Euclidean metric, we denote the corresponding bundle metric by round brackets
(\cdot,\cdot)_{x}:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that throughout the paper, this is the only metric tensor that we consider.

9.3
Multi-vector fields and differential
k
-forms

In Section
9.1
, we introduced the notion of smooth section of the tensor bundle and, in particular, defined (smooth) vector fields as the special case corresponding to
(r,s)=(1,0),
and (smooth) differential one-forms as the special case corresponding to
(r,s)=(0,1)
. We now introduce an important generalisation of these two concepts, namely, (smooth) multi-vector fields and (smooth) differential
k
-forms.

We first introduce the exterior product
\wedge
, which is an operation
\wedge:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{V}\times\mathcal{T}^{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}_{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{(r+r^{\prime},s+s^{\prime})}_{V}
defined by

\mathbf{F}\wedge\mathbf{G}:=\mathbf{F}\otimes\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{G}\otimes\mathbf{F}
, where the tensor product
\otimes
was defined in (
131
). Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we define
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the space consisting of tensors of the form
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}
, where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Likewise, we define
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the space consisting of tensors of the form
\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\alpha_{k}
, where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define the alternating bundles
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the tensor bundles whose fibres at point
x
correspond to
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively.

Multi-vector fields.

A smooth multi-vector field of degree
k
(or simply, a
k
-vector field)
\mathbf{U}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth
(k,0)
-tensor field such that for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{k}
(here,
S_{k}
denotes the permutation group) and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}(x)(\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,\alpha_{\sigma(k)})=\text{sgn}(\sigma)\mathbf{U}(x)(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}),\text{ for all }\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
\text{sgn}(\sigma)
is the signature of the permutation
\sigma\in S_{k}
. One can check that smooth multi-vector fields are simply sections of
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We denote the space of smooth
k
-vector fields by
\mathfrak{X}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Differential
k
-forms.

A smooth differential
k
-form (or simply, a
k
-form)
\mathbf{K}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
(0,k)
-tensor field such that for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{k}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(x)(v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(k)})=\text{sgn}(\sigma)\mathbf{K}(x)(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}),\text{ for all }v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Again, smooth differential
k
-forms are simply sections of
\bigwedge^{k}\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We denote the space of smooth
k
-forms by
\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

As a standard convention in differential geometry, the canonical basis of
\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}
(i.e., the basis obtained by standard coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, see Remark
16
) is denoted

\{\partial/\partial{x_{1}}(x),\ldots,\partial/\partial{x_{n}}(x)\}
and its corresponding dual basis is denoted by
\{\mathrm{d}x_{1}(x),\ldots,\mathrm{d}x_{n}(x)\}
. Note that these are related by
\left<\partial/\partial{x_{i}}(x),\mathrm{d}x_{j}(x)\right>=\delta_{ij}
. Thus, in standard coordinates, a vector field
X
and a one-form
\alpha
can be expressed explicitly as

\displaystyle X(x)=X^{i}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}(x),\qquad\alpha(x)=\alpha_{i}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{i}(x),

respectively. Likewise, a
k
-vector field
\mathbf{U}\in\mathfrak{X}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a
k
-form
\mathbf{K}\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}^{n}U^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{k}}}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}^{n}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}}(x),

respectively. Differential
k
-forms are especially important in differential geometry and physics since they allow to extend the notion of integration to general oriented manifolds. For a
k
-form
\mathbf{K}\in\Omega^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we define its integral over a
k
-dimensional submanifold
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\int_{U}\mathbf{K}:=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}^{n}\int_{U}K_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}},

where
\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{i_{k}}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This helps formalise the notions of circulation, flux, and density that arise in physics.

9.4
Tensor calculus

Here, we review some elementary differential operations defined on smooth tensor fields.

Pull-back and push-forward.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth map. The push-forward
\varphi_{*}X
of a vector field
X=X^{i}(x)\partial/\partial x_{i}(x)
with respect to
\varphi
is defined in coordinates as

\displaystyle(\varphi_{*}X)(\varphi(x)):=X^{i}(x)\frac{\partial\varphi^{j}}{\partial x_{i}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}(\varphi(x)).

The pull-back
\varphi^{*}\alpha
of a one-form
\alpha=\alpha_{i}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{i}(x)
with respect to
\varphi
is defined as

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\alpha)(x):=\alpha_{i}(\varphi(x))\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial x_{j}}(x)\mathrm{d}x_{j}(x).

Furthermore, when
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism, one can also define the pull-back of a vector field as
\varphi^{*}X:=(\varphi^{-1})_{*}X,
and the push-forward of a one-form as
\varphi_{*}\alpha:=(\varphi^{-1})^{*}\alpha
.

In fact, this notion can be readily extended to arbitrary
(r,s)
-tensor fields. Indeed, for a smooth tensor field
\mathbf{T},
we define its pull-back
\varphi^{*}\mathbf{T}
with respect to a diffeomorphism
\varphi
by

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\mathbf{T})(x)(\alpha^{1}(x),\ldots,\alpha^{r}(x),v_{1}(x),\ldots,v_{s}(x))

\displaystyle\quad:=\mathbf{T}(\varphi(x))(\varphi_{*}\alpha^{1}(\varphi(x)),\ldots,\varphi_{*}\alpha^{r}(\varphi(x)),\varphi_{*}v_{1}(\varphi(x)),\ldots,\varphi_{*}v_{s}(\varphi(x))),

for any
\alpha^{1},\ldots,\alpha^{r}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
This has the coordinate expression

\displaystyle(\varphi^{*}\mathbf{T})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)

\displaystyle=T^{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}}_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{s}}(\varphi(x))\frac{\partial\psi^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{p_{1}}}(\varphi(x))\cdots\frac{\partial\psi^{i_{r}}}{\partial x_{p_{r}}}(\varphi(x))\frac{\partial\varphi^{q_{1}}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)\cdots\frac{\partial\varphi^{q_{s}}}{\partial x_{j_{s}}}(x),

where
\psi:=\varphi^{-1}
. Similarly, we can define the push-forward of an
(r,s)
-tensor field as
\varphi_{*}\mathbf{T}:=(\varphi^{-1})^{*}\mathbf{T}
.

Lie derivative.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an arbitrary smooth
(r,s)
-tensor field and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
a smooth vector field. Let
\varphi_{t},

t\in[0,\infty)
be the local flow of
X
, whose existence is guaranteed by the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem. The Lie derivative of
\mathbf{T}
with respect to
X
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\mathbf{T}:=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}\left(\varphi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{T}-\mathbf{T}\right).

The explicit formula (in coordinates) for the Lie derivative of a smooth tensor field
\mathbf{T}
with respect to a smooth vector field
b\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be computed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathcal{L}_{b}\mathbf{T})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)=b^{l}(x)\frac{\partial T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)-T^{l,i_{2}\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{i_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\\
&\hskip 60.0pt-\cdots-T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1},l}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{i_{r}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\\
&\hskip 60.0pt+T^{i_{1}\ldots,i_{r}}_{l,j_{2}\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial x_{j_{1}}}(x)+\cdots+T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s-1},l}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial x_{j_{s}}}(x),\end{split}

(132)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and its corresponding
L^{2}
-adjoint operator applied to a smooth tensor
\mathbf{\Theta}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{b}\mathbf{\Theta})^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)=-\frac{\partial(b^{l}\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}})}{\partial x_{l}}(x)-\Theta^{l,i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\\
&\hskip 60.0pt-\cdots-\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1},l}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{l}}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}(x)\\
&\hskip 60.0pt+\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{l,j_{2},\ldots,j_{s}}(x)\frac{\partial b^{j_{1}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)+\cdots+\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{s-1},l}(x)\frac{\partial b^{j_{s}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x),\end{split}

(133)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

10
Banach sections of tensor bundles

Here, we introduce some Banach sections of the
(r,s)
-tensor bundle
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
that are used in this paper.
We only consider tensor fields on the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the standard Euclidean metric for simplicity; however, these notions can be extended to general Riemannian manifolds (see for instance
[
36
,
12
,
6
]
). In Section
9
, we introduced the space of smooth sections of the tensor bundle
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted
\Gamma(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. However, since in this paper we will work with sections of tensor bundles that are of lower regularity, we will employ the notation
\Gamma_{\text{alg}}(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
to denote sections in the algebraic sense (i.e., maps
s:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
that satisfy
\pi\circ s(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
without the smoothness requirement), and
\Gamma_{\text{meas}}(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
to denote algebraic sections that are measurable with respect to the Borel sigma-algebra.

L^{p}
-tensor fields.
For
1\leq p<\infty
, we wish to define the
L^{p}
-norm of a measurable tensor field
\mathbf{T}\in\Gamma_{\text{meas}}(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}).
For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one can induce a bundle metric on
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by making use of the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(i.e. the Euclidean metric), which we denote by rounded brackets
(\cdot,\cdot)_{x}:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(see Appendix
9
for more details about the construction of a bundle metric). The corresponding associated norm is denoted by
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{x}:\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}_{x}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
. For
1\leq p<\infty
, we define the
L^{p}
-norm of
\mathbf{T}\in\Gamma_{\text{meas}}(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
by

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{T}(x)\rvert_{x}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

and for
p=\infty
, we define the supremum norm

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{\infty}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{T}(x)\rvert_{x}.

For
p\in[1,\infty]
, the
L^{p}
-section of
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted by
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
is an equivalence class of tensor fields
\mathbf{T}
with
\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}}<\infty,
where two tensor fields
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}
are identified if and only if
\|\mathbf{T}_{1}-\mathbf{T}_{2}\|_{L^{p}}=0
.

Let
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the Borel algebra. Given
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact closure, we define the
L^{p}
-norm of
\mathbf{T}
on
U
by

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}(U,\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})}:=\left(\int_{U}\lvert\mathbf{T}(x)\rvert_{x}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{X}_{U}\lvert\mathbf{T}(x)\rvert_{x}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

for
1\leq p<\infty,
and

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{\infty}(U,\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})}:=\sup_{x\in U}\lvert\mathbf{T}(x)\rvert_{x},

(134)

for
p=\infty
.
If
\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}(U,\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
for any
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact closure, then we say that
\mathbf{T}
is of class
L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For convenience, we will denote by
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{R}}
the local
L^{p}
-norm on the open ball
B(0,R)
.

In the special case
p=2
, the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
is furthermore equipped with an inner product
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, defined by

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{G}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathbf{F}(x),\mathbf{G}(x))_{x}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x,

for any
\mathbf{F},\mathbf{G}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, making it a Hilbert space. More generally, for
\mathbf{F}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbf{G}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
q,p\in[1,\infty]
such that
1/q+1/p=1
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{F},\mathbf{G}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathbf{F}(x),\mathbf{G}(x))_{x}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lvert\mathbf{F}(x)\rvert_{x}\lvert\mathbf{G}(x)\rvert_{x}\,\mathrm{d}^{n}x

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{F}\|_{L^{q}}\|\mathbf{G}\|_{L^{p}}<\infty,

where we have made use of the classical Cauchy-Schwartz and Hölder’s inequalities.

C^{k}
-tensor fields.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}.
If every component
T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}
of an
(r,s)
-tensor field
\mathbf{T}
is
k
-times differentiable, then we say that
\mathbf{T}
is a
C^{k}
-section of the tensor bundle, denoted by
C^{k}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. We note that this
is not a Banach space
, but a Fréchet space. Given an
(r,s)
-tensor field
\mathbf{T}
of class
C^{1}
, we define its derivative to be the
(r,s+1)
-tensor field

\nabla\mathbf{T}(x):=\frac{\partial T^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\otimes\cdots\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}(x)\otimes\mathrm{d}x_{l}\otimes\mathrm{d}x_{i_{r+1}}(x)\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{d}x_{i_{r+s}}(x).

Analogously, we can define the
k
-th derivative
\nabla^{k}\mathbf{T}
for
C^{k}
-tensor fields
\mathbf{T}
, which is an
(r,s+k)
-tensor. Following standard conventions, we also use the notation
D
for the derivative
\nabla
(e.g.
D\mathbf{T}
,
D^{2}\mathbf{T}
). We denote by
C^{k}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
the class of
C^{k}
tensor fields with compact support, which is a Banach space, equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{C_{0}^{k}}:=\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\|\nabla^{m}\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{\infty}}

We note that all the operators we defined in Section
9.4
can be defined for tensors of class
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}).
In particular, the coordinate expressions for the Lie derivative and its
L^{2}
-adjoint in the smooth case (
132
), (
133
) remain the same.

W^{k,p}
-tensor fields.

One can also define Sobolev sections of tensor fields in the same way one defines the usual Sobolev spaces. First we provide the notion of weak derivatives of tensor fields.

Definition 10
.

Given
\mathbf{T}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we say that
\mathbf{S}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s+1)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a weak derivative of
\mathbf{T}
if it satisfies

\langle\langle\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\langle\mathbf{T},\nabla^{T}\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}
for any test tensor field
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s+1)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
\nabla^{T}
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint operator of
\nabla:C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s+1)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which in coordinates reads

\nabla^{T}\mathbf{\Theta}(x):=-\frac{\partial\Theta^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}_{l,i_{r+1},\ldots,i_{r+s}}}{\partial x_{l}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}(x)\otimes\cdots\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}(x)\otimes\mathrm{d}x_{i_{r+1}}(x)\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{d}x_{i_{r+s}}(x),

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We denote the weak derivative
\mathbf{S}
of a tensor field by
\nabla_{w}\mathbf{T}
, where “
w
” is usually omitted if it is understood from the context.

Weak notions of
k
-th derivatives are defined in a similar manner and we define the Sobolev norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{k,p}}
of a tensor field, for
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
p\in[1,\infty],
by

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{T}\|_{W^{k,p}}:=\|\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}}+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\|\nabla_{w}^{m}\mathbf{T}\|_{L^{p}}.

We say that
\mathbf{T}
is in the Sobolev section
W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
if all of the weak derivatives
\nabla_{w}\mathbf{T},\ldots,\nabla_{w}^{k}\mathbf{T}
exist and
\|\mathbf{T}\|_{W^{k,p}}<\infty
. As usually, we identify two elements
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}
if and only if
\|\mathbf{T}_{1}-\mathbf{T}_{2}\|_{W^{k,p}}=0
. Furthermore, if the section
\mathbf{T}_{U}:=\mathcal{X}_{U}\mathbf{T}\in\Gamma_{\text{meas}}(\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}))
is of class
W^{k,p}
for every
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact closure, then we say that
\mathbf{T}
is of class
W^{k,p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The vector field case.

In the case
(r,s)=(1,0)
a tensor field
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a vector field and hence can be interpreted as a function
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Remark
17
). In this case, for the Banach classes of vector fields we will employ the notations
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),

W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),

C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n}),
etc, instead of the geometric notations
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}),

W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}),

C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}),
etc. The reason behind this choice is making this article more accessible to readers who are less familiar with differential geometry.

Distribution-valued tensor fields.

Given the space
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
of smooth
(r,s)
-tensor fields with compact support, a distribution-valued tensor field
\mathbf{T}
is a continuous (with respect to the LF topology) linear functional
\mathbf{T}:C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We denote the space of all distribution-valued tensor fields by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is equipped with the weak-
*
topology.

Definition 11

(Mollification of tensor fields)
.

Consider a tensor field
\mathbf{T}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a mollifier
\rho^{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
We define the mollified tensor field
\mathbf{T}^{\epsilon}
as the smooth
(r,s)
-tensor field satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{T}^{\epsilon}(x)(\alpha^{1}(x),\ldots,\alpha^{r}(x),v_{1}(x),\ldots,v_{s}(x))\\
&\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho^{\epsilon}(x-y)\mathbf{T}(y)(\tau_{x}^{y}\alpha^{1}(x),\ldots,\tau_{x}^{y}\alpha^{r}(x),\tau_{x}^{y}v_{1}(x),\ldots,\tau_{x}^{y}v_{s}(x))\,\mathrm{d}^{n}y,\end{split}

(135)

for any
\alpha^{i}\in\Gamma_{\text{alg}}(\mathcal{T}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
i=1,\ldots,r,

v_{j}\in\Gamma_{\text{alg}}(\mathcal{T}\mathbb{R}^{n}),

j=1,\ldots,s,
where
\tau_{x}^{y}
denotes the parallel transport from
x
to
y
along the geodesic with respect to the metric.

When the metric is the Euclidean one, identifying vector fields and one-forms simply as maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Remark
17
), we have
\tau_{x}^{y}v(x)=v(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\tau_{x}^{y}\alpha(x)=\alpha(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(in this case, the right hand side of (
135
) makes sense by interpreting
\mathbf{T}
as a map
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{(r,s)}
). We employ the standard convolution notation
\mathbf{T}^{\epsilon}:=\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathbf{T}
to denote the mollification of a tensor.

Definition 12

(Mollification of distribution-valued tensor fields)
.

Given a distribution-valued tensor field
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a mollifier
\rho^{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we define the mollified tensor field
\mathbf{T}^{\epsilon}
as the smooth
(r,s)
-tensor field satisfying

\displaystyle\langle\langle\mathbf{T}^{\epsilon},\mathbf{\Theta}\rangle\rangle_{L^{2}}:=\mathbf{T}(\rho^{\epsilon}*\mathbf{\Theta}),

for any test tensor field
\mathbf{\Theta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

label
